Moreover, if we assume that there exists
1\leq i\leq r
such that
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\phi(x_{i}))>i+1
then, by Axiom
iii)
for
K,
there exists
y\in K
such that
\phi(x_{i-1})\subsetneq y\subsetneq\phi(x_{i}).
Since
\phi^{-1}
is a bijective poset homomorphism, this implies
x_{i}\subsetneq\phi^{-1}(y)\subsetneq x_{i+1},
a contradiction with the Axiom
i)
of
J.

Therefore
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\phi(x_{i}))+1=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\phi(x_{i+1}))
for all
1\leq i\leq r
and
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\phi(x_{r}))=r
and this proves the point
2)
as we assumed that
x_{r}\in J^{[r]}.

1)
clearly implies
3)
and this concludes the proof.
∎

The following remark is an observation from
[
20
]
which we will refer to later in Chapter
3
, where we will in effect find a way around this fact.

Remark 1.3

(Remark 3.1 in
[
20
]
)
.

An edge of a cc
K
has more than one vertex as the definition of cc asserts that all singletons are vertices (i.e. have rank
0
), so the
co-boundary
of
K,
defined as the set of cells containing a 1-cell made of only one vertex, is empty.

The next remark is an observation that allows to better compare our definition of cc with the definition in
[
20
]
and which will be used later in Chapter
4
when discussing the notion of collapse.

Remark 1.4
.

The definition of cc in
[
20
]
demands the additional condition that if
\operatorname{rk}(x)\geq 1
then
x=\bigvee\triangle\left(x\right),
where the least upper bound
\bigvee
is taken in the poset
(K,\subset).
This condition is always satisfied in our context since cells are defined as subsets of a vertex set. Indeed if
y
is an upper bound of
\triangle\left(x\right)
then in particular for every vertex
v\in x
there is a face
x^{\prime}\in\triangle\left(x\right)
such that
v\in x^{\prime}\subset y
and therefore
x\subset y.
So
x
always fulfils the criteria to be the least upper bound of
\triangle\left(x\right).
As a consequence, a cell of non-zero rank is always equal to the union of its faces. In summary, if
\operatorname{rk}(x)\geq 1
then

x=\bigvee\triangle\left(x\right)=\bigcup_{y\in\triangle\left(x\right)}y.

(1)

The definition of cc allows edges to have more than two vertices. Adding the requirement that edges have exactly two vertices constitutes the notions of graph-based cc. Adding what will later be understood as the dual of the latter requirement gives the notion of non-singular cc introduced next.

Definition 1.5

(Graph-based, non-singular and closed cc)
.

A cc
K
of rank
R
is said to be
graph-based
if every edge of
K
has (no more than) two vertices.
K
is
non-singular
if it is graph-based, pure and
non-branching,
i.e. every
(R-1)
-cell is a face of at-most two
R
-cells.

K
is said to be
closed
if it is non-singular and has empty boundary, i.e. every
(R-1)
-cell is contained in exactly two
R
-cells.

For example, as a consequence of the diamond property
iv)
, if
K
is a non-singular cc then for all
x\in K
the cc
\partial(K\cap x)
is closed. In
[
20
]
, what we call a closed cc is called a
manifold-like
cc, but we prefer the former term as it better encapsulates the idea of having an empty boundary and is not related to the notion of manifold. If
K
contains a sub-maximal cell
y
such that
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(y\right)\right|>2
then we say that
K
is
branching
and that
y
is a
(
K
-)branching
.

The following technical lemma is used repeatedly in this work and, in a sense that will become clear in the next section, shows when the property dual to (
1
) is verified.

Lemma 1.6
.

Let
x
be a cell of a cc
K
such that
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2
then

x=\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)=\bigcap_{y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)}y.

Proof.

Since
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2
Axiom
ii)
implies
x^{\prime}:=\bigcap_{y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)}y\in K.
If
y^{\prime}
is a lower bound of
\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
then
y^{\prime}\subset y,\penalty\ \forall y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
, hence
y^{\prime}\subset x^{\prime}.
The latter implies

x^{\prime}=\bigcap_{y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)}y=\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right).

We also have that
x\subset x^{\prime}
since
x
is a lower bound of
\bigtriangledown\left(x\right).
If by contradiction
x^{\prime}\neq x
then by
i)

\operatorname{rk}(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))>\operatorname{rk}(x),
hence
\operatorname{rk}(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))=\operatorname{rk}(x)+1.
But if
y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
then
\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\subset y
and
\operatorname{rk}(y)=\operatorname{rk}(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))
so that
y=\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
by
i)
, which implies
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|=1
and contradicts
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2.

∎

Introducing a greatest lower bound
\bigwedge
raises the question of whether it exists at all. This next remark therefore helps us clarify this point in Chapter
4
, when discussing the notions of reduction and collapse.

Remark 1.7
.

A consequence of the proof of Lemma
1.6
is that
\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
exists for all cell
x
in a cc
K
and

\displaystyle\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)=\begin{cases}x,&\text{if }\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2,\\
y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right),&\text{if }\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)=\{y\},\\
\emptyset&\text{if }x\text{ is maximal.}\end{cases}

1.3
Simplicial complexes, graphs, paths and local cc

In this section, we first introduce simplicial complexes and graphs. We then introduce a number of definitions and some terminology related to paths that in particular allows us to define the notion of connected, cell-connected and local cc. We end by a lemma we use to introduce the notion of representation of a cell. These definitions are used especially in the next Chapter
2
, whose main goal is to prove our results on reconstruction.

The definition of simplicial complex presented now, sometimes called ”abstract simplicial complex” in other contexts, constitutes a particular case of cc and encompasses the notion of graph introduced afterwards.

Definition 1.8

(Simplicial complex)
.

Let
S
be a finite set. A collection
K\subset\mathcal{P}\left(S\right)
is called a
simplicial complex
if it satisfies the two following properties:

a
)

If
x\subset y\in K
then
x\in K,

b
)

every one element subset (vertex) of
S
is in
K.

An element
x\in K
is called a
simplex
and we define the rank function of
K
by

\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)=\left|x\right|-1.

Conditions
a)
and
b)
characterizing simpicial complexes clearly imply the four axioms of cell complexes, hence our choice of definition: it is sufficient to check these two conditions to establish whether a given collection of subsets of a set is a simplicial complex. We could have also defined a simplicial complex to be a cc
K
with rank function
\operatorname{rk}_{K}
satisfying condition
a)
. In this case, as a consequence of Axiom
iii)
, the rank function satisfies
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)=\left|x\right|-1.

The notion of graph used in our context can naturally be defined as follows.

Definition 1.9

(Graph)
.

A graph
\mathcal{G}
is a simplicial complex of rank 1. We denote by
\mathcal{V}
the set of vertices of a graph and
\mathcal{E}
its set of edges.

In particular a graph
\mathcal{G}
is a 1-cc where we use the notation
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{G}^{[0]}
and
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{G}^{[1]}
and there cannot be multiple edges between two vertices nor self-edge (linking a vertex with itself) in a graph. As expected, if a cc
K
is graph-based then
K^{(1)}
is a graph and we will sometimes use the notation
\mathcal{E}
for
K^{[1]},
the set of edges of
K,
when there is no ambiguity on the cc
K.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph. For
v\in\mathcal{V},
we define

\mathcal{N}_{v}:=\{w\in\mathcal{V}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \{v,w\}\in\mathcal{E}\}

the set of
neighbours
of
v
in
\mathcal{G}
and

\mathcal{E}_{v}:=\{e\in\mathcal{E}\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\in e\}

the set of
edges of
\mathcal{G}
incident to
v.
We define the
degree
of a vertex
v
to be

\deg(v):=\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}\right|=\left|\mathcal{N}_{v}\right|.

More generally, if
A\subset\mathcal{V},
we will sometimes use the notation

\mathcal{E}_{A}:=\{e\in\mathcal{E}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \left|A\cap e\right|=1\}.

We will often use paths in Chapter
2
, where for example in Section
2.4
we define a notion of homotopy for paths in cell complexes of rank higher or equal to 2. For this, we chose to use the following definitions related to paths in a graph
\mathcal{G}
and, by extension, in any graph-based cc.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
v_{0},v_{k}\in\mathcal{V}.
We say that
p\in(\mathcal{E})^{k}
is a
path in
\mathcal{G}
from
v_{0}
to
v_{k}
of
length

\left|p\right|:=k
if
p=(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})
and there exists
v_{1},\dots v_{k-1}
such that
e_{i}=\{v_{i-1},v_{i}\}
for all
1\leq i\leq k.
In this case we sometimes write
p:v_{0}\rightsquigarrow v_{k}.
We conventionally denote by
\emptyset_{v}
the
empty path
of length 0 from
v
to
v.
We say that an edge
e
is contained in
p
and write
e\in p
if
e=e_{i}
for possibly multiple
1\leq i\leq k.
We define the vertices of the path
p
to be
p^{[0]}=\{v\in\mathcal{V}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \exists e\in p,\penalty\ v\in e\}
and its edge to be
p^{[1]}=\{e\in\mathcal{E}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\in p\}.
The
inverse
of
p
is defined by
p^{-1}=(e_{k},e_{k-1},\dots,e_{1}),
which is a path from
v_{k}
to
v_{0}.
A path
p^{\prime}
is called a
sub-path
of
p,
noted
p^{\prime}\leq p,
if it is a path such that
(p^{\prime})^{[1]}\subset p^{[1]}.
A path from
v
to
v
is called a
cycle based at
v
. We say that a path of length
k
is
simple
if
\left|p^{[0]}\right|=\left|p\right|+1,
which implies that the path
p=(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})
”does not pass through each
v\in p^{[0]}
more than once”:

\left|\{1\leq i\leq k\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\in e_{i}\}\right|\leq 2\quad\forall v\in p^{[0]},

and that
p
”never uses an edge more than once”:

\left|\{1\leq i\leq k\penalty\ |\penalty\ e=e_{i}\}\right|\leq 1\quad\forall e\in p^{[1]}.

A simple cycle is called a
loop.

If
p_{1}
is a path from
v_{0}
to
v_{1}
and
p_{2}
is a path from
v_{1}
to
v_{2}
then we denote by
p_{1}\ast p_{2}
the
concatenation
of the paths
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, which is the path from
v_{0}
to
v_{2}
composed of the edges of
p_{1}
followed by the edges of
p_{2}.
The concatenation of paths is thereby associative and has
the empty paths
\emptyset_{v}
based at
v\in\mathcal{V}
as neutral elements, i.e. if
p
is a path from
v
to
v^{\prime}
then
p\ast\emptyset_{v^{\prime}}=\emptyset_{v}\ast p=p.
We set
\emptyset_{v}^{-1}=\emptyset_{v}
by convention.

A graph is
connected
if any two of its vertices can be linked by a path. For our purposes, we choose to define connectedness for cc in the following way, implying in particular that a connected cc is graph-based.

Definition 1.10

(Connected and cell-connected cc)
.

A cc
K
is
connected
if it is graph-based and
K^{(1)}
is connected.
K
is said to be
cell-connected
if it is graph-based and
K\cap x
is connected for all
x\in K.

The following notion of locality for cc is often a case of focus in this work, hence we also chose an abbreviation for local cc.

Definition 1.11

(Local cell complex (lcc))
.

A
local cell complex,
abbreviated lcc, of dimension
R
or
R
-lcc
is a connected cell-connected
R
-cc

It will be convenient to have the following notion of representation of a connected component of a 2-cell in a graph-based cc, in particular when using what we will call the loop associated to a connected component of a 2-cell defined afterwards. Not assuming locality in this lemma allows us to formulate some results of Chapter
2
in more generality, however thinking of
C
in the next lemma simply as a
2
-cell will be sufficient for most of this work.

Lemma 1.12

(Representation of a 2-cell)
.

Let
K
be a graph-based
R
-cc with
R\geq 2.
If
C
is a connected component of 2-cell of
K
then there exits
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in K^{[0]}
such that
C=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
and such that
\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\},

1\leq i\leq k-1
and
\{v_{k},v_{1}\}
are the only edges of
K^{(1)}\cap C.
In this case we write
C=[v_{1}\dots v_{k}]
and call
[v_{1}\dots v_{k}]
a representation of
C.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the claim when
C\in K^{[2]}
is connected. The diamond property
iv)
implies that if
C\in K^{[2]}
and
v\in C
then

\left|\mathcal{N}_{v}\cap C\right|=2.

(2)

Let
v_{2}\in C
and let
v_{1},v_{3}\in\mathcal{N}_{v_{2}}\cap C.
If
v_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{v_{3}}\cap C
then
C=\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
and

\{\{v_{1},v_{2}\},\{v_{2},v_{3}\},\{v_{1},v_{3}\}\}\subset K^{[1]},

is the set of all the edges in
K^{(1)}\cap C.
If
v_{1}\notin\mathcal{N}_{v_{3}}\cap C
then, since
\left|\mathcal{N}_{v_{3}}\cap C\right|=2,
there exists
v_{4}\in K^{[0]}\setminus\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
such that
\mathcal{N}_{v_{3}}\cap C=\{v_{2},v_{4}\}.
By repeating this argument, if at step
j
we have
v_{i}\notin\mathcal{N}_{v_{j-1}}\cap C
for all
1\leq i\leq j-3,
we can add
v_{j}\in K^{[0]}\setminus\{v_{1},\dots,v_{j-1}\}
to the set
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{j-1}\}\subset C
such that
\mathcal{N}_{v_{j-1}}\cap C=\{v_{j-2},v_{j}\}.
Since
K^{[0]}
is finite, this process must terminate after a finite number
k
of steps such that for all
4\leq j\leq k
we have
\{v_{j-1},v_{j}\}\in K^{[1]}
by step
j
and
\{v_{k},v_{l}\}\in K^{[1]}
by the step
k,
for some
1\leq l\leq k-2.
If
1<l\leq k-2
then
\{v_{l-1},v_{l+1},v_{k}\}\subset\mathcal{N}_{v_{l}}\cap C,
which is a contradiction with (
2
), therefore
l=1.
The same contradiction occurs if there exists an edge in
K^{(1)}\cap\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
different than
\{v_{j},v_{j+1}\}
for
1\leq j\leq k-1
or
\{v_{k},v_{1}\}.
Moreover, we have
C=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
since if
v\in C\setminus\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
then by the same argument
\{v,v_{j}\}\notin K^{[1]}
for all
1\leq j\leq k
which is in contradiction with the fact that
K^{(1)}\cap C
connected.
∎

Definition 1.13

(Loops associated with a connected component of a 2-cell)
.

Let
K
be a graph-based
R
-cell complex with
R\geq 2.
A representation
[v_{1}\dots v_{k}]
of a connected component
C
of a 2-cell induces the definition of
2k
loops
l_{C}(v_{j}),l_{C}^{-1}(v_{j})

1\leq j\leq k
of length
\left|C\right|
based at
v_{j}
and defined by

l_{C}(v_{j}):=(\{v_{j},v_{j+1}\},\dots,\{v_{j-1},v_{j}\}),\penalty\ \penalty\ l_{C}^{-1}(v_{j}):=(\{v_{j},v_{j-1}\},\dots,\{v_{j+1},v_{j}\}).

1.4
Duality map for closed cc

In this section we first introduce the notion of duality map at the center of the focus in this work. We first define it on closed cc and then generalize on what can be considered as more general cc, called cobordisms, in Chapter
3
.
We start by defining the general notion of dual set defined for pure cc.

Definition 1.14

(Dual set)
.

Let
K
be a pure
R
-cc and let
A\subset K^{[0]}
be non-empty. We define the
dual set
of
A
to be

\overline{A}:=\{z\in K^{[R]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ A\subset z\}.

If we need to specify the cc relative to which one is taking the dual, we use the notation
\overline{A}^{K}=\overline{A}.

Using the notion of dual set, we can then simply associate a poset to any pure cc in the following way.

Definition 1.15

(Dual of a cc)
.

Let
K
be a pure
R
-cc We define the poset
\left(\overline{K},\subset\right)
by

\overline{K}:=\{\overline{x}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in K\}.

We illustrate examples of dual cells in cc of rank 3 in Figure
5
.
Before proving that
\overline{K}
is a cell complex for
K
closed, we start by proving the following lemma introducing the duality map. Similar lemmas showing an equivalence between inclusion relation of certain cells will repeatedly be a first step when proving that a newly introduced poset satisfies the axioms of a cell complex.

Figure 5
:
This figure illustrates a portion of a closed 3-cc
K
made of two 3-cells. One of these 3-cell is a tetrahedra
z_{1}
and the second is a 3-cell
z_{2}
on 6 vertices sharing a triangle with the tetrahedra. The dual of the 3-cells in
K
are represented using star-shaped vertices. We indicated one of these vertices
\overline{z},
representing one of the 3-cells around
z_{1}
and
z_{2}.
Two of these vertices are linked by an edge whenever the corresponding 3-cells share a face. The picture includes the dual of the edge
e
(thicker line) forming a 2-cell on five vertices represented with a grey shade. We also use circles to indicate the vertices included in
\overline{v},
the 3-cell dual to the vertex
v\in K.

Lemma 1.16
.

Let
K
be a closed cc and
x,y\in K.
Then we have the following equivalence

x\subsetneq y\quad\text{ if and only if }\quad\overline{y}\subsetneq\overline{x},

and the duality map
x\mapsto\overline{x}
is a bijection from
K
to
\overline{K}.

Proof.

We first prove the following statement: if
x,y\in K
are such that there exists
v\in y\setminus x
then
\overline{x}
contains a maximal cell which does not contain
v,
and thus
\overline{x}\not\subset\overline{y}.

This can be shown by induction on
n:=R-\operatorname{rk}(x).
For
n=0,

x
has rank
R
and is then a maximal cell in
\overline{x}
which does not contain
v
by assumption. Assume
n\geq 1,
therefore
\operatorname{rk}(x)<R
and by Lemma
1.6
, we have
x=\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right).
This implies that there exists
x^{\prime}\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
such that
v\notin x^{\prime},
since otherwise
v\in x^{\prime}
for all
x^{\prime}\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
and in particular
v\in\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)=x,
which is a contradiction. By induction
x^{\prime}
is contained in a
R
-cell which does not contain
v,
and so does
x,
and this shows the statement.

As a consequence, if there exists
v\in y\setminus x
then
\overline{x}
contains a maximal cell
z
such that
v\notin z.
Together with the obvious implication
x\subset y\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \overline{y}\subset\overline{x}
this shows that
x\subsetneq y\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ \overline{y}\subsetneq\overline{x}.
Conversely if
\overline{x},\overline{y}\in\overline{K}
are such that
\overline{y}\subsetneq\overline{x}
then
x\setminus y=\emptyset
by the first statement, and since
x\neq y,
this implies
x\subsetneq y.

Similarly if
\overline{x}=\overline{y}
then
x\setminus y=y\setminus x=\emptyset,
so that
x=y.
The duality map
x\mapsto\overline{x}
is then injective, hence bijective.
∎

We could rephrase the latter lemma by saying that the duality map is an anti-isomorphism of posets.
The following proposition shows that the above definitions define a duality map on closed cell complexes. The absence of boundary in closed cc allows us to prove this result in an otherwise rather broad generality, as for example no assumption related to the notion of locality has to be included. This allows us to introduce several notions of Chapter
2
also without assuming locality and also to prove certain results for example only using the assumption that the cc is graph-based. Nevertheless, the main results Chapter
2
related to reconstruction of cc are proven under the assumption of locality.
Focusing on the local case also prevents to run into too many complications in Chapters
3
and
4
even though some results from this chapters could probably be formulated in more general terms.

Proposition 1.17
.

If
K
is a closed
R
-cc then so is
\overline{K}
with rank function

\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{K}}(\overline{x})=\overline{\operatorname{rk}}(\overline{x}):=R-\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)

and we have

\overline{\overline{K}}\cong K.

Proof.

The rank function
\overline{\operatorname{rk}}
is well defined as a consequence of the bijectivity of the duality map showed in Lemma
1.16
. The latter lemma together with condition
i)
for
K
also imply that for
x,y\in K,
if
\overline{y}\subsetneq\overline{x}
then
x\subsetneq y,
hence
\operatorname{rk}(x)<\operatorname{rk}(y)
and therefore
\overline{\operatorname{rk}}(\overline{y})<\overline{\operatorname{rk}}(\overline{x}).
This shows that
\overline{\operatorname{rk}}
is strictly compatible with the inclusion in
\overline{K}.

Axiom
ii)
is satisfied for
\overline{K}
by the following argument. Suppose that there is no cell of
K
containing
x\cup y
then in particular
\overline{x\cup y}=\overline{x}\cap\overline{y}=\emptyset.
If not then let
w
be the cell containing
x\cup y
with minimal rank. The cell
w
is well defined, since if
w^{\prime}
is a cell of
K
such that
x\cup y\subset w^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{rk}(w^{\prime})=\operatorname{rk}(w)
then
x\cup y\subset w\cap w^{\prime}\in K.
If
\operatorname{rk}(w\cap w^{\prime})<\operatorname{rk}(w),
it contradicts the minimality of
\operatorname{rk}(w),
so
\operatorname{rk}(w\cap w^{\prime})=\operatorname{rk}(w)
and this implies
w\cap w^{\prime}=w=w^{\prime}
by
i)
. We therefore obtain
\overline{x}\cap\overline{y}=\overline{w}\in\overline{K},
since the definition of
w
implies the following equivalences: a maximal cell
z
contains
w
if and only if
z
contains
x\cup y,
if and only if
z\in\overline{x}\cap\overline{y}.

In order to prove Axiom
iii)
for
\overline{K},
we pick
\overline{x},\overline{y}\in\overline{K}
such that
\overline{y}\subsetneq\overline{x}.
Lemma
1.16
implies
x\subsetneq y
and as a direct consequence of Axiom
iii)
for
K
there exists a face
y^{\prime}
of
y
containing
x.
By Lemma
1.16
, we have the following identity.

\displaystyle\{\overline{z}\penalty\ |\penalty\ z\in K,\penalty\ z\in\bigtriangledown\left(y^{\prime}\right)\}

\displaystyle=\{\overline{z}\penalty\ |\penalty\ z\in K,\penalty\ y^{\prime}\subset z,\penalty\ \operatorname{rk}(z)=\operatorname{rk}(y^{\prime})+1\}

\displaystyle=\{\overline{z}\in\overline{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \overline{z}\subset\overline{y^{\prime}},\penalty\ \overline{\operatorname{rk}}(\overline{z})=\overline{\operatorname{rk}}(\overline{y^{\prime}})-1\}=\triangle\left(\overline{y^{\prime}}\right).

Therefore we have that
\overline{y}
is a face of
\overline{y^{\prime}}\subset\overline{x}.

Similarly, the following argument shows that the diamond property
iv)
for
\overline{K}
is equivalent to the diamond property for
K.
Take
\overline{y}\subsetneq\overline{x}
such that
\overline{\operatorname{rk}}(\overline{x})=\overline{\operatorname{rk}}(\overline{y})+2
then we have
x\subsetneq y
and
\operatorname{rk}(x)=\operatorname{rk}(y)-2
and this implies that

\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\triangle\left(y\right)=\{z_{1},z_{2}\}

for some
z_{1},z_{2}\in K^{[\operatorname{rk}(x)+1]}=\overline{K}^{[\overline{\operatorname{rk}}(\overline{y})+1]}.
This directly implies that

\triangle\left(\overline{x}\right)\cap\bigtriangledown\left(\overline{y}\right)=\{\overline{z}\penalty\ |\penalty\ z\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\}\cap\{\overline{z}\penalty\ |\penalty\ z\in\triangle\left(y\right)\}=\{\overline{z_{1}},\overline{z_{2}}\}.

Therefore
\overline{K}
is a cc, it is also pure and satisfies
\operatorname{Rk}(\overline{K})=\operatorname{Rk}(K)
by the following reasons: the maximal cells of
\overline{K}
of rank
R
are the images of vertices of
K
under the duality map and since every cell of
K
contains at least one vertex, we have that every cell of
\overline{K}
is contained in
\overline{v}
for some
v\in K.
Since
K
is graph-based, Lemma
1.16
implies that every sub-maximal cell in
\overline{K}
is contained in exactly two maximal cells, hence
\overline{K}
is non-singular and has empty boundary.
Lemma
1.16
also implies that the map
x\mapsto\overline{\overline{x}}
is a poset isomorphism from
K
to
\overline{\overline{K}},
which defines a cc-isomorphism by Lemma
1.2
since
\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{\overline{K}}}(\overline{\overline{x}})=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x).

∎

By Lemma
1.16
, we have in particular that if
v,w\in K^{[0]}
are distinct vertices then
\overline{v}\not\subset\overline{w}.
Therefore if
\overline{v}\subset\overline{w}
then
v=w.
This implies that

\overline{\overline{v}}=\{\overline{w}\in K^{[R]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \overline{v}\subset\overline{w}\}=\{\overline{v}\}.

The vertices of
\overline{\overline{K}}
are then also sets containing one element.

The following notion of dual graph is convenient to formulate the notion of pinch and strongly connected cell complex that are introduced afterwards. It is also used later in the proof of Theorem
3.14
.

Definition 1.18

(Dual graph)
.

Let
K
be a non-singular cc. We defined

\mathcal{G}_{\overline{K}}:=(K^{[R]},\{\overline{y}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\in(K\setminus\partial K)^{[R-1]}\})

to be the
dual graph
of
K.

We point out that the non-singularity of
K
and the diamond property
iv)
guarantee that
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{K}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\partial K}}
are graphs. The notion of strong connectedness defined next is common to different treatments of simplicial complexes and is often used to defined a
pseudo-manifold
as a strongly connected closed simplicial complex.

Definition 1.19

(Strongly connected cc)
.

Let
K
be a non-singular cc.
K
is said to be
strongly-connected
if
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{K}}
is connected.

The following notions of pinch and non-pinching cc will be important when defining our notion of cobordisms introduced in Chapter
3
.

Definition 1.20

(Non-pinching cc)
.

A
pinch
, or
K
-pinch,
is a cell
x\in K
such that
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{K}}\cap\overline{x}
is
not
connected.
K
is said to be
non-pinching
if it contains no
K
-pinch and no
\partial K
-pinch.

Figure
6
illustrates a simple example of pinch. As explained in the caption, this illustration can also help us understand the reason for adding in our definition of a non-pinching cc
K
the condition that
K
also do not contain any
\partial K
-pinch.

Figure 6
:
There are three different ways to look at this illustration. First, one can see it as a portion of a closed 2-cell complex
K
and the central vertex
v
gives an example of a
K
-pinch. The graph
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{K}}\cap\overline{v}
would then be constituted of two disconnected cycles, each containing three vertices and each such vertex corresponding to a 2-cell (triangle) of
K
containing
v.
Second, one could look at this illustration as a portion of a non-singular 3-cell complex
L,
in which
v
is included in two tetrahedra considered as two different 3-cells of
L.
In this case
v
is both a
L
-pinch and a
\partial L
-pinch. The third way to look at this illustration is to consider that the two tetrahedra of
L
containing
v
form a single cell of a non-singular 3-cell complex
L^{\prime}.
In this case
v
is a
\partial L^{\prime}
-pinch, but not a
L^{\prime}
-pinch.

This next lemma and the remark that follows will be useful in Chapter
3
, when considering the boundary of cobordisms.

Lemma 1.21
.

If
K
is a non-singular cc such that there is no
K
-pinch in
\partial K
then
\partial K
is closed.

Proof.

Let
R=\operatorname{Rk}(K).
It is sufficient to show that if
\partial K
contains no
K
-pinch then

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\partial K\right|=2,\quad\forall x\in(\partial K)^{[R-2]}.

We start by showing that
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\partial K\right|\geq 2,
which in particular implies that
\partial(\partial K)=\emptyset.
Since
x\in\partial K
there is
y_{0}\in(\partial K)^{[R-1]}
and
z_{0}\in K^{[R]}
such that
x\subset y_{0}\subset z_{0}
and by the diamond property there exists
y_{1}\in K^{[R-1]}
such that
\triangle\left(z_{0}\right)\cap\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)=\{y_{0},y_{1}\}.
If
y_{1}\not\in\partial K
then there exist
z_{1}\in K^{[R]},\penalty\ y_{2}\in K^{[R-1]}
such that
\bigtriangledown\left(y_{1}\right)=\{z_{0},z_{1}\}
and
\triangle\left(z_{1}\right)\cap\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)=\{y_{1},y_{2}\}.
By repeating this argument we can construct a sequence of elements in
K^{[R-1]},
distinct by non-singularity of
K,
until at some step
k
we get
y_{k}\in\partial K
and this implies
\{y_{0},y_{k}\}\subset\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\partial K.
If by contradiction we assume that there is more that two elements in
\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\partial K
then the previous construction would show that it contains at least four elements and that the corresponding two sequences of elements in
K^{[R]}
sharing an element of
K^{[R-1]}
constitute two disconnected components of
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{K}}\cap\overline{x}.
This would imply that
x\in\partial K
is a
K
-pinch, a contradiction with our hypothesis.
∎

Remark 1.22
.

Proposition
1.17
allows us to make the following observations.
A closed cc
K
is strongly-connected and non-pinching if
\overline{K}
is connected and cell-connected.
Hence a closed
R
-cc
K
is local if and only if
\overline{K}
is a closed strongly-connected non-pinching
R
-cc. We can also note that if a non-singular cc
K
is connected and non-pinching then it is strongly connected.
Therefore, in order to define a class of cc invariant under the duality map, we need the assumptions of locality and non-pinchingness. This will justify our choices of definitions in Chapter
3
.

1.5
Barycentric subdivision and how it determines the underlying cc

In this section, we first introduce a combinatorial definition of barycentric subdivision that one can associate to any poset and explain how it is sometimes used to defined a different notion of dual cell than the one used in our framework. In Chapter
4
we will introduce a generalization of the notion of barycentric subdivision that we will simply call subdivision. We then show Theorem
1.28
, stating the barycentric subdivision characterizes the cc from which it is constructed under relatively general conditions.

We use the following abstract analogue of the first derived barycentric subdivision of a poset, also called order complex.

Definition 1.23

(Barycentric subdivision)
.

Let
(K,\subset)
be a poset. The
(first derived) barycentric subdivision
or
order complex
of
K
is the poset noted
\widecheck{K}
of totally ordered subsets of
K.

Figure
7
gives examples of cells in the barycentric subdivision of a 2-cc. In our context, the term barycentric subdivision is therefore interpreted in a broad sense, but for the case of a simplicial complex obtained from a triangulation of a manifold, this definition corresponds to the face lattice of the barycentric subdivision of the triangulation as defined for example in
[
29
]
.
The barycentric subdivision of a cc is a simplicial complex since condition
a)
follows from the observation that a subset of a totally ordered set is a totally ordered set and condition
b)
is a direct consequence of the definition of the barycentric subdivision.

The barycentric subdivision provides an interesting way to relate a closed cc with its dual, as shown in the next proposition.

Proposition 1.24
.

The barycentric subdivision of a closed cc is isomorphic to the barycentric division of its dual. In other words,
\widecheck{K}\cong\widecheck{\overline{K}}.

Proof.

This follows from the observation that, by Lemma
1.16
, the bijection
\{x\}\mapsto\{\overline{x}\}
from
\widecheck{K}^{[0]}
to
\widecheck{\overline{K}}^{[0]}
induced by the duality map defines an isomorphism of cc. This bijection indeed maps totally ordered subsets of
K
to totally ordered subsets of
\overline{K}
as

\{x_{1}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{k}\}\mapsto\{\overline{x_{k}}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq\overline{x_{1}}\}.

(3)

∎

The barycentric subdivision is sometimes used to define the notion of dual cell, as for example in
[
29
]
. This definition can also be defined in a purely combinatorial manner, but it corresponds to different cells than the cells obtained by the duality map leading to the involution property of Proposition
1.17
. We explain here how to precisely compare these two definitions.

Let us extend a notion we use for a cc
K,
to arbitrary subsets
\mathcal{A}\subset K
by setting

\mathcal{A}^{[r]}:=\mathcal{A}\cap K^{[r]}.

We can also introduce the sets of elements respectively
above
and
below
an element
w
in a poset
(L,\subset):

A_{L}(w):=\{y\in L\penalty\ |\penalty\ w\subset y\},\quad B_{L}(w):=\{y\in L\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\subset w\}.

Let
K
be a closed cc. We will more specifically consider the sets
A_{L}(w),B_{L}(w)
in the cases where
L=\widecheck{K}
and
w=\{x\}\in\widecheck{K}^{[0]}
and the case
L=\widecheck{\overline{K}}
and
w=\{\overline{x}\}\in\widecheck{\overline{K}}^{[0]}
respectively. These sets can be more explicitly expressed as

A_{\widecheck{K}}(\{x\})=\{\{y_{1}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq y_{k}\}\in\widecheck{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\subset y_{1}\},\quad B_{\widecheck{\overline{K}}}(\{\overline{x}\})=\{\{\overline{y_{1}}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq\overline{y_{k}}\}\in\widecheck{\overline{K}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \overline{x}\supset\overline{y_{k}}\}.

Then
A_{\widecheck{K}}(\{x\})
and
B_{\widecheck{\overline{K}}}(\{\overline{x}\})
are isomorphic as posets using the map (
3
), so that we have

A_{\widecheck{K}}(\{x\})\cong\cap B_{\widecheck{\overline{K}}}(\{\overline{x}\})=\widecheck{\overline{K}\cap\overline{x}}.

For the case of a simplicial complex
K,
the map
K\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}\left(\widecheck{K}\right)
defined by

x\longmapsto\overline{x}^{B}:=\widecheck{\overline{K}\cap\overline{x}}\cong\{\{y_{1}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq y_{k}\}\in\widecheck{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\subset y_{1}\}

is equivalent to the definition of dual cell used in
[
29
]
(which explains our choice for the letter
B
in the notation
\overline{x}^{B}
). The dual cell
\overline{x}^{B}
is therefore seen as a sub-simplicial complex of
\widecheck{K}.

Figure 7
:
This illustration represents certain cells of the barycentric subdivision
\widecheck{K}
of a 2-cc
K
and gives three examples of cells in
\overline{K}^{B}.
Being a simplicial complex, each 2-cell of the barycentric subdivision is a triangle and the vertices of the barycentric subdivision are represented using three different symbols depending of the rank of the cells it represent: a vertex of
\widecheck{K}
of the form
\{C\}
where
C\in K^{[2]}
is represented by a cross, a vertex of the form
\{e^{\prime}\}
where
e^{\prime}\in K^{[1]}
is represented by a dash and a vertex of the form
\{v\}
simply corresponds to a vertex of
K.
There are two 2-cells of
\overline{K}^{B},
the first
\overline{v}^{B}
is filled the black wavy pattern, the other
\overline{w}^{B}
is filled with the white wavy pattern. We also have the 1-cell
\overline{e}^{B},
the dual of
e=\{v,w\},
represented by the two thick dashed lines. As a comparison, the dual cell
\overline{v}
used in our framework simply corresponds to the triangle whose vertices are the three 2-cells containing
v.

We can then define the poset
\overline{K}^{B}:=\{\overline{x}^{B}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in K\}
with rank function

\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{K}^{B}}(\overline{x}^{B}):=\operatorname{Rk}(\widecheck{\overline{K}\cap\overline{x}})=\overline{\operatorname{rk}}(\overline{x}).

It is possible to show that the map
\phi(\overline{x}^{B}):=\overline{x}
defines an isomorphism of posets from
\overline{K}^{B}
to
\overline{K}
such that
\overline{\operatorname{rk}}(\phi(\overline{x}^{B}))=\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{K}^{B}}(\overline{x}^{B}).
Nevertheless
\overline{K}^{B}
is not a cc since the cells in
\overline{K}^{B}
are not subsets of
(\overline{K}^{B})^{[0]}
as these cells also contain elements in
\widecheck{K}^{[0]}\setminus(\overline{K}^{B})^{[0]}.

The remainder of this section aims to present an adaptation of the Theorem 3 given by Bayer in
[
21
]
, stating that the barycentric subdivision of a polyhedral complex determines the underlying complex up to duality. The notion of polyhedral complex from
[
21
]
is identical to the notion of complex of polytopes defined for example in
[
77
]
, which will be discussed in more detail in Section
2.3
. We sometimes designate such convex polytopes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as ”embedded polytopes”. Bayer’s proof can nevertheless be easily adapted to our purely combinatorial framework. Theorem
1.28
stated below can then be seen as a relatively straightforward generalization of Bayer’s result to local cell complexes. The main point from Bayer’s proof that needs an additional justification is an observation about the existence of a type of ordering of the faces of an embedded polytope. This observation is also valid for the sub-cells of a cell in a cell complex and this constitutes our Lemma
1.26
. Note that by ”face of a polytope”, we mean any sub-polytope and not necessarily of dimension one lower than the original polytope (as is defined for the cells of a cc).

We start by summarizing the notions needed to state the proof of Theorem
1.28
, closely following the section 3 of
[
21
]
. First, we define what is an orientation in a graph-based cc. For a set of edges
\mathcal{E}\subset\binom{\mathcal{V}}{2}
we use the notation
\mathcal{E}_{\rightarrow}
to denote the set of ordered two-sets of
\mathcal{E}.
An element
e=(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}_{\rightarrow}
is called an
directed edge
,
e_{s}:=x
is called its
source
and
e_{t}:=y
its
target
.

Definition 1.25

(Orientation of a cc)
.

An
orientation
of a graph-based cc
K
is a map
g:K^{[1]}\mapsto K^{[1]}_{\rightarrow}
such that
g(e)^{[0]}=e.

We say that an orientation
g
is
transitive
if, whenever

g(\{x,y\})=(x,y),\quad g(\{y,z\})=(y,z)

for
x,y,z\in K^{[0]}
such that
\{x,y\},\{y,z\}\in K^{[1]}
then
\{x,z\}\in K^{[1]}
and

g(\{x,z\})=(x,z).

In particular an
oriented graph

(\mathcal{G},g)
is a graph
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}),
for example the 1-skeleton of a graph-based cc, with an orientation
g:\mathcal{E}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{\rightarrow}.
An isomorphism of oriented graphs is a cc-isomorphism
\phi:\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
between two oriented graphs
(\mathcal{G},g)
and
(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},g^{\prime})
such that
g^{\prime}
is the image of
g
through
\phi
in the sense that

g^{\prime}(\phi(e))=(\phi(g(e)_{s}),\phi(g(e)_{t})).

We now turn to the notion of uniquely partially orderable cc, a key notion in the proof of the Theorem
1.28
, in which we essentially prove that the barycentric subdivision of a local cell complex is uniquely partially orderable. Let
g
be a transitive orientation of a graph-based cc
K
and let
(x,y),(w,z)\in K^{[1]}_{\rightarrow}.
We say that
(x,y)

directly forces

(w,z)
if either
x=w
and
\{y,z\}\notin K^{[1]}
or
y=z
and
\{x,y\}\notin K^{[1]}.
More generally, we say that
(x,y)

forces

(y,z)
if there is a finite sequence of directed edges starting from
(x,y)
and ending at
(y,z)
such that each directed edge in the sequence directly forces the next one. This relation is an equivalence relation, which allows to decompose
K^{[1]}_{\rightarrow}
into equivalence classes called
implication classes.

K
is called
uniquely partially orderable
if and only if
K
admits exactly two transitive orientations, each being the reverse of the other, or equivalently, if and only if there exists only two possible implication classes in
K^{[1]}_{\rightarrow}.
For example, a transitive orientation on
\widecheck{K}
is simply given by
g_{K}:\widecheck{K}^{[1]}\longrightarrow\widecheck{K^{[1]}}_{\rightarrow},
the orientation induced by the inclusion relation in
K,
i.e. if
x,y\in K
satisfy
x\subsetneq y,
or equivalently
\{x\subsetneq y\}\in(\widecheck{K})^{[1]},
then
g_{K}(\{x\subsetneq y\})=(x,y).
(Recall that our notation
\{x\subsetneq y\}
is a shorthand for an - unordered - set
\{x,y\}
whose elements are totally ordered by the inclusion relation in
K,
so that we also specify this total order by writing
\{x\subsetneq y\}
). A second transitive orientation of
\widecheck{K}
is the
reverse of
g_{K},
denoted
g^{-1}_{K},
and defined by
g^{-1}_{K}(\{x\subsetneq y\}):=(y,x).
In case where
K
is a closed cc then Proposition
1.24
implies

\widecheck{K}\cong\widecheck{\overline{K}}.

Using the later cc-isomorphism, we can identify
\{x\subsetneq y\}\in\widecheck{K}^{[1]}
with
\{\overline{y}\subsetneq\overline{x}\}\in\widecheck{\overline{K}}^{[1]}
and using this identification we have that
g^{-1}
corresponds to the transitive orientation
g_{\overline{K}}
induced by the inclusion relation in
\overline{K}.

We prove the following lemma, which, in the proof from
[
21
]
, corresponds to an observation about the faces of convex polyhedra, where by faces we mean any sub-polyhedra. We will see that it also holds in the case of cc of the form
K\cap z,
for
z\in K^{[R]},
as a consequence of Axioms
iv)
and
ii)
.

Lemma 1.26
.

Let
K
be a
R
-lcc and let
z\in K^{[R]}.
For any
0\leq r\leq R-2
and any
r
-cell
y\in K\cap z,
there is a
(r+1)
-cell
w\in K\cap z
such that
y\not\subset w.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the claim for the case
r=R-2.
The case
r=0,
i.e.
R=2
and
y
is a vertex of a 2-cell
z,
is direct since by Lemma
1.12

z
is a cycle of at least 3 edges. Suppose that
r>0
and take
x\in\triangle\left(y\right),
i.e. such that
\operatorname{rk}(x)=r-1.
By the diamond property
iv)
there exist
\{w_{1},w_{2}\}\subset K^{[r+1]}
and
y^{\prime}\in K^{[r]}
such that

\bigtriangledown\left(y\right)\cap\triangle\left(z\right)=\{w_{1},w_{2}\},\quad\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\triangle\left(w_{1}\right)=\{y,y^{\prime}\}.

Moreover there exists
w\in K^{[r+1]}
such that

\bigtriangledown\left(y^{\prime}\right)\cap\triangle\left(z\right)=\{w_{2},w\}

and
w\in K^{[r+1]}\setminus\{w_{1},w_{2}\}
since otherwise
\{y,y^{\prime}\}\subset w_{1}\cap w_{2}
which contradicts
ii)
. Also
y\not\subset w
since
\bigtriangledown\left(y\right)\cap\triangle\left(z\right)=\{w_{1},w_{2}\}
and this concludes the proof.
∎

We also add the following lemma, which is considered as an obvious statement in
[
21
]
for the case of face lattices of polytopes.

Lemma 1.27
.

A cell complex
K
is determined up to cc-isomorphism by the oriented 1-skeleton of its barycentric subdivision
(\widecheck{K}^{(1)},g_{K}).

Proof.

Let
\{x\}
be a vertex of
\widecheck{K}.
Lets us prove this result by first indicating how to associated to
\{x\}
a set of vertices of
\widecheck{K}
of the form
\{v\}
where
v\in K^{[0]}
corresponding to the vertices of
K
contained in
x.
The elements of the form
\{v\}\in\widecheck{K}^{[0]}
such that
v\in x
are the starting points of all directed paths (i.e. paths using directed edges which target is equal to the source of the next directed edges or the endpoint of the directed path) in
(\widecheck{K}^{(1)},g_{K})
having
\{x\}
as an endpoint and which length is maximal with this property. The rank of
\{x\}\in\widecheck{K}^{[0]}
is the length of any of such directed path. This is well defined by Axiom
iii)
.
∎

We now have the required notions and preliminary results to state and prove the following theorem. This result seems important to us as a general fact about lcc and will be interesting for us in relation to the reconstruction results of Chapter
2
, as explained in Remark
2.47
. It also lead us to introduce a notion of orientation in a cc, but this notion as defined here will not be used again in this work. The work of Basak
[
20
]
introduces another notion of orientation which is central to his treatment of the Poincaré duality for his definition of combinatorial cell complexes.

Theorem 1.28
.

Let
K
and
J
be two lcc having at least two maximal cells and such that
\widecheck{K}\cong\widecheck{J}.
Then we have the following possibilities:

•

if
\partial K
is non-empty then
\partial J
is also non-empty and
K\cong J;

•

if
K
and
J
are closed then either
K\cong J
or
\overline{K}\cong J.

Proof.

Let
(\widecheck{K}^{(1)},g_{K})
and
(\widecheck{J}^{(1)},g_{J})
be the oriented 1-skeletons of the barycentric subdivision of
K
and
J,
where both
g
and
h
are the orientation induced by the inclusion relation in
K
and
J
respectively. Our assumption implies in particular that
\widecheck{K}^{(1)}
and
\widecheck{J}^{(1)}
are cc-isomorphic, we therefore consider these two graphs as identical for simplicity. The strategy of the proof is to show that
\widecheck{K}
is uniquely partially orderable, which would directly imply that
g_{J}=g_{K}
or
g_{J}=g_{K}^{-1}
as defined above.

In case
K
and
J
are closed, the two possibilities
g_{J}=g_{K}
and
g_{J}=g_{K}^{-1}
lead consistent cases since
g_{K}^{-1}=g_{\overline{K}}
and Lemma
1.27
lead
K\cong J
or
\overline{K}\cong J.

If
\partial K
is non-empty then
g^{-1}_{K}
cannot be a transitive orientation induced by the inclusion relation of a local cell complex. To justify this statement, let us assume by contradiction that

g_{K}^{-1}=g_{L}:\widecheck{L}^{[1]}\longrightarrow\widecheck{L}_{\rightarrow}^{[1]}

is a transitive orientation induced by the inclusion relation of a lcc
L.
Consider a maximal cell
y\in\partial K,
which is therefore included in a unique maximal cell
z
of
K.
Then, as shown in Lemma
1.27
,
\{z\}
corresponds to a vertex of
L
and
\{y\}
corresponds to an edge of
L
and this is a contradiction with the fact that every edge of
L
has two vertices. As a consequence, it only leaves
g_{K}=g_{J}
as a possibility and Lemma
1.27
implies
K\cong J.

We therefore turn to the proof that
\widecheck{K}
is uniquely partially orderable by induction on the rank of
K.

For
\operatorname{Rk}(K)=1
we have that
K
is a connected graph and so is
\widecheck{K}.
Every edge
e=\{v,w\}
of
K
is subdivided into two ”half-edges”
\{\{v\},\{e\}\}
and
\{\{w\},\{e\}\}
in
\widecheck{K}^{[1]}.
Moreover, any two edges
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}
of
\widecheck{K}
sharing a vertex in
\widecheck{K}^{[0]}
correspond either to a subdivided edge of
K
or to two ”half-edges of
K
” i.e.
\varepsilon=\{\{v\},\{e\}\},

\varepsilon^{\prime}=\{\{v\},\{e^{\prime}\}\}
where
e,e^{\prime}\in K^{[1]}
and
v=e\cap e^{\prime}\in K^{[0]}.
In both cases there is no edge between the other ends of
\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon^{\prime}
and this means that an orientation of
\varepsilon
directly forces an orientation of
\varepsilon^{\prime}.
The connectedness of
\widecheck{K}
then implies that an orientation of any edge in
\widecheck{K}
force an orientation of any other edge in
\widecheck{K},
which shows that
K
is uniquely partially orderable.

Let
K
be a
R
-lcc with
\left|K^{[R]}\right|\geq 2
and assume that
\widecheck{(K^{\prime})}^{(1)}
is uniquely partially orderable for any lcc
K^{\prime}
of rank smaller or equal to
R-1
with more than one maximal cell. Let
K^{\prime}:=K^{(R-1)}
and
G^{\prime}:=(\widecheck{K^{\prime}})^{(1)}.
Then
K^{\prime}
is a
(R-1)
-lcc with more than one maximal cell and
G^{\prime}
is the sub-graph of
G
restricted to the vertices with corresponding cell in
K
having a rank smaller or equal to
R-1.
By induction
G^{\prime}
is uniquely partially orderable and we show that an orientation of
G^{\prime}
forces an orientation of
G.

Let
z\in K^{[R]}
and let
\{z\}
be the corresponding vertex in
G.
Since there are at least two maximal cells in
K,
there is one vertex
v
of
z
contained in an edge
e\in K^{[1]}
such that
e\not\subset z.
Therefore we obtain

\{\{v\},\{z\}\}\in G^{[1]},\quad\{\{v\},\{e\}\}\in(G^{\prime})^{[1]}\text{ and }\{\{z\},\{e\}\}\notin G^{[1]}

and this implies that
(\{v\},\{e\})
directly forces
(\{v\},\{z\}).
We conclude the proof by showing that
(\{v\},\{z\})
directly forces all edges of
G
containing
\{z\}.

Indeed, all edges of
G
containing
\{z\}
are of the form
\{\{z\},\{w\}\}
where
w
is a sub-cell of
z.
By Lemma
1.26
we can find an ordering of the cells in
K\cap z,
starting with any vertex in
z,
so that no two consecutive sub-cells contain one another. This corresponds to an ordering of the vertices in
G
neighbouring
\{z\},
so that no two consecutive vertices are linked by an edge. We can suppose that this vertex ordering starts with
v
and this means that
(\{v\},\{z\})
forces each edge containing
\{z\}
in G and this concludes the proof.
∎

1.6
Topological considerations

In this section, we mention two different ways to assign a topology to a cc: the first one is from
[
20
]
and the second, using a notion of a geometrical realization, is from
[
75
]
. It seems clear that these two topologies are equivalent, but we have not proven this here. We then give the definitions of star, link and homogeneity in a cc and define the notions of cellular and simplicial manifold. All homeomorphisms here are piecewise-linear homeomorphisms (as defined e.g. in
[
29
]
).

In
[
20
]
(setion 2.4) the topology of a cc
K
is defined by setting all closed subsets
C
of
K
to be the subsets of
K
such that if
x\in K,\penalty\ y\in C,\penalty\ x\subset y
then
x\in C.
If we define the closure of a subset
S\subset K
to be the set of cells of
K
that are contained in some cell in
S
then the closed subsets of
K
are given by the closures of subsets of
K.
As a consequence,
K
is not Hausdorff if it has rank one or above, since each set of the form
\{x\}
where
x\in K
has a rank higher or equal to 1 is a non-closed ”point” in this topology. For example if
x=\{v,w\}\in K^{[1]},
this topology implies that
\{\{v,w\}\}
is an ”open edge” whereas the closure of
\{x\}
corresponds to a ”closed edge”
\{\{v,w\},\{v\},\{w\}\}
and this is coherent with the following definitions obtained using a geometrical realization.

Another way to define a topology on a cc
K,
described for example in
[
75
]
, is to consider
K
simply as a poset to which one associates the topology obtained from the geometrical realization of its barycentric subdivision
\widecheck{K}.
The geometrical realization of a simplicial complex is defined in the following way. A
d
-dimensional geometric simplex
\sigma
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined as the convex hull of
d+1
points in
\mathbb{R}^{n},
each such point being a vertex of
\sigma.
The set of sub-simplicies of
\sigma,

B(\sigma),
contains all the convex hulls of subsets of vertices of
\sigma.
A geometric simplicial complex
\Lambda
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined as a non-empty collection of geometric simplicies satisfying that if
\sigma\in\Lambda
and
\tau\in B(\sigma)
then
\tau\in\Lambda
and if
\tau,\sigma\in K
then
\tau\cap\sigma\in B(\sigma)\cap B(\sigma)
(the roles of
K
and
\Lambda
are exchanged compared to the notations used in
[
75
]
). From a geometric simplicial complex
\Lambda,
one can define a (abstract) simplicial complex
K(\Lambda)
corresponding to the poset of non-empty faces of
\Lambda
and say that
\Lambda
realizes
K.
It is an exercise in
[
67
]
(2.27(1)) to show that every simplicial complex can be realized as a geometric simplicial complex and such a realization is unique up to homeomorphism. We can therefore define the topology of
K
to be the topology of
\Lambda
as a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n},
with usual topology. As a direct consequence of Proposition
1.24
,
K
and
\overline{K}
are homeomorphic as topological spaces.

The notions of star and link are usually defined in the context of simplicial complexes. These definitions are easily adapted to the case of cc in the following way.

Definition 1.29

(Star and link of a cell)
.

The
star
of a cell
x
of a cell complex
K
is defined by

\operatorname{St}_{K}(x)=\{y\in K\penalty\ |\penalty\ \exists w\in K\text{ such that }x\vee y=w\}.

The
link
of
x
is defined as

\operatorname{Lk}_{K}(x)=\{y\in\operatorname{St}_{K}(x)\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\cap y=\emptyset\}.

The previous definitions of star and link are then equivalent to the definitions given for example in
[
29
]
for the case of simplicial complex, since if
\sigma
and
\eta
are sub-simplicies of a simplex in a simplicial complex
K
then
\sigma\vee\eta=\sigma\cup\eta\in K.

Homogeneity in a cc is then defined using the star of each vertex and the topology associated to a cc introduced before.

Definition 1.30

(Homogeneous vertex in a cc)
.

A vertex
v
in a
R
-cc is
homogeneous
if any geometrical realization of the cc
K\cap\operatorname{St}_{K}(v)
is homeomorphic to the closed
R
-dimensional Euclidean ball.
If every vertex in
K
is homogeneous, we say that
K
is
homogeneous
.

An example of non-homogeneous vertex is given in Figure
17
of Chapter
3
. It is then natural to introduce the following definitions of cellular manifold and simplicial manifold.

Definition 1.31

(Cellular and simplicial manifold)
.

A
cellular
R
-manifold
is a homogeneous
R
-cc. A
simplicial (cellular) manifold
is a cellular manifold such that the underlying cc is simplicial.

A
discretization
of a piecewise-linear manifold
M
is a cellular manifold
K
which geometrical realization is homeomorphic to
M
and a
triangulation
of
M
is a simplicial discretization of
M.

2  Structures in a simple cell complex and reconstruction

The aim of this chapter is to prove Theorem
2.42
as well as Corollaries
2.45
and
2.46
of Section
2.6
. These results can be interpreted as a method for reconstructing a local even simply connected simple cell complex from its 2-skeleton. The core of this chapter is thus devoted to the introduction of the notions of fullness, evenness, monodromy-freedom and simple connectedness and prove some related basic facts, with the exception of Section
2.3
where we make links with some well-known notions of discrete geometry. A key technical result that allows to prove the results on reconstruction is the ”Extension Lemma”
2.40
. This lemma will be the first result involving all notions required for the main results of Section
2.6
as well as two newly introduced notions of this chapter defined in the context of a cc of rank 2 or higher: a connection and covariant edge fields.
We start with a short section introducing these two new notions, followed by Section
2.2
where we introduce the notions of full and simple cell complexes that characterize the duals of simplicial cell complexes. In Section
2.3
, we make a digression from the reconstruction problem about an abstract definition of polytopes and shellability, using the notion of simplicity. We first show the Euler-Poincaré formula for shellable local cell complexes. We then introduce a notion of 2-shellability, only involving the cells of the 2-skeleton, and show in Theorem
2.25
that the 2-shellability of a simple cell complex is equivalent to the shellability of its dual. We then come back to the discussion of reconstruction-related notions in Section
2.4
, a mostly technical section whose principal goal is to define simply connected cc. Simple connectedness in a cc is defined using a combinatorial notion of homotopy that naturally arise from the structure of 2-cc. In Section
2.5
, we introduce the concept of monodromy-free and even cc. We then have all the ingredients needed to give a relatively quick proof of the Extension Lemma at the beginning of Section
2.6
, which allows us to prove the main results on reconstruction in the remainder of this last section.

In this chapter 2-cc play an important role. We often use the letter
C
to denote a
2
-cell of a cc and we also recall that if
K
is a pure 2-cc then the dual set of
A\subset K^{[0]}
corresponds to

\overline{A}:=\{C\in K^{[2]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ A\subset C\}.

In what follows, we will choose to state certain results using a 2-cc
K
in order to formulate the proof in terms of dual sets as above. In these cases
K
often play the role of the 2-skeleton of a cc of higher rank for which the corresponding result is also true.

It will also be convenient to use the following notation to denote the set of edges included in a cell
x\in K
and containing a vertex
v\in x,

\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}:=\{e\in\mathcal{E}_{v}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\subset x\}.

More generally, if
\mathcal{A}\subset K,
we define

\mathcal{E}_{v}^{\mathcal{A}}:=\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}.

As an example used in Section
2.1
, if
v,w
are vertices of a pure graph-based
2
-cc
K
such that
\{v,w\}\in K^{[1]},
we consider the following subset of
\mathcal{E}_{v}:

\mathcal{E}_{v}^{\overline{w}}:=\{e\in\mathcal{E}_{v}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \exists C\in\overline{w}\text{ s.t. }e\subset C\}=\{e\in\mathcal{E}_{v}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \overline{\{v,w\}}\cap\overline{e}\neq\emptyset\}.

In Section
2.2
we introduce the notion of full cc and this notion in particular implies that
\mathcal{E}_{v}^{\overline{w}}=\mathcal{E}_{v}
for all
w\in\mathcal{N}_{v}.
Since these edge sets will for the most part be used in the case of full cc, the set
\mathcal{E}_{v}^{\overline{w}}
is almost exclusively used to introduce a general definition of connection in the next section.

2.1
Connection and covariant edge fields

In this first section, we define the notion of a connection as a mapping between edges of neighbouring vertices canonically associated to a pure graph-based cc of rank 2 or higher. This connection is an important tool for this chapter, as well as the notion of covariant edge fields introduced next which is the main object of the Extension Lemma
2.40
.

The next Lemma is used to introduce the
connection

\nabla
for the case of a pure graph-based cc
K
of rank 2. The connection associated to a pure graph-based cc
L
of rank higher or equal to 2 is simply defined as the connection associated to its 2-skeleton
K=L^{(2)}.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
K
be a pure graph-based 2-cc and
\{v,w\}\in K^{[1]}.
There is a bijection

\displaystyle\nabla_{w}^{v}:\mathcal{E}_{v}^{\overline{w}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{w}^{\overline{v}}

\displaystyle e_{v}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\nabla_{w}^{v}e_{v}=:e_{w},

as illustrated in Figure
8
, such that

1
)

\nabla_{w}^{v}\{v,w\}=\{v,w\},

2
)

If
C\in\overline{\{v,w\}}
then
e_{v}\subset C
if and only if
e_{w}\subset C,

3
)

(\nabla_{w}^{v})^{-1}=\nabla_{v}^{w}.

Figure 8
:
This figure illustrates how the connection
\nabla_{w}^{v}
maps an edge
e_{v}\in\mathcal{E}_{v}\setminus\{v,w\}
to an edge
e_{w}\in\mathcal{E}_{w}\setminus\{v,w\}
using a
2
-cell
C_{e_{v}}
defined as the unique
2
-cell in
\overline{v}
containing
e_{v}
and
\{v,w\}.

Proof.

We set
\nabla_{w}^{v}\{v,w\}=\{v,w\}.
Let
e_{v}\in\mathcal{E}_{v}^{\overline{w}}\setminus\{v,w\}
and let
C_{e_{v}}\in\overline{e_{v}}\cap\overline{\{v,w\}}.
Since
e_{v}\neq\{v,w\}
we have
\left|\overline{e_{v}}\cap\overline{\{v,w\}}\right|=1
as two 2-cells cannot intersect on more than one edge by Axiom
ii)
.
C_{e_{v}}
is then the unique 2-cell containing
e_{v}
and
\{v,w\}.
In particular
C_{e_{v}}\in\overline{w},
so there exists a unique edge
e_{w}\in\mathcal{E}_{w}\setminus\{v,w\}
such that
\{\{v,w\},e_{w}\}\subset C_{e_{v}},
which implies
e_{w}\in\mathcal{E}_{w}^{\overline{v}},
so that we can set
\nabla_{w}^{v}e_{v}=e_{w}.
The uniqueness of
C_{e_{v}}
and
e_{w}
ensures
2)
and
\nabla_{v}^{w}e_{w}=e_{v}.

∎

The connection is also a convenient tool in the formulation of Lemma
2.7
and in Definition
2.33
of monodromy-free cc. From this mapping, one is also naturally lead to consider the notion of covariant edge fields, introduced next.

Definition 2.2

(Covariant Edge field)
.

Let
K
be a cc a let
\mathcal{D}\subset K^{[0]}
be non-empty. An
edge field on
\mathcal{D}
is a map

\phi:\mathcal{D}\longrightarrow K^{[1]}

such that
\phi(v)\in\mathcal{E}_{v}
for all
v\in\mathcal{D}.
We will sometimes denote the
domain of
\phi

\mathcal{D}
by
\mathcal{D}_{\phi}.

An edge field
\phi
in a full graph-based cc
K
is said to be
covariant
if, whenever an edge
\{v,w\}\in K^{[1]}
is included in
\mathcal{D}_{\phi}
then
\nabla_{w}^{v}\phi(v)=\phi(w).

Covariant edge fields, encoding the edge mapping induced by the connection, are a key tool in the proof of the Theorem
2.42
where they allow to construct ”induced cells” by identifying edge fields that can be interpreted as ”tangent” and ”normal” to a given cell. In this sense the connection can in fact be interpreted as defining a notion of ”parallel transport” in a cc.

2.2
Full and simple cc

In this section we introduce the notions of regularity and edge-regularity in a cc and we explain how these notions are implied by the notion of fullness in a cc that is defined next. Fullness is the object of two lemmas and is used as a characteristic property in our definition of simple cc. We finally show that simple cc are precisely the duals of simplicial complexes.

The following notion of
n
-regularity is common to many contexts involving graphs.

Definition 2.3

(
n
-regular cc)
.

A graph-based cc
K
is said to be
n
-regular if
\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}\right|=n
for all
v\in K^{[0]}
or equivalently if
K^{(1)}
is a
n
-regular graph.

When considering cc of rank 2, it is natural to also define the notion of edge-regularity as follows.

Definition 2.4

(
n
-edge-regular 2-cc)
.

A 2-cc
K
is
n
-edge-regular
if
\left|\overline{e}\right|=n
for all
e\in K^{[1]}.

As a consequence of the diamond property
iv)
, the notions of regularity and edge-regularity in a cc are related. The precise relation is given in the next remark mentioned in Lemma
2.7
.

Remark 2.5
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
n-1\geq m\geq 0.
An
n
-regular 2-cc is
m
-edge regular if and only if
\left|\overline{v}\right|=nm/2
for all
v\in K^{[0]},
as showed by the following counting argument:

\displaystyle 2\left|\overline{v}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{C\in\overline{v}}2=\sum_{C\in\overline{v}}\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}^{C}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\{(e,C)\in\mathcal{E}_{v}\times\overline{v}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\subset C\}\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\mathcal{E}_{v}}\left|\overline{e}\right|=m\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}\right|=m\deg(v)=mn,

where the second equality uses that
v
as two neighbors in
C
by the diamond property
iv)
and the penultimate equality uses that
K
is
m
-edge-regular. In particular, in a 2-edge-regular 2-cc we have
\deg(v)=\left|\overline{v}\right|.

Fullness in a cc, introduced next, is a key characteristic of cc dual to simplicial complexes and formalizes the idea that one cannot add more cell to the set of cells containing a given vertex while complying with Axiom
ii)
. This dualizes the property of (maximal) simplices in a simplicial complex for which one cannot add more faces, as their faces are already given by all subsets of their set of vertices. Since in what follows we will particularly need to use this assumption for the case of 2-cc, or 2-skeletons of higher dimensional cc, we chose to reserve the terminology ”full” to denote the fullness property for the case of 2-cc and only specify a rank
r
when using the more general notion of
r
-fullness.

Definition 2.6

(Full cc)
.

Let
K
be a cc of rank
R\geq 2
and let
2\leq r\leq R.
We say that
K
is
r
-full
if for all
v\in K^{[0]},
for all
2\leq k\leq r
and for all
S\subset\mathcal{E}_{v}
such that
\left|S\right|=k,
there is a (unique, by Lemma
2.8
)
r
-cell
x\in K
such that
\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}=S.
We simply say that
K
is
full
if
K
is 2-full.

In particular a
R
-full
R
-cc is pure. The following lemma constitutes a first useful observation one can make about full connected cc.

Lemma 2.7
.

A connected cc
K
is full if and only if there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
K
is a
(n-1)
-edge-regular
n
-regular 2-cc.

Proof.

First, as a consequence of Remark
2.5
, a
n
-regular 2-cc is
(n-1)
-edge regular if and only if
\left|\overline{v}\right|=n(n-1)/2.

Therefore if
K
is full and
\{v,w\}\in K^{[1]}
then
\nabla_{w}^{v}
is a bijection from
\mathcal{E}_{v}
to
\mathcal{E}_{w}.
Also, since
K^{(1)}
is connected,
K^{(1)}
is
n
-regular where
n=\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}\right|
and

\left|\overline{v}\right|=\left|\binom{\mathcal{E}_{v}}{2}\right|=\binom{\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}\right|}{2}=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}.

Conversely, if
K
is a
(n-1)
-edge-regular
n
-regular 2-cc and
v\in K^{[0]}
then

\frac{n(n-1)}{2}=\left|\overline{v}\right|\leq\left|\binom{\mathcal{E}_{v}}{2}\right|=\frac{n(n-1)}{2},

so
\left|\overline{v}\right|=\left|\{\{e_{1},e_{2}\}\subset\mathcal{E}_{v}\}\right|
and
K
is full.
∎

An important consequence of
k
-fullness is the following useful result, which will be used several times in the remainder of this chapter.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
K
be an
R
-cc and suppose that
K
is
k
-full for a given
k
such that
2\leq k\leq R.
Then for all
x\in K
such that
1\leq\operatorname{rk}(x)\leq k
and all
v\in x,
we have

\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}\right|=\operatorname{rk}(x)

and
x
is the unique
\operatorname{rk}(x)
-cell
y
such that
\mathcal{E}_{v}^{y}=\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
1\leq\operatorname{rk}(x)\leq k.

The claim for
\operatorname{rk}(x)=1
is direct. For
x\in K^{[2]}
and
v\in x,
we have
\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}\right|=2
by the diamond property
iv)
and if
x,y\in K^{[2]}
satisfy
\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}=\mathcal{E}_{v}^{y}
then
e\subset x\cap y
for all
e\in\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}.
This implies
\operatorname{rk}(x\cap y)\geq 2
and therefore
x\cap y=x=y.

Suppose that the claim holds for all cells in
K^{(r)}
for some
2\leq r\leq k-1
and let
x\in K^{[r+1]}
be such that
v\in x.
By the property
iii)
, there exists
x_{s}\in K^{[s]},

s=r-1,r
such that

v\in x_{r-1}\subset x_{r}\subset x.

By the diamond property there exists
x_{r}^{\prime}\in K^{[r]}\setminus\{x_{r}\}
such that
x_{r-1}\subset x_{r}^{\prime}\subset x
and by the induction hypothesis there is an edge
e_{r}\in\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x_{r}^{\prime}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x_{r}}\subset\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}.
Since
K
is
k
-full, there is
y\in K^{[r+1]}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{v}^{y}=\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x_{r}}\cup\{e_{r}\}.
Therefore we have that
\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x\cap y}\right|=r+1
and this implies
\operatorname{rk}(x\cap y)\geq r+1
by induction hypothesis. Hence
x\cap y=x=y
and we have
\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}\right|=r+1=\operatorname{rk}(x).
Similarly, the uniqueness part is proven as follows. Let
y\in K^{[r+1]}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{v}^{y}=\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}.
By the previous argument
\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x\cap y}\right|=\operatorname{rk}(x\cap y)
and
\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x\cap y}\right|=\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}\right|=r+1
and this implies that
x\cap y=x=y.

∎

The following then serves as our definition of a simple cc.

Definition 2.9

(Simple cc)
.

A
R
-cc such that
R\geq 2
is
simple
if it is closed and
R
-full. A
R
-cc such that
R\leq 1
is
simple
if and only if it is closed.

The next lemma provides an alternative definition of a simple cc as a graph-based
R
-full
(R+1)
-regular
R
-cc.

Lemma 2.10
.

A connected
R
-full
R
-cc is closed if and only if it is graph-based and
(R+1)
-regular.

Proof.

Let
K
be a connected
R
-full cell complex of rank
R.
By Lemma
2.7

K
is
n
-regular and Lemma
2.8
implies that
n\geq R
and that for all
1\leq r<s\leq R
the number of
s
-cells containing a given
r
-cell
x
is given by

\left|\{S\subset\mathcal{E}_{v}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x_{r}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \left|S\right|=s-r\}\right|=\binom{n-r}{s-r},

where
v\in x_{r}.
For the case
s=R,\penalty\ r=R-1
and for any
y\in K^{[R-1]},
we obtain that

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(y\right)\right|=\binom{n-r}{s-r}=\binom{n-R+1}{1}=n-R+1=2\quad\text{if and only if}\quad n=R+1.

∎

Using the previous lemma, we are then able to prove the main result of this section.

Proposition 2.11
.

A connected closed cc
K
is simple if and only if
\overline{K}
is simplicial.

Proof.

Suppose that
K
is a connected simple
R
-cc. In order to show that
J=\overline{K}
is simplicial it is sufficient to prove that any subset of a maximal cell
z
of
J
is a cell of
J.
Since
K
is
(R+1)
-regular by Lemma
2.10
,
\overline{z}\in K^{[0]}
is contained in
(R+1)
edges, which implies that
\left|\triangle\left(z\right)\right|=R+1.

By Lemma
2.8
we have that

\left|z\right|=\left|\overline{\overline{z}}\right|=\left|\{y\in K^{[R]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \overline{z}\in y\}\right|=\binom{R+1}{R}=R+1.

As a consequence of Lemma
2.8
, we also have that
\left|x\right|=R
for all
x\in\triangle\left(z\right),
since each vertex
v\in x\subset z
corresponds to a configuration
\overline{z}\in\overline{x}\subset\overline{v}\in K^{[R]},
which is bijectively mapped to a set
S\subset\mathcal{E}_{\overline{z}}
such that
\left|S\right|=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\overline{v})=R
and
\overline{x}\in S
and there are
\binom{R}{R-1}=R
such sets. Therefore we have

\triangle\left(z\right)=\{z\setminus\{v\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\in z\}.

Using that
J\cup\{\emptyset\}
is closed under intersection, this implies that
J\cap z=\mathcal{P}\left(z\right)
and thus
J
is simplicial.

Conversely, if
J=\overline{K}
is a connected closed simplicial
R
-cc and
v\in K^{[0]},
let
S\subset\mathcal{E}_{v},\penalty\ \left|S\right|=r.
If we set
z:=\overline{v}
we obtain the following inclusions

\{\overline{e}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\in S\}:=\{y_{1},\dots,y_{r}\}\subset\triangle\left(z\right)\subset J^{[R-1]}.

Since
J
is simplicial, for all
1\leq i\leq r
we have
y_{i}=z\setminus\{x_{i}\}
for some (distinct)
x_{i}\in z
and therefore

y_{1}\cap\dots\cap y_{r}=z\setminus\{x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\}\in J^{[R-r]},

which implies that
\overline{y_{1}\cap\dots\cap y_{r}}
is the
r
-cell of
K
containing all edges in
S.

∎

2.3
Digression on polytopes and shellability

Now that we introduced the notion of simple cell complex, we would like to discuss some links between the cell complexes considered in this thesis and some common notions of discrete geometry that could be relevant when stepping into physical considerations: polytopes and the related notion of shellability for cell complexes. Polytopes or polyhedral complexes appear as a natural choice to generalize simplices or simplicial complexes. For example, in
[
25
]
three dimensional polyhedra are used as natural generalizations of a tetrahedron to define a model of three-dimensional quantum geometry related to the spin foam or Loop Quantum Gravity approach. The notion of shellability is an assumption one can make on some discretizations to ensure that it satisfies the Euler-Poincaré formula. In spirit, shellable objects are iteratively defined as being constructed using pieces of a given dimension
d
glued together in a given order, satisfying that the next piece is intersecting the previous ones on a shellable object of dimension
d-1.

Convex polytopes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(or equivalently polyhedra), can be defined in many equivalent ways (see e.g. Theorem 2.15
[
77
]
). We sometimes designate such convex polytopes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as
embedded polytopes,
as opposed to the notion of abstract polytopes we introduce below. The notion of
face of an embedded polytope
(defined on p.51 of
[
77
]
) is more general than the notion of face used in this work. Polytopes can be arranged into a
polyhedral complex
: a finite collection
\mathcal{C}
of polyhedra in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
containing every face of each of its polyhedron and such that the intersection of two polyhedra
P,Q\in\mathcal{C}
is a face of both
P
and
Q,
with the convention that the empty polyhedron is a face of any polyhedron.

Within their combinatorial theory, convex polytopes are often reduced to their face lattice, i.e. the poset of their faces ordered by inclusion. The boundary of a polytope forms a polyhedral complex and the face lattice of a polytope is characterized by the face lattice of its boundary. The face lattices obtained in this way constitute examples of cell complexes as defined here. The term ”simple” is used in this context to denote polytopes such that (the face lattice of) their boundary is dual to a simplicial complex. Therefore our notion of simplicity for cell complexes is equivalent to the corresponding notion for polytopes when restricted to cc that can be realized as such.
Typical examples of cc that cannot be realized as a face lattice of a polyhedral complex are given using cc having cells with non-trivial topology. We will come back to this point in the discussion given after the Proposition
2.20
on the Euler-Poincaré formula.

In
[
58
]
, McMullen and Schulte introduced the notion of abstract polytopes, a notion that is designed to possess the main characteristics of the face lattice of polytopes, but without any a priori notion of embedding in Euclidean space nor convexity and thus share some properties with our definition of cell complexes. Their definition of polytopes is however more general than the definition we give next, as it does not require faces to be characterized by their vertices nor to be stable under intersection (including
\emptyset
as a face).

In order to introduce the definition of polytopes, we need the following notion of section.

Definition 2.12
.

A
section
in a cc
K
is a sub-poset of
K
of the form

\mathopen{[}x,y\mathclose{]}:=\{w\in K\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\subset w\subset y\},

where
x\in K\cup\{\emptyset\}
and
y\in K
such that
x\subset y.
A section
\mathopen{[}x,y\mathclose{]}
is
connected
if
\operatorname{rk}(y)-\operatorname{rk}(x)\leq 2,
where we set
\operatorname{rk}(\emptyset)=-1,
or, for all
x\subsetneq w,w^{\prime}\subsetneq y,
there exists a sequence
w_{0}=w,w_{1},\dots,w_{k}=w^{\prime}
such that
x\subsetneq w_{i}\subsetneq y
and
w_{i}\subset w_{i+1}
or
w_{i+1}\subset w_{i}
for all
i=0,\dots,k-1.

Definition 2.13

(Polytope)
.

An pure
R
-cc
K
is a
polytope of rank
R
if
\left|K^{[R]}\right|=1
and every section in
K
is connected.

For example, if
K
is a polytope of rank
R
then in particular
K
has no
\partial K
-pinch
x
such that
\operatorname{rk}(x)\geq R-2,
as it would imply that the section
\mathopen{[}x,z\mathclose{]}
is not connected, where
z
is the unique maximal cell of
K.
In
[
58
]
, the property that each section is connected is called strong-connectedness. We already use this terminology here to denote the characteristic property of a pseudo-manifold.

The following result gives sufficient conditions for the cells of a local cell complex to be seen as abstract polytopes. It is the only result in this work that do not hold for cell complexes of arbitrary rank.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let
K
be a local simple cell complex of rank
R\leq 4.
Then
K\cap x,
and thus
\overline{K}\cap\overline{x},
are polytopes for all
x\in K.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the case
R=4.
Let
\mathopen{[}a,b\mathclose{]}
be a section in
K\cap x.
If
\operatorname{rk}(b)-\operatorname{rk}(a)\leq 2
then
\mathopen{[}a,b\mathclose{]}
is connected by definition and if
a=\emptyset,b\in K
then
\mathopen{[}a,b\mathclose{]}
is connected since
K
is cell-connected.

In the case
a\in K^{[0]},b\in K^{[3]}\cup K^{[4]},

\mathopen{[}a,b\mathclose{]}
is connected since
K
is full and therefore for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{v}
there is a 2-cell
C\in K^{[2]}
containing
v
as well as
e_{1}
and
e_{2}.

Finally, for the case
a\in K^{[1]},b\in K^{[4]},
the section
\mathopen{[}a,b\mathclose{]}
is connected if for all
C_{1},C_{2}\in K^{[2]}
such that
a\subset C_{i}\subset b,i=1,2,
there is
B\in K^{[3]}
such that
C_{i}\subset B\subset b,\penalty\ i=1,2.
Finding such
B
amounts to find an edge
e\in\overline{K}^{[1]}
such that

\overline{K}^{[0]}\ni\overline{b}\in e\subset\overline{a}\in\overline{K}^{[3]}

and such that
e\in\overline{C_{1}}\cap\overline{C_{2}}.
Such an edge exists as
\overline{K}
is a simplicial complex by Proposition
2.11
and therefore
\overline{a}
is a tetrahedron and
\overline{C_{i}}
is a triangle of
\overline{a}
for
i=1,2.

∎

We now turn to the notion of shelling of a cc and the proof the Euler-Poincaré formula for shellable complexes in Proposition
2.20
. Both the proof of the latter result and the following definition of shelling are closely inspired by the content of Sections 8.1 & 8.2 of
[
77
]
, containing also many examples of shellable polyhedral complexes that also constitute examples for our framework.

Definition 2.15

(Shelling and shellable cell complex)
.

A shelling of a non-singular
R
-cell complex
K
is defined in the following recursive manner.

1.

For
R=0,

K
is a point and has a shelling which is the trivial linear order on the 1-element set
K^{[0]}=K.

2.

For
R\geq 1,
a shelling of
K
is a linear ordering of its facets
K^{[R]}=\{z_{1},\dots,z_{N}\}
such that
\partial(K\cap z_{1})
has a shelling and for all
1\leq k\leq N,
the cc

K\cap\left(z_{k}\cap\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{k-1}z_{j}\right)\right)

(4)

is a non-singular sub-
(R-1)
-cell complex of
K
which has a shelling that can be completed into a shelling of
\partial(K\cap z_{k})
and such that (
4
) has non-empty boundary for
1<k<N
and empty boundary for
k=N
(i.e. (
4
) is equal to
\partial(K\cap z_{N})
) only if
k=N
and
K
is closed.

A cc is shellable if it is non-singular and has a shelling.

We make the following remark about what are 1-dimensional shellings according to our definition, which will be used in the proof of the Euler-Poincaré formula
2.20
.

Remark 2.16
.

The previous definition differs slightly from the one given in
[
77
, p.233]
as in our setup a shellable 1-cell complex is either a tree, if it has a non-empty boundary, or a cycle, if it is closed, as opposed to being any connected graph as allowed by Ziegler’s definition.
This allows to dervie the Euler-Poincaré formula also for shellable cell complexes that are not the boundary complex of a polytope.
Also, we require the cc (
4
) to have a non-empty boundary for all
1<k<N,
as it is not clear a priori that it is the case in general. This allows to prove that the reverse order of a shelling is also a shelling.

The barycentric subdivision of a shellable cell complex is shellable, as shown in the next lemma. This is a relatively basic result that we will use in Corollary
2.21
. It is also for example shown in
[
1
]
that the second derived subdivision of any convex polytope is shellable and that a triangulated manifold is a PL-sphere or PL-ball if and only if for some
m\geq 0
its
m
-th derived subdivision is shellable.
There exists however an example of a non-shellable triangulation of a PL-ball (
[
53
]
) or even simple examples of convex polytopes such as a tetrahedra (
[
70
]
) admitting non-shellable triangulations.

Lemma 2.17
.

The barycentric subdivision of a shellable cell complex is shellable.

Proof.

Let
K
be a shellable cell complex. We prove the statement by induction on the rank
R=\operatorname{Rk}(K).
We first argue that it is sufficient to prove that the statement holds for each sub-cell complex
K\cap z
where
z\in K^{[R]}.
This follows from the observation that if we have a shelling order of
K\cap z
for each maximal cell
z
then we can obtain a shelling of
\widecheck{K}
by combining the shellings of each sub-cell complex
K\cap z
according to the order on
K^{[R]}
defined by the shelling of
K.
For this we use that
\widecheck{K}
is a simplicial complex, therefore the cell complex defined at each step using (
4
), with
\widecheck{K}
instead of
K
, is simply the restriction of
\widecheck{K}
to a
(R-1)
-simplex. By the previous observation, the proof for the cases
R=0,1
is clear. This same observation also implies the induction step relatively directly, by the following argument. By Definition
2.15
a maximal cell
z\in K^{[R]}
satisfies that
\partial(K\cap z)=K\cap\triangle\left(z\right)
is shellable and by induction we also have that its barycentric subdivision
\widecheck{\partial(K\cap z)}
is shellable. A shelling of the latter cell complex directly provides a shelling of
K\cap z,
as the map between
\left(\widecheck{\partial(K\cap z)}\right)^{[R-1]}
and
\widecheck{(K\cap z)}^{[R]}
defined by

\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{R-1}\}\mapsto\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{R-1}\subsetneq z\}

is clearly a poset isomorphism. Hence the image of a shelling of
\widecheck{\partial(K\cap z)}
through this map is a shelling of
\widecheck{(K\cap z)}.
Therefore we obtain that
\widecheck{K\cap z}
is shellable for all
z\in K^{[R]}
and we can conclude that
\widecheck{K}
is also shellable.
∎

For our purposes, the Euler characteristic is defined as follows.

Definition 2.18

(Euler characteristic)
.

The
Euler characteristic
of a cell complex
K
of rank
R
is defined by

\chi(K)=\sum_{r=0}^{R}(-1)^{r}\left|K^{[r]}\right|.

As a direct consequence of the latter definition, we can make the following observation, used later in Corollary
2.26
.

Remark 2.19
.

Since
\left|\overline{K}^{[R-r]}\right|=\left|K^{[r]}\right|
we have
\chi(K)=(-1)^{R}\chi(\overline{K}),
for
K
closed.

We are now able to state the Euler-Poincaré formula for cc, which proof is essentially identical to the proof of Corollary 8.17 of
[
77
]
.

Proposition 2.20

(Euler-Poincaré formula)
.

Let
K
be a shellable
R
-cell complex. Then if
\partial K\neq\emptyset,
we have

\chi(K)=1,

otherwise, i.e. if
K
is closed, we have

\chi(K)=1+(-1)^{R}.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction on
R\geq 0
and
N=\left|K^{[R]}\right|,
using that for any sub-cell complex
J,J^{\prime}
of
K,

\chi
satisfies
