\chi(J)+\chi(J^{\prime})=\chi(J\cup J^{\prime})+\chi(J\cap J^{\prime}).

(5)

The claim for
R\leq 1
and all
N\geq 0
is clear by Remark
2.16
. For
R\geq 2,
let
K^{[R]}=\{z_{1},\dots,z_{N}\}
be a shelling of
K.

For
1\leq k\leq N,
let
J_{k}:=K\cap\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}z_{j}\right).
By (
5
), we get

\chi\left(J_{k}\right)=\chi(K\cap z_{k})+\chi\left(J_{k-1}\right)-\chi\left((K\cap z_{k})\cap J_{k-1}\right).

By the induction hypothesis, we have that

\chi\left(J_{k-1}\right)=1,

as well as

\chi(K\cap z_{k}))=\chi(\{z_{k}\})+\chi(\partial(K\cap z_{k}))=(-1)^{R}+1+(-1)^{R-1}=1,

since
\partial(K\cap z_{k})
is a closed
(R-1)
-cell complex and the shellability of
K
implies in particular that the boundary of each of its facet is shellable.

For
1\leq k<N,
the shelling property and the induction hypothesis also implies

\chi\left((K\cap z_{k})\cap J_{k-1}\right)=1.

For
k=N,
we have the following two cases:

\displaystyle\chi\left((K\cap z_{N})\cap J_{N-1}\right)=\begin{cases}\chi\left(\partial(K\cap z_{N})\right)=1+(-1)^{R-1},&\text{if }K\text{ is closed,}\\
1,&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

This concludes the proof.
∎

We can for example consider the boundary of a
R
-simplex, which clearly defines a closed shellable simplicial complex
K
of rank
R-1
and Proposition
2.20
therefore implies that
\chi(K)=1+(-1)^{(R-1)}.
As a consequence, the Euler characteristic of a simplex (seen as a polytope) is
1.

As noted in the next corollary, the previous results imply that the Euler characteristic is invariant under taking the barycentric subdivision, provided that the complex we started with is shellable.

Corollary 2.21
.

If
K
is a shellable cell complex then
\chi(\widecheck{K})=\chi(K).

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Lemma
2.17
and Proposition
2.20
.
∎

In our context, cc are not shellable in general and their Euler characteristic can therefore take various values. For example, a 3-cc
T
could be defined so that
\partial T
is a discretization of a torus, seen as a surface of genus 1, and
\left|T^{[3]}\right|=1,
hence
\chi(T)=-1
(such an cc is given in Figure
17
as an example of cobordism in Chapter
3
). It is a standard result from Bruggesser and Mani (Theorem 8.11 in
[
77
]
) that convex polytopes are shellable. Therefore
T
also provides a case of cc that cannot be realized as the face lattice of an embedded convex polytope, although it would satisfy the conditions of (abstract) polytope defined in Definition
2.13
(the homogeneity of
\partial T
in particular implies that
\partial T
is local and non-pinching, which implies the connectedness of all sections in
T
). Similarly, a
R
-cc
K
having a facet
z\in K^{[R]}
such that
K\cap z=T
cannot be realized as the face lattice of a polyhedral complex. In such an example, the
3
-cell
z
would be a center of an non-homogeneous vertex in
\widecheck{K}

In the remainder of this section we introduce a definition of 2-shellability that applies to cc of rank higher or equal to 2 and prove that the 2-shellability of a simple cc is equivalent to the shellability of its dual. 2-shellability is characterized only using the cells of the 2-skeleton and is inspired by the notion of ”weak-shellability” given by Vince in
[
74
]
.

Definition 2.22

(2-shellable cell complex)
.

A cell complex
K
is
2-shellable
if there is a linear ordering on
K^{[0]}:=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\}
such that for all
C\in K^{[2]}
the graph

K_{j}\cap(C\setminus\{v_{j+1}\})

is connected, where
K_{j}:=K\cap\{v_{1},\dots,v_{j}\}
is a cell complex. Such linear ordering is called a
2-shelling
of
K.

Remark 2.23
.

We notice that Definition
2.22
implies that the 2-skeleton of a 2-shellable cell complex can be constructed by successively adding each 2-cell in such a way that at each step of the construction, the new 2-cell intersects the previous ones on a simple path. Indeed, using the notation of Definition
2.22
, a 2-shelling satisfies the following property. At step
j+1,
if
C\in K^{[2]}
contains
v_{j+1}
and
C\cap\{v_{1},\dots,v_{j}\}\neq\emptyset
then there is no gap between
v_{j+1}
and the previous vertices in
C,
in the sense that there exists
1\leq k\leq j
such that
C\supset\{v_{j+1},v_{k}\}\in K^{[1]}.
The reason for this is that if there were a gap and if we take the first
l>j+1
such that

v_{l}\in C\setminus\{v_{1},\dots,v_{j+1}\}

we have that
K_{l}\cap(C\setminus\{v_{l}\})
is disconnected, contradicting that
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\}
is a 2-shelling. We can therefore suppose, without loss of generality, that
l=j+2,
i.e. the shelling order continues by adding all the vertices in
C
first, before completing another 2-cell.

This following technical result will be used in the proof of the next Theorem
2.25
.

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
K
be a simple cc such that
\overline{K}
is shellable. Let
\{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{N}\}
be a shelling of
\overline{K}
and define
v_{i}:=\overline{\sigma_{i}},
for
i=1,\dots N.
We also set
K_{j}:=K\cap\{v_{1},\dots,v_{j}\}
and define the following simplicial complex:

\overline{K}_{j}:=\overline{K}\cap\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{j}\sigma_{i}\right).

Then we have that
K_{j}\cap\overline{\omega}
is connected for all
\omega\in\overline{K}_{j}
and all
1\leq j\leq N.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that there exists
1<j\leq N
such that
K_{j}\cap\overline{\omega}
is not connected for some
\omega\in\overline{K}_{j},
which implies
\operatorname{rk}(\overline{\omega})\geq 2.
Since
\overline{\omega}\in K=K_{N}
is connected, we can take
j
maximal with the property that
K_{j}\cap\overline{\omega}
is not connected, so that
K_{j+1}\cap\overline{\omega}
is connected. Hence there are at least two vertices
v_{k},v_{l}
in
\overline{\omega}\cap\mathcal{N}(v_{j+1})
that belong to two different connected components of
K_{j}\cap\overline{\omega}.
Since
K
is full, there is a 2-cell
C\in K^{[2]}
containing the edges
\{v_{j+1},v_{k}\}
and
\{v_{j+1},v_{l}\}
and thus
K_{j}\cap(C\setminus\{v_{j+1}\})
is not connected, which contradicts that
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\}
is a 2-shelling of
K.

∎

The following theorem is analogous to Theorem 7 given in
[
74
]
stated in terms of
n
-graphs. Our proof is novel as the definition of
n
-graph allows to use some equivalence relations between edges that do not exist in general for simple cell complexes. This result given in terms of simple cc is nevertheless not a generalization of Vince’s result since
n
-graphs are more general than our notion of graphs, as in particular they allow multiple edges to contain the same two vertices.

Theorem 2.25
.

A simple cell complex
K
of rank
R\geq 2
is 2-shellable if and only if
\overline{K}
is shellable.

Proof.

Let
K
be a simple cell complex such that
\overline{K}
is shellable with shelling
\{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{N}\}
and define

v_{i},

K_{j}
and
\overline{K}_{j}
as in Lemma
2.24
.

We show that
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\}
is a 2-shelling of
K.

For this, let us assume by contradiction that there is
C\in K^{[2]}
such that
K_{j}\cap(C\setminus\{v_{j+1}\})
is not connected. We consider
j
to be maximal with this property for
C
fixed (
j
is well defined since
K\cap C
is connected). The maximality of
j
implies that
K_{j+1}\cap C
is connected, therefore
v_{j+1}
has two neighbours in
K_{j}\cap C,
linked to
v_{j+1}
by the edges
e,e^{\prime}.
Since
\sigma_{j+1}
is a simplex, the two faces
\overline{e},\overline{e^{\prime}}
of
\sigma_{j+1}
share the
(R-2)
-cell
\overline{C}.
But
K_{j+1}\cap C\neq C
by definition of
j,
therefore
\overline{C}\notin\overline{K}_{j+1},
contradicting the intersecting property
ii)
of the
(R-1)
-cell complex

\overline{K}_{j+1}\cap\left(\sigma_{j+1}\cap\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{j}\sigma_{i}\right)\right).

Conversely, assume that
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{N}\}
is a 2-shelling of
K.
We show that
\{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{N}\}
is a shelling of
\overline{K}
where
\sigma_{i}=\overline{v_{i}}.

Since any subset of the faces
\triangle\left(\sigma\right)
of an
R
-simplex
\sigma\in\overline{K}
is a
(R-1)
-sub-cell complex that can be completed into a shelling of
\partial(\overline{K}\cap\sigma),
it is sufficient to show that for all
1<j\leq N,
if
\omega:=\sigma_{k}\cap\sigma_{j}\neq\emptyset
for some
1\leq k<j
then there exists
1\leq l<j
such that

w\subset\sigma_{l}\cap\sigma_{j}\in\triangle\left(\sigma_{j}\right).

The latter implies that
\overline{K}_{j-1}\cap\sigma_{j}
is indeed a
(R-1)
-cc that has a shelling which can be completed in to a shelling of
\partial(\overline{K}\cap\sigma_{J}).

By Lemma
2.24
, such
\omega
satisfies that
K_{j}\cap\overline{\omega}
is connected, therefore there is
1\leq k<j
such that
\sigma_{k}\in\overline{\omega}
and
\{\sigma_{k},\sigma_{j}\}\in K_{j}^{[1]},
i.e.
\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{K}}(\sigma_{k}\cap\sigma_{j})=R-1,
and this completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 2.26
.

If
K
is a local simple 2-shellable
R
-cell complex then
K
satisfies Euler-Poincaré formula

\chi(K)=1+(-1)^{R}.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.11
and Theorem
2.25
,
\overline{K}
is a shellable simplicial complex, which satisfies Euler-Poincaré formula by Proposition
2.20
. As noticed in Remark
2.19
and since
K
is in particular closed, we have

\chi(K)=(-1)^{R}\chi(\overline{K})=(-1)^{R}+1.

∎

2.4
Simply connected cc

In this section, we introduce a combinatorial notion of homotopy in cell complexes of rank higher or equal to 2 defined using
2
-cells and edges. Although the idea is simply to allow paths to be ”deformed through”
2
-cells, the exact formulation of these notion is somewhat technical. For example, in order to define such deformation on every paths, we have to deal with the case were a path goes several times through a given edge. This can create ambiguities on which part of the path is to be deformed through a given
2
-cell. Two avoid this, we added a condition in the definition of such ”path move” so that it acts on the part of the path which first goes through the
2
-cell that the move is based on. Similar questions arise when defining the move of paths through an edge
e,
which acts non-trivially on paths that contains a sub-path of the form
(e,e)
(using the notations from Section
1.3
), i.e. when a path goes through the edge
e
in both directions in a row.

Let
K
be a graph-based 2-cc and let
v,w\in K^{[0]}.
We denote by
\mathcal{P}
the set of paths in
K^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}(v,w)
the set of paths from
v
to
w
in
K^{(1)}.
For a 2-cell
C\in\overline{v}\cap\overline{w},
a simple path
p\in\mathcal{P}(v,w)
in
K^{(1)}\cap C
and
C_{p}
the connected component of
C
containing
p,
we define the
complementary path
{p}^{C}
of
p
in
C
to be the sub-path of
l_{C_{p}}(v)
from
v
to
w
such that

l_{C}(v)=p\ast({p}^{C})^{-1},

as in Figure
9
, where
l_{C_{p}}(v)
was introduced in Definition
1.13
.

Figure 9
:
On this illustration we have a simple path
p
of length 2 from the vertex
v
to the vertex
w
contained in a 2-cell
C.
The path
p
can be seen as as sub-path of a longer path going through other edges before
v
and after
w
(including the edges in
C
). Since we do not a priori assume cells to be connected, a
2
-cell can have more than one connected component,
C_{p}
is therefore the connected component of
C
containing
p.
By Lemma
1.12
we have that the edges in
C_{p}
form a loop
l_{C}(v)
that we can define as based at the vertex
v
and such that
p\leq l_{C}(v).
The complementary path
p^{C}
of
p
through
C_{p}
is then defined as the sub-path of
l_{C}(v)
going from
v
to
w
and disjoint from
p.

Definition 2.27

(Moves of a path)
.

Let
C\in K^{[2]}
and let
p
be a simple path in
K^{(1)}\cap C.
The
2-cell move of
p
through
C
is a function
m_{C}^{p}:\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
defined by

\displaystyle m_{C}^{p}(q)=\begin{cases}q_{1}\ast{p}^{C}\ast q_{2},&\text{if }q=q_{1}\ast p\ast q_{2}\text{ with }q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathcal{P},p\not\leq q_{1};\\
q&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We can see that
m_{C}^{p}
is a bijection with inverse
m_{C}^{{p}^{C}}.

Let
e\in K^{[1]}
and
v\in e.
The
edge-move through
e
based at
v
is a function
m_{e,v}:\mathcal{P}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}
defined by

\displaystyle m_{e,v}(q)=\begin{cases}q_{1}\ast q_{2},&\text{if }q=q_{1}\ast(e,e)\ast q_{2}\text{ with }q_{i}\in\mathcal{P},e\cap q_{i}^{[0]}={v},i=1,2,(e,e)\not\leq q_{1};\\
q&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In this case, the inverse of
m_{e,v}(q)
is the maps
m^{-1}_{e,v}
which adds
e
to a path
q
if
e\cap q^{[0]}=\{v\}
and leaves
q
invariant otherwise.
We denote by
\mathcal{M}_{1}
the set of edge-moves and their inverses and by
\mathcal{M}_{2}
the set of 2-cell-moves and their inverses and define the
set of moves
to be
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\cup\mathcal{M}_{2}.

Note that if
q
is a path from
v
to
w
then so is
m(q)
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}.
Therefore moves of paths can be seen as functions on
\mathcal{P}(v,w).

Definition 2.28

(Homotopy, homotopy class of paths and contractible cycle)
.

An
homotopy
is a map
H:\mathcal{P}(v,w)\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(v,w)
given by a finite composition of moves in
\mathcal{M}.
Two paths
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(v,w)
are
(homotopically) equivalent,
noted
p_{1}\sim p_{2},
if there exists an homotopy
H
such that
H(p_{1})=p_{2}.
The homotopy class of a path
p
is denoted by
[p].
A cycle
p
based at
v
is
contractible
if
[p]=[\emptyset_{v}].

Definition 2.29

(Simply connected cc)
.

A graph-based cc
K
is
simply connected
if every cycle in
K
is contractible in
K^{(2)}.

The next remarks on simple connectedness will be used in the discussion at the end of this chapter.

Remark 2.30
.

It seems clear that the notion of homotopy used here is equivalent to the usual notion of homotopy of paths on a manifold, in the sense that if
K
is a cell complex which geometrical realization is a discretization of a cellular manifold (implying that
K\cap x
is geometrically realized as a ball for all
x\in K
) then
K^{(2)}
is simply connected (in the sense of Definition
2.28
) if and only if
M
is simply connected in the usual topological sense. A sketch of an argument proving this can be formulated as follows, using that in this case
\overline{K}
also admits a geometrical realization discretizing
M
(this can be argued using the fact that
\widecheck{K}\cong\widecheck{\overline{K}}
by the Proposition
1.24
). A path
p
in
M
can be projected onto
K^{(1)}
by decomposing
p
into sub-paths such that there is one sub-path for each crossing of
p
from one maximal cell of
\overline{K}
to another. One can then continuously deform each sub-path as to be included into the edge of (the geometric realization of)
K
composed of the two corresponding maximal cells of
\overline{K}
and the combination of these deformations defines a usual homotopoy of paths on
M.
The resulting path can be interpreted as a path on the graph
K^{(1)}
and an homotopy as defined in these notes also corresponds to a usual homotopy on
M.

Remark 2.31
.

It is a well-known result (
[
27
]
) that shellable pseudomanifolds are homeomorphic to a sphere (using the notion of geometrical realization mentioned in Section
1.6
).
As a consequence of Theorem
2.25
, a 2-shellable simple local cell complex
K
satisfies that
\overline{K}
is simply connected.

2.5
Monodromy-free and even cc

We now turn to the notions of monodromy-freedom and evenness in a cc. The latter corresponds simply to the property of having an even number of vertices in each (connected component of each)
2
-cell. However we do not yet fully understand how evenness can be interpreted in the context of the reconstruction results of Section
2.6
, although we suspect that it has something to do with some notion of orientation. Monodromy-freedom essentially means that the connection does not produce a monodromy around any
2
-cell and it is the notion we focus on first.

In the case of full graph-based cc, the notation for the connection introduced in Section
2.1
can be extended to paths in the following way.

Definition 2.32

(
\nabla_{p}
)
.

For a full graph-based 2-cc
K,
we have that, for all
\{v,w\}\in K^{[1]},

\mathcal{E}_{v}^{\overline{w}}=\{e\in\mathcal{E}_{v}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \overline{e}\cap\overline{\{v,w\}}\neq\emptyset\}=\mathcal{E}_{v}.

Therefore if
p=(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})
is a path in
K^{(1)},
the map

\nabla_{p}:=\nabla_{v_{k}}^{v_{k-1}}\dots\nabla_{v_{1}}^{v_{0}}

is a bijection from
\mathcal{E}_{v_{0}}
to
\mathcal{E}_{v_{k}},
where
e_{i}=\{v_{i-1},v_{i}\}
for
1\leq i\leq k.
Also,
\nabla_{p}^{-1}=\nabla_{p^{-1}},

\nabla_{p\ast p^{\prime}}=\nabla_{p^{\prime}}\nabla_{p}
and
\nabla_{\emptyset_{v}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{E}_{v}}.

We say that
\nabla_{p}e\in\mathcal{E}_{v_{k}}
is
associated to

e\in\mathcal{E}_{v_{0}}
by
p.

If
K
is a full graph-based cc and
p
is a path in
K^{(1)}
then the connection
\nabla_{p}
in
K
is defined as the corresponding connection in
K^{(2)}.

Let
C
be a connected component of a 2-cell of a full graph-based cc
K.
Recall that

\mathcal{E}_{C}:=\{e\in K^{[1]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \left|e\cap C\right|=1\}.

For
\{v,w\}\subset C,

\nabla_{w}^{v}
maps
\mathcal{E}_{v}\cap\mathcal{E}_{C}
bijectively onto
\mathcal{E}_{w}\cap\mathcal{E}_{C},
since if
e\not\subset C
then
\nabla_{w}^{v}e\not\subset C
by Lemma
2.1

2)
. Therefore
\nabla_{l_{C}(v)}
restricted to
\mathcal{E}_{v}\cap\mathcal{E}_{C}
is a bijection from
\mathcal{E}_{v}\cap\mathcal{E}_{C}
onto itself.

We can now introduce our definition of monodromy-free cell, illustrated in Figure
10
and define the notion of monodromy-free cc.

Definition 2.33

(Monodromy-free cc)
.

A connected component
C
of a 2-cell in a full graph-based cc
K
is said to be
monodromy-free
if
\nabla_{l_{C}(v)}
restricted to
\mathcal{E}_{v}\cap\mathcal{E}_{C}
is the identity map on
\mathcal{E}_{v}\cap\mathcal{E}_{C}
for some
v\in C
(and hence for all
v\in C
). We say that a full graph-based cc
K
is
monodromy-free
if every connected component of every 2-cell in
K^{[2]}
is monodromy-free.

Figure 10
:
In this figure we see an example of a monodromy free (connected component of a) 2-cell
C,
seen as a cell in a 3 dimensional cc. We also pictured the loop
l_{C}(v)
based at the vertex
v
and how the connexion
\nabla_{l_{C}(v)}
maps the edges of
\mathcal{E}_{C}
around
C.
In this case, since there are only two edges in
\mathcal{E}_{v^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{C}
for all
v^{\prime}\in C,
it is sufficient to check that say
e
as on the figure is mapped to itself by
\nabla_{l_{C}(v)}
to deduce that
C
is monodromy-free.

The following Lemma
2.34
shows that the monodromy-freedom condition naturally arises when considering
R
-full cell complexes for
R\geq 3.
This fact motivates our definition of monodromy-free cell complexes, as it is a key assumption when considering the reconstruction of simple cell complexes from their 2-skeleton in Section
2.6
. We recently found that a similar notion was introduced in the last chapter of
[
38
]
, where several conditions are discussed in order to establish whether some space defined as ”simplicial glueings” in dimensions 3 and 4 define a manifold. A simplicial glueing is simply a set of simplices of a given dimension where faces are identified according to some face-pairing. One of these conditions, called ”
\mathsf{Cycl}
” is stated in dimension 4 and gives a condition on each triangle (i.e. 2-simplices) that is precisely the condition of monodromy-freedom as defined here. It is suggested that one can easily devise an extension to all dimensions and we believe that the definition of monodromy-free cell complex indeed corresponds to the extensions of the condition
\mathsf{Cycl}
to dimensions higher or equal to 3.

Lemma 2.34
.

A 3-full graph-based cell complex is monodromy-free.

Proof.

Let
[v_{1}\dots v_{N}]
be a representation of the connected component of a 2-cell
C\in K^{[2]}
and let
l:v_{1}\rightsquigarrow v_{1}
be the loop associated with this representation based at
v_{1}
and starting with the edge
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}.
By the diamond property
iv)
, if
e\in\mathcal{E}_{v_{1}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{C},
there are 2 edges
e_{0},e_{1}\in\mathcal{E}_{v}
included in
C
and since
K
is 3-full, there is a 3-cell
B
containing
e_{0},e_{1}
and
e.
Since
K
is full, there is a unique 2-cell
C_{e}
containing
e
and
e_{1}
and
by Axiom
ii)
since
e,e_{1}\subset B\cap C_{e}

we have
C_{e}\subset B.
Therefore
\nabla_{v_{2}}^{v_{1}}e\subset B.
By the same reasoning, there is a unique edge
e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{v_{2}}\setminus\{e_{1}\}
included in
C
and there is a unique 2-cell
C_{e}^{\prime}
containing
\nabla_{v_{2}}^{v_{1}}e
and
e_{2}
and contained in
B,
which implies
\nabla_{v_{3}}^{v_{2}}e\in B.
By repeating this argument
N
times we get
\nabla_{l}e\in B
and this implies that
\nabla_{l}e=e,
by Lemma
2.8
, since
K
is 3-full.
∎

The notion of evenness of a
2
-cell and the corresponding notion for cc, introduced in the following definition, will also be crucial for the reconstruction in Section
2.6
. The idea is that the assumption of evenness leads to the invariance of the connection
\nabla_{p}
under homotopies, as shown in Lemma
2.38
. One can better picture the effect of even
2
-cell versus odd (non-even)
2
-cell on the connection using Figure
11
.

Definition 2.35

(Even
2
-cell and even cc)
.

A connected component of a 2-cell is said to be
even
if it contains an even number of vertices. A cc
K
is said to be
even
if every connected component of every 2-cell in
K^{[2]}
is even.

Remark 2.36
.

If
C
is even and monodromy-free then for all
v\in C
we have

\nabla_{l_{C}(v)}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{E}_{v}},

since
\nabla_{l_{C}(v)}
induces a pairing of the edges contained in
C.

Figure 11
:
In these pictures, we see how the connection acts on the edges within an even 2-cell (on the left) versus within an odd 2-cell (on the right). The even case then leads to a pairing of the edges induced by the connection.

An important example of even cc is given in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.37
.

The dual of the barycentric subdivision of a closed cc is an even cc.

Proof.

Let
J
be a closed
R
-cc and let
K=\overline{\widecheck{J}}.
A 2-cell
C\in K^{[2]}
is then of the form
C=\overline{y}
where

y=\{x_{1}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{R-1}\}\in\widecheck{J}^{[R-2]}.

We can then see that elements in
\overline{y}
come in pairs by the following observation. Since
\widecheck{J}
is a simplicial complex, every
z\in\overline{y}\subset\widecheck{J}^{[R]}
satisfies
\left|z\setminus y\right|=2.
Since
z
defines a totally ordered subset of
J,
the minimum rank of an element in
z\setminus y=:\{x,x^{\prime}\},
say
\operatorname{rk}(x),
is strictly less than
R.
This means that
z=y\cup\{x\subsetneq x^{\prime}\}
and by the diamond property
iv)
(and since
J
is graph-based for the case
\operatorname{rk}(x)=0
), there exists a unique
x^{\prime\prime}
such that
\{y\cup\{x\subsetneq x^{\prime\prime}\},z\}\subset\overline{y}.

∎

The following lemma states under what assumptions the connection
\nabla_{p}
is invariant under homotopies, which is a key property in order to prove the Extension Lemma
2.40
in the next section.

Lemma 2.38
.

Let
K
be a full, graph-based, monodromy-free, even cc and let
q\in\mathcal{P}
and
m\in\mathcal{M}.
Then we have

\nabla_{q}=\nabla_{m(q)}.

In particular if
p\sim q
then
\nabla_{p}=\nabla_{q}.

Proof.

If
m(q)=q
it is trivial. If not then either
m=m_{C}^{p}
for some 2-cell
C
and a simple path
p
in
K^{(1)}\cap C
and
q=q_{1}\ast p\ast q_{2},
or
m=m_{e,v}
(or
m=m_{e,v}^{-1}
) for some edge
e
and vertex
v
such that
v\in e\cap q^{[0]}
and
q=q_{1}\ast(e,e)\ast q_{2}.

In the first case
\nabla_{l_{C}(v)}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{E}_{v}}
for all
v\in C,
by Remark
2.36
, therefore we can choose a representation of
C
and some
v\in C
such that
l_{C}(v)=({p}^{C})^{-1}\ast p.
One then obtains the following relations

\displaystyle\nabla_{m(q)}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{q_{1}}\nabla_{{p}^{C}}\nabla_{q_{2}}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{q_{1}}\nabla_{l_{C}(v)}\nabla_{{p}^{C}}\nabla_{q_{2}}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{q_{1}}\nabla_{p}\nabla_{{p}^{C}}^{-1}\nabla_{{p}^{C}}\nabla_{q_{2}}=\nabla_{q}.

In the second case, we have that
\nabla_{(e,e)}=\nabla_{v}^{w}\nabla_{w}^{v}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{E}_{v}}
assuming
e=\{v,w\}
and
(e,e)
in
q=q_{1}\ast(e,e)\ast q_{2}
is a path from
v
to
v,
so that we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{m(q)}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{q_{1}}\nabla_{q_{2}}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{q_{1}}\nabla_{(e,e)}\nabla_{q_{2}}=\nabla_{q}.

The case
m=m_{e,v}^{-1}
is similar to the second case.
∎

2.6
Extension Lemma and reconstruction

In this section, we prove that local even full monodromy-free simply connected 2-cell complexes can be associated a corresponding ”ambient” cell complex of rank
R\geq 3
having the original 2-cell complex as its 2-skeleton. The main step for this construction, carried out in Theorem
2.42
, is to define a collection of ”induced” sub-2-cell complexes whose vertices then serve as higher dimensional cells of the ambient cell complex. In particular, Corollary
2.46
shows that the ambient cell complex associated to the 2-skeleton of a local even simple simply connected cell complex is equal to the original cell complex. This result is in line with a number of results about the reconstruction of the face lattice of (embedded) polytopes, see e.g.
[
22
]
for a recent review on this topic. A well-known result from
[
28
]
and
[
51
]
states that the face lattice of a simple polytope, i.e. a polytope which dual is simplicial, is determined by its 1-skeleton. As we discussed in Section
2.3
, here we are dealing with local cell complexes that are more general than the face lattice of complexes of polytopes but we have the additional assumptions of simple connectedness and evenness. We are not aware of an equivalent result in the literature for complex of polytopes.

The first key result of this section is the next ”Extension Lemma”
2.40
which will be essential for the proof of Theorem
2.42
. The assumptions of fullness, evenness, monodromy-freedom and simple connectedness introduced in this chapter can therefore be considered as chosen so that this lemma holds. The proof of the Extension Lemma is efficiently forumulated using covariant edge fields. One reason for this is that when defined on an appropriate domain, covariant edge fields satisfy the property that their value over their entire domain is determined by their value at one vertex. We formulate this fact more precisely in the following remark on which the proof of the Extension Lemma relies.

Remark 2.39
.

If
\phi
is a covariant edge field in a full graph-based cc
K
and if
p
is a path in
K^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{D}_{\phi},
from
v
to
w
then
\nabla_{p}\phi(v)=\phi(w).
In particular if
K^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{D}_{\phi}
is connected then
\phi
is determined by its value at any given vertex in
\mathcal{D}_{\phi}.

We now formulate the Extension Lemma and its proof in terms of growing domains of covariant edge fields as it incorporates well the idea that will be used in our formulation of the proof of Theorem
2.42
. Considering that we assume the entire cc
K
to be simply connected, one can also read the result simply using
\mathcal{D}_{\phi}=\{v\}
for any
v\in K^{[0]}
and
D=K^{[0]}.

Lemma 2.40

(Extension Lemma)
.

Let
K
be a graph-based, full, even, monodromy-free, simply connected cc and let
\phi:\mathcal{D}_{\phi}\longrightarrow K^{[1]}
be an covariant edge field in
K
such that
K^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{D}_{\phi}
is connected. If
D\subset K^{[0]}
such that
K^{(1)}\cap(\mathcal{D}_{\phi}\cup D)
is connected then there exists a unique covariant edge field
\hat{\phi}:\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\phi}}\longrightarrow K^{[1]}
such that
\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\phi}}=\mathcal{D}_{\phi}\cup D
and
\hat{\phi}|_{\mathcal{D}_{\phi}}=\phi.

Proof.

Let
v\in D,

w\in\mathcal{D}_{\phi}
and let
p
be a path from
w
to
v
in
K^{(1)}\cap(\mathcal{D}_{\phi}\cup D),
as in Figure
12
. The main idea of the proof is to define
\hat{\phi}(v)=\nabla_{p}\phi(w)
and to show that it is well defined. This amounts to show that if
w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\phi}
and
p^{\prime}
is a path from
w^{\prime}
to
v
then there is a path
q
from
w
to
w^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}_{\phi}
and we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{p}\phi(w)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{p^{-1}\ast q\ast p^{\prime}}\nabla_{p}\phi(w)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{p^{\prime}}\nabla_{q}\phi(w)=\nabla_{p^{\prime}}\phi(w^{\prime}).

This is indeed the case by the following argument. Since
K
is simply connected and even, Lemma
2.38
implies that every cycle
q^{\prime}
in
K
satisfies
\nabla_{q^{\prime}}=\nabla_{\emptyset_{v}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{E}_{v}}
for any
v\in(q^{\prime})^{[0]}.
The first equality then follows from the fact that
p^{-1}\ast p\ast p^{\prime}
is a cycle. For the second equality, we recall that
\nabla_{p_{1}\ast p_{2}}=\nabla_{p_{2}}\nabla_{p_{1}}
and the last equality is a consequence of
\nabla_{q}\phi(w)=\phi(w^{\prime})
as noticed in Remark
2.39
. The latter remark also gives an argument for the uniqueness of
\hat{\phi}.
Finally
\hat{\phi}
defined thereby is a covariant edge field since if
\{v,v^{\prime}\}
is an edge of
K^{(1)}\cap D
and
p
is a path linking
v
with
w\in\mathcal{D}_{\phi}
then
p^{\prime}=p\ast(\{v,v^{\prime}\})
is a path linking
v^{\prime}
with
w
therefore

\hat{\phi}(v^{\prime})=\nabla_{p^{\prime}}\phi(w)=\nabla_{v^{\prime}}^{v}\nabla_{p}\phi(w)=\nabla_{v^{\prime}}^{v}\hat{\phi}(v).

∎

Figure 12
:
Configuration of Lemma
2.40
.

Remark 2.41
.

As a consequence of Lemma
2.40
, a full, even, monodromy-free, simply connected and connected cc
K
is
properly
(R+1)
-edge-colorable,
meaning that one can color each edge of
K
with
R+1
colors so that no two edges sharing a vertex have the same color. Another way to say this is that
K^{(1)}
is an
(R+1)
-graph, where the notion of
n
-graph is a tool used in works on so-called crystallizations of topological manifolds (see e.g. in
[
54
]
,
[
74
]
or
[
6
]
), introduced in the research related to minimal atlases of topological manifold initiated by Pezzana and al. (see
[
42
]
for a review on this topic). However, not all
n
-regular graphs are
n
-graph, i.e. properly
n
-edge-colorable.

Now comes the main result of this chapter, in which we define the notion of
induced sub-cell complex.

Theorem 2.42
.

Let
K
be a local even full monodromy-free simply connected 2-cell complex. Then for all
v\in K^{[0]}
and all
S_{v}\subsetneq\mathcal{E}_{v}
such that
r:=\left|S_{v}\right|\geq 2
there is a unique sub-cell complex
J(v,S_{v})
of
K
such that
J(v,S_{v})
is a
r
-regular local even full monodromy-free connected 2-cell complex and

J(v,S_{v})^{[1]}\cap\mathcal{E}_{v}=S_{v}.

(6)

Moreover,
J(v,S_{v})
satisfies the following properties:

1
)

J(v,S_{v})=J(v^{\prime},S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})
if and only if
v^{\prime}\in J(v,S_{v})^{[0]}
and
S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}=J(v,S_{v})^{[1]}\cap\mathcal{E}_{v^{\prime}},

2
)

S_{v}\subsetneq S^{\prime}_{v}
if and only if
J(v,S_{v})\lneq J(v,S^{\prime}_{v}).

3
)

If
\left|S_{v}\right|=2
then
S_{v}=\mathcal{E}_{v}^{C}
for some
C\in K^{[2]}
and
J(v,S_{v})=K\cap C.

We call such
J(v,S_{v})
an induced sub-cell complex of
K.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.7
,
K
is
n
-regular and
(n-1)
-edge-regular for some
n\geq 2.

Let
v\in K^{[0]}
and
S_{v}\subset\mathcal{E}_{v}.
By the Extension Lemma
2.40
, for any given
e_{v}\in S_{v}
and for all
w\in K^{[0]}
there is an edge
e_{w}
in
\mathcal{E}_{w}
associated to
e_{v},
meaning that the map
w\mapsto e_{w}
is an induced edge field. Hence for all
w\in K^{[0]}
we can define the set
S_{w}\subset\mathcal{E}_{w}
of edges in
\mathcal{E}_{w}
associated to the edges in
S_{v}.
Moreover, for each
w\in K^{[0]},
we can define the following set of 2-cells

\mathcal{C}_{w}:=\{C\in\overline{w}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{E}_{w}^{C}\subset S_{w}\},

(7)

where we used again the notation
\mathcal{E}_{w}^{C}:=\{e\in\mathcal{E}_{w}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\subset C\}
and
\overline{w}
is the dual set of
w
in the 2-cc
K.

The strategy of the proof is to inductively define a finite sequence of connected domains
\mathcal{D}_{k}\subset K^{[0]},\penalty\ 1\leq k\leq N
such that for all
1\leq k\leq N
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{k}_{w}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{E}_{w}\cap\left(K\cap\mathcal{D}_{k}\right)^{[1]}\subset S_{w},

(8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{w}^{k}

\displaystyle:=\{C\in\overline{w}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{E}_{w}^{C}\subset\mathcal{E}_{w}^{k}\}\subset\mathcal{C}_{w},

(9)

for all
w\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
and such that
\mathcal{D}_{N}=J(v,S_{v})^{[0]}.
Since the inclusion in (
8
) implies the inclusion on line (
9
), we only need to check that the first inclusion holds.

Define
\mathcal{D}_{1}=\{v\},
let
k\geq 1
and suppose that we defined the domains
\mathcal{D}_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq k
such that (
8
) is satisfied for all
w\in\mathcal{D}_{j}
and for all
1\leq j\leq k.
Let us fix
w_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
and a 2-cell

C_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{w}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{w}^{k}.

We then define
\mathcal{D}_{k+1}:=\mathcal{D}_{k}\cup C_{0},
which implies that for all
w\in\mathcal{D}_{k+1},

\mathcal{E}_{w}^{k+1}=\mathcal{E}_{w}^{k}\cup\mathcal{E}_{w}^{C_{0}},

where
\mathcal{E}_{w}^{C_{0}}=\emptyset
if
w\notin C_{0}.
The definition of the sets
S_{w},\penalty\ w\in K^{[0]}
(relying on the induced edge field
w\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{w}
) and the fact that
C_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{w_{0}}
imply that
C_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{w}
for all
w\in C_{0}.
We then have that
\mathcal{E}_{w}^{C_{0}}\subset S_{w}
for all
w\in C_{0}
and therefore (
8
) is satisfied for all
w\in\mathcal{D}_{k+1}.

We can then define
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
to be the first integer such that

\mathcal{C}_{w}^{N}=\mathcal{C}_{w}\quad\forall w\in\mathcal{D}_{N}.

(10)

An consquence of (
10
) we also get

\mathcal{E}_{w}^{N}=S_{w}\quad\forall w\in\mathcal{D}_{N},

(11)

by the following argument. Since
\left|S_{w}\right|\geq 2
for all
w\in K^{[0]},
if
e\in S_{w}
then there exists
e^{\prime}\in S_{w}\setminus\{e\}.
Since
K
is full, we can also pick
C\in\overline{e}\cap\overline{e^{\prime}}.
Hence the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{w}
implies that
C\in\mathcal{C}_{w}
and (
10
) in turn implies that
C\in\mathcal{C}_{w}^{N}.
The definition of
\mathcal{C}_{w}^{k}
in (
9
) for
k=N
yields
\mathcal{E}_{w}^{C}\subset\mathcal{E}_{w}^{N}
which in particular implies that
e\in\mathcal{E}_{w}^{N}
since
\mathcal{E}_{w}^{C}=\{e,e^{\prime}\}.

We therefore have that
J=J(v,S_{v}):=(J^{[0]},J^{[1]},J^{[2]})
defined by

J^{[0]}:=\mathcal{D}_{N},\quad J^{[1]}:=\bigcup_{w\in J^{[0]}}S_{w},\quad J^{[2]}:=\bigcup_{w\in J^{[0]}}\mathcal{C}_{w},

is a sub-2-cell complex of
K.
Axiom
i)
is clearly satisfied for
J,
as well as Axioms
ii)
,
iii)
and
iv)
since they are satisfied for
K
and if
C\in J^{[2]}
then the cc
K\cap C\subset J.
The cc
J
is clearly connected, cell-connected, even, full and monodromy-free. The property (
11
) then implies that

J^{[1]}\cap\mathcal{E}_{w}=S_{w}\quad\forall w\in J^{[0]}.

(12)

Let’s first note that point
3)
is clear as it corresponds to the case where
N=2
in the above construction.

We can now argue that property
1)
is satisfied. Fix
(v,S_{v})
and
J=J(v,S_{v})
as defined above. Assume that we pick

v^{\prime}\in K^{[0]}
and
S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{E}_{v^{\prime}}
and let
J^{\prime}=J(v^{\prime},S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})
be defined by the construction above, using
(v^{\prime},S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})
instead of
(v,S_{v}).
It is clear that if
J=J^{\prime}
then
v^{\prime}\in J^{[0]}
and
S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}=J^{[1]}\cap\mathcal{E}_{v^{\prime}}
by property (
12
) applied to
J^{\prime}=J.
Therefore it suffices to show to converse statement. For this we assume that
v^{\prime}\in J^{[0]}
and
S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}=J^{[1]}\cap\mathcal{E}_{v^{\prime}}
and we show that
J^{\prime}=J(v^{\prime},S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})=J.
First we notice that
S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}=S_{v^{\prime}},
the set of edges in
\mathcal{E}_{v^{\prime}}
associated to
S_{v}.
Therefore, for all
w\in K^{[0]},
the set of edges in
\mathcal{E}_{w}
associated to
S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
is identical to
S_{w},
the set of edges in
\mathcal{E}_{w}
associated to
S_{v}.
The corresponding sets of 2-cells also remain unchanged, i.e.

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{w}=\{C\in\overline{w}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathcal{E}^{C}_{w}\subset S^{\prime}_{w}\}=\mathcal{C}_{w},\quad\forall w\in K^{[0]}.

Let’s define an equivalence relation on
K^{[0]}
from the set of edges in
K^{[1]}
associated to a given set
S\subset\mathcal{E}_{v}
as follows. Two vertices
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
are called ”
S
-equivalent” if they can be linked by a sequence of 2-cells in the following manner: there are sequences
\{C_{i}\}_{i=1}^{M}\subset K^{[2]}
and
\{w_{i}\}_{i=0}^{M}\subset K^{[0]}
such that
v_{0}:=w_{0}\in C_{1},

v_{1}=w_{M}
and

C_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{w_{i}}\cap\mathcal{C}_{w_{i-1}}\quad\forall i=1,\dots,M,

where
\mathcal{C}_{w}
for all
w\in K^{[0]}
is defined by the formula (
7
) choosing
S_{w}
to be the set of edges in
\mathcal{E}_{w}
associated to
S.

We then have that
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
are vertices of
J
if and only
v_{0}\in J
and
v_{1}
is
S_{v}
-equivalent to
v_{0}.

By our assumptions,
v
and
v^{\prime}
are
S_{v}
-equivalent and, as we noticed before,
S^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
-equivalence classes are identical to
S_{v}
-equivalence classes and this indeed implies that
J^{\prime}=J.

Finally, the implication that
S_{v}\subsetneq S^{\prime}_{v}
if
J(v,S_{v})\lneq J(v,S^{\prime}_{v})
of point
2)
is clear and the reversed implication is a consequence of the following observation: the inclusion
S_{v}\subsetneq S^{\prime}_{v}
implies that for each vertex
w\in K^{[0]}
the set
S_{w}
of edges associated to
S_{v}
is also strictly included in the set
S^{\prime}_{w}
of edges associated to
S^{\prime}_{v}.
Similarly, the corresponding sets of 2-cells satisfy
\mathcal{C}_{w}\subsetneq\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{w}
and this implies that
J(v,S_{v})^{[i]}\subsetneq J(v,S^{\prime}_{v})^{[i]}
for
i=0,1,2.

∎

Our formulation of the proof of Theorem
2.42
directly provides an algorithm to construct the induced sub-cell complexes associated to a local even full monodromy-free simply connected 2-cell complex. Using the same notations as in the proof, the algorithm simply consists in the following steps, starting with all couples of the form
(v,S_{v})
marked as ”unselected” and running until all such couples have been selected:

•

select one unselected couple
(v,S_{v}),

•

construct
J(v,S_{v})
by iteratively adding 2-cells to a domain
\mathcal{D}_{k}
as in the proof,

•

at each step, mark each couple
(w,S_{w})
as ”selected” where
w\in\mathcal{D}_{k+1}
and
S_{w}
are the edge in
\mathcal{E}_{w}
associated to
S_{v}.

We introduce the following notion in order to simplify the formulation of the next proofs. The idea is simply to connect the induced cell complex defined in Theorem
2.42
to the Definition
1.1
of a cell complex, where a cell is a subset of vertices.

Definition 2.43

(Collection of induced
r
-cells)
.

Let
K
be a local even full monodromy-free simply connected 2-cell complex.
K
is
n
-regular for some
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
by Lemma
2.7
) and let us also fix an integer
2\leq r\leq n-1.
We denote by
\mathcal{C}^{K}_{r}
the collection of vertex sets of all
r
-regular induced sub-cell complexes of
K
given by Theorem
2.42
.
\mathcal{C}^{K}_{r}
is called the
set of induced
r
-cells of
K.
By convention we set
\mathcal{C}_{r}^{K}=\emptyset
for all
r>n.

Note that the point
3)
of Theorem
2.42
implies in particular that
\mathcal{C}_{2}^{K}=K^{[2]}.
We also need the following condition on the induced cells of a local even full monodromy-free simply connected 2-cell complex in order to define an higher dimensional cell complex from these cells.

Definition 2.44

(Non-singular collection of cells)
.

Let
K
be a local cell complex and let
\mathcal{C}
be a collection of subsets of
K^{[0]}.

\mathcal{C}
is
non-singular
if
K\cap(x\cap y)
is connected or empty for all
x,y\in\mathcal{C}.

We are now ready to formulate the following two corollaries of Theorem
2.42
. The Corollary
2.45
gives the sufficient conditions on a
2
-cc
K
for the existence of an
ambient cell complex
K_{amb}
such that the
2
-skeleton of
K_{amb}
is
K.
Corollary
2.46
gives the uniqueness of
K_{amb}
and can be interpreted as reconstructing a local even simply connected simple cc of rank higher or equal to 3 from the data of its 2-skeleton.

Corollary 2.45
.

Let
K
be a local even full monodromy-free simply connected 2-cell complex such that
\mathcal{C}^{K}_{r}
is non-singular for all integer
2\leq r\leq R,
where
R
is the largest integer
r
such that
\mathcal{C}_{r}^{K}\neq\emptyset.
Then there exists a local even simply connected simple
R
-cc
K_{amb}
such that

(K_{amb})^{(2)}=K.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.7
,
K
is
n
-regular for some
n\geq 2.
Define
(K_{amb},\operatorname{rk}_{K_{amb}})
by
K_{amb}^{[r]}=K^{[r]}
for
0\leq r\leq 2
and
K_{amb}^{[r]}=\mathcal{C}^{K}_{r}
for
r\geq 3.
By Theorem
2.42
,
\operatorname{Rk}(K_{amb})=\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}\right|-1=n-1,
where
v\in K^{[0]}.

The Axiom
i)
is satisfied for
K_{amb}
by point
2)
of Theorem
2.42
. In order to show Axiom
ii)
, consider
x,y\in K_{amb},
where
x\supsetneq x\cap y\subsetneq y
and, say,
\operatorname{rk}(x)\leq\operatorname{rk}(y).

We first consider the case where
x\cap y
contains no 2-cell. Since
\mathcal{C}^{K}_{r}
is non-singular for all
1\leq r\leq n-1
then
x\cap y
is either a point or contains a unique edge by the following arguments. If there are two or more edges in
x\cap y
then
\operatorname{rk}(y)\geq 3
(as otherwise it would contradict
ii)
for
K
) and the non-singularity of induced cells implies in particular that
x\cap y
is connected, therefore there exist at least two edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
in
x\cap y
incident to the same vertex
v.
Since
K
is full and by definition of the induced cc
J(v,\{e,e^{\prime}\}),
the induced cell
y
contains the 2-cell
C
such that
\mathcal{E}_{v}^{C}=\{e,e^{\prime}\}.
If
\operatorname{rk}(x)\geq 3
then we also have
C\subset x
which contradicts our assumption and if
x\in K^{[2]}
then there are two edges in
x\cap C
and this contradicts Axiom
ii)
for
K.

We then consider the case were there exists
C\in\left(K\cap(x\cap y)\right)^{[2]}
and let
v\in C.

By Theorem
2.42
there is an induced cell
w
such that
\mathcal{E}^{C}_{v}\subset\mathcal{E}_{v}^{w}=\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x\cap y}=\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x}\cap\mathcal{E}_{v}^{y}.
Suppose by contradiction that
w\subsetneq x\cap y.
By the non-singularity of induced cells
x\cap y
is connected and this implies that there is a path
p:v\rightsquigarrow v^{\prime}
in
x\cap y
to a vertex
v^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{v^{\prime}}^{w}\subsetneq\mathcal{E}_{v^{\prime}}^{x\cap y}.
This is a contradiction since
\left|\mathcal{E}_{v}^{x^{\prime}}\right|=\left|\mathcal{E}_{v^{\prime}}^{x^{\prime}}\right|
for all
x^{\prime}\in K
by the bijectivity of the connection
\nabla_{p}:\mathcal{E}_{v}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{v^{\prime}}.

Therefore
w=x\cap y
and
ii)
is satisfied. Properties
iii)
and
iv)
are direct consequences of the point
2)
from Theorem
2.42
.
K_{amb}
is local and full since so is
K.

By Lemma
2.10
,
K_{amb}
is simple since it is
n
-regular with
n=\operatorname{Rk}(K_{amb})+1.

K_{amb}
is clearly even, cell-connected and simply connected.
∎

Corollary 2.46
.

K_{amb}
given by Corollary
2.45
is unique, i.e. if
L
is a local even simply connected simple
R
-cell complex such that
R\geq 3
then

(L^{(2)})_{amb}=L.

Proof.

Let
K=L^{(2)}.
By Lemma
2.34

K
is monodromy-free. By Lemma
2.8
the induced cells of
K
are exactly the cells of
L
of rank greater or equal to
3.

∎

Remark 2.47
.

By Theorem
1.28
on the determination of local cell complexes by their barycentric subdivision and Lemma
2.37
, a consequence of Corollary
2.45
is that local simply connected cell complexes are determined by the 2-skeleton of the dual of their barycentric subdivision.

Physicists are sometimes looking at discrete spaces as ”Feynman diagrams” of some generalized matrix models called group field theories (see e.g.
[
45
]
for a review about this topic,
[
38
]
for a discussion in dimension 4 and
[
37
]
with a definition of 2-cell complexes similar to ours). Here, we would like to point out that, as a consequence of the results of these first two chapters, the set of 2-shellable even local full 2-cell complexes appears to be an interesting class of cell complexes to consider in this context. By Remark
2.23
we can see this class of 2-cc as being constructed by successively adding 2-cells while respecting some natural connectedness assumptions that involves only local conditions. On the other hand Corollary
2.45
allows to uniquely associate a higher dimensional ”ambient” cell structure to such 2-cell complexes, which also satisfy the axioms of a cc if they fulfil the condition of non-singularity from Definition
2.44
. If this is the case, Theorem
2.25
and Remark
2.31
imply that the dual of the resulting ambient cell complex is a shellable simplicial complex which is then a triangulation of an Euclidean ball or sphere.

3  Duality map for cobordisms

The goal of this chapter if to introduce the definition of a duality map that applies to non-singular cell complexes with arbitrary boundary and which is equivalent to the Definition
1.15
for closed cell complexes. Since we are mainly interested in the case of local cell complexes and, as noticed in Remark
1.22
, the dual of a closed local cc (lcc) is local only if the original lcc is also non-pinching, we will restrict our treatment to the set, noted
\mathsf{C},
of non-singular non-pinching lcc. We also denote by
\mathsf{C}^{R}
the elements of rank
R
in
\mathsf{C}.
Boundaries of elements in
\mathsf{C}
will also play an important role in what follows and, by Lemma
1.21
, these consist of closed but not necessarily connected cell complexes. We therefore also introduce the notations
\mathsf{B}
for the set of closed non-pinching cell-connected cc and
\mathsf{B}^{R}
for the set of elements in
\mathsf{B}
of rank
R.
Since
\mathsf{C}
also includes the case of closed cell complexes, we assume by convention that the
empty cell complex
\{\emptyset\}
is included in
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{B}^{R}
for all
R\geq 0.

As we mentioned in the introduction, our goal is to mimic the behaviour of a Fourier-like transformation in certain physical models that provides a duality between two equivalent formulations of the same physical data. In this case, one has in particular that the double dual of a model is equivalent to the original model, an involution property we obtained for the cases of closed cc that one would like to preserve when the duality map acts on cell complexes in
\mathsf{C},
or at least for a certain sub-class thereof. As we will explain here, this idea is what lead us to consider the concept of cobordism, a geometrical space seen as interpolating between its boundary components, originally defined in the context of differentiable manifolds by Thom in
[
72
]
and defining a category used in the formulation of the axioms of TQFT by Atiyah in
[
10
]
. The parallel with TQFT motivated us to look for a suitable category of cobordisms defined from our combinatorial notion of cell complexes. This is the aim of the next Chapter
4
, where we define a composition operation for cobordisms leading us to the introduction of the category of causal cobordisms.

In order to obtain an involutive duality map on a certain sub-class of cc in
\mathsf{C}
, we have to focus on how this duality acts on the cells of the boundary. This property is indeed already satisfied for the other cells, as a consequence of Lemma
1.16
. For this purpose, we introduce a different type of dual set, noted
\widetilde{A}
for
A\subset K^{[0]}.
If
K\in\mathsf{C}^{R}
and
y\in(\partial K)^{[R-1]}
is a maximal cell in the boundary, it must be in particular that the dual of
y
is an edge of the dual of
K,
as it becomes therefore again a sub-maximal cell of the double dual of
K.
Since
\widetilde{y}
therefore has to contain at least two elements, it is natural to define

\widetilde{y}=\{y,z\},

where
z
is the unique element of
K^{[R]}
containing
y.
In fact, the ”generalized dual” of a cell
x\in\partial K
will be defined as

\widetilde{x}:=\{y\in(\partial K)^{[R-1]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\subset y\}\sqcup\{z\in K^{[R]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\subset z\}=\overline{x}^{\partial K}\sqcup\overline{x}^{K},

where this definition also applies to any set
A\subset K^{[0]},
with the convention that
\overline{A}^{\partial K}=\emptyset
whenever
A\not\subset\partial K^{[0]}.

The first issue we encounter then is that
\widetilde{K}
is not a cell complex, as for example in the case of
y\in(\partial K)^{[R-1]},

\widetilde{K}
does not contain the vertex
\{y\}=\overline{y}^{\partial K}.
One can easily fix this by considering the dual of
K
to be
\widetilde{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K}
but then this notion of dual does not satisfy any involution property as it ”adds” more cells to the original cell complex: for example the dual of a graph made of one edge, therefore having two boundary components each made of one vertex, is a graph made of two edges forming a simple path of length 2. The double dual would then be a graph forming a simple path of length 3, etc. The radically opposite alternative would be to ”ignore” the cells in
\partial K
and define the dual of
K
to be
\overline{K\setminus\partial K},
with obvious drawbacks when it comes to the involution property, as this definition ”removes” cells. It turns out that alternating between these two definitions is essentially the idea behind the construction of the duality of this chapter: the dual of a path of length 1 is a path of length 2 and the double dual is again a path of length 1.

But how can we signal when to use the definition that adds cells around the boundary versus the definition that removes cells? We chose to do this by defining our duality map on ”relative cell complexes”, i.e. couples of the type
(K,J)
where
J\leq K,
in the particular case where
K\in\mathsf{C}
and
J\in B^{[R-1]},\penalty\ J\leq\partial K.
The dual of such a relative cell complex would use the definition that adds cells on the cells in
\partial K\setminus J
and use the definition that removes cell on the cells in
J.

This is where the picture with cobordisms arises: an element
(K,J)
where
K\in\mathsf{C}^{R}
and
J\in\mathsf{B}^{[R-1]},\penalty\ J\leq\partial K,
can be interpreted as a cobordism from some ”ingoing components”
J
to some ”outgoing components”
L:=\partial K\setminus J.
In this picture, the duality maps the ingoing components to the outgoing components of the dual. For example, the two-dimensional lattice we used to define the Ising model in Section
0.1
can be seen as a cobordism
(K,J)
where
J=\partial K
is the only boundary component of
K,
for which we indeed defined the dual using the definition that ”ignores” the cells in
\partial K.

To make this idea precise, we will need the notion of midsection, defined in the next Section
3.1
, a crucial notion for this chapter and Chapter
4
. In Section
3.2
, we introduce the notions of non-degenerate, local and exactly collared relative cell complexes. Non-degenerate and local relative cc are used in Section
3.3
to introduce our definition of cobordism and the corresponding notion of duality. Exactly collared relative cc will single out a sub-class of cobordisms having additional regularity around the ingoing boundary component. We state our main result in Section
3.3
implying in particular that the dual of a cobordism is also a cobordism. As a corollary of this result we show that the double dual of an exactly collared cobordism is isomorphic to the original cobordism. In Section
3.4
, we illustrate some examples of duals of cobordisms, before giving the proof of the main theorem in Section
3.5
.

3.1
Midsections

We recall that the notation
\mathsf{C}
is used for the set of non-singular non-pinching lcc and
\mathsf{C}^{R}
for the set of elements in
\mathsf{C}
of rank
R.

\mathsf{B}^{R}
is the set of closed non-pinching cell-connected
R
-cc and by convention contains the empty cc
\{\emptyset\},
which is a sub-cc of every cc. Similarly
\mathsf{B}
denotes the union of all elements in
\mathsf{B}^{R},
for
R\geq 0.
As a consequence, the boundary of an element in
\mathsf{C}^{R}
is an element of
\mathsf{B}^{R-1}.

Let us introduce a generalization of the notations previously used, named ”collar”. This tool is a central element of the definition of a midsection and will be used throughout the rest of this work.

Definition 3.1

(Collar)
.

Let
A
be a finite set and
\mathcal{A}
be a collection of subsets of
A.
If
B\subset A
we define the
collar of
B
in
\mathcal{A},
by

\mathcal{A}_{B}:=\{x\in\mathcal{A}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \emptyset\neq x\cap B\neq x\}.

Moreover, if
C\in\mathcal{A},
we use the following definition and notation for the collar of
B
in
\mathcal{A}
restricted to
C:

\mathcal{A}_{B}^{C}:=\{x\in\mathcal{A}_{B}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\subset C\}.

By abuse of notation, in case
\mathcal{B}
is also a collection of subsets of
A,
the notation
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{B}}
is used to denote
\mathcal{A}_{B},
where
B:=\cup_{x\in\mathcal{B}}x
is the set of elements of
A
contained in a set in
\mathcal{B}.

For example if
K\in\mathsf{C}
and
J\leq K
we have

K_{J}=\{x\in K\penalty\ |\penalty\ \emptyset\neq x\cap J^{[0]}\neq x\}.

Since we use the notation
\mathcal{E}
for the set of edges in
K
when there is no ambiguity on what the cell complex
K
is, we will often use the following notation for
x\in K:

\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}=\{e\in K^{[1]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \emptyset\neq e\cap J^{[0]}\neq e,\quad e\subset x\}.

The following lemma is an important first step in order to endow the notion of midsection with the structure of cc.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
K
be a local cell complex and
J\leq K.
Then the map

x\longmapsto\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x},\quad x\in K_{J}

is an injective poset homomorphism such that the inverse is a poset homomorphism.

Proof.

We first notice that since
K
is cell-connected we have that
\mathcal{E}^{x}_{J}\neq\emptyset
for all
x\in K_{J}.

Let
x,y\in K_{J}
be such that
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x\cap y},
where the second equality is simply a consequence of the definition of a collar. By this equality, in order to show that
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}
implies
x=y,
it is sufficient to consider the case where say
x\subset y.
For this we proceed by induction on
r=\operatorname{rk}(x)\geq 1.

If
\operatorname{rk}(x)=1
then
x=\{v,w\}\in\mathcal{E}_{J},
where
v\in J^{[0]},\penalty\ w\in K^{[0]}\setminus J^{[0]}
and if we suppose by contradiction that
y\neq x
then by axiom
iii)
we can without loss of generality suppose that
\operatorname{rk}(y)=\operatorname{rk}(x)+1=2,
so that
y\in K^{[2]}_{J}.
By the diamond property
iv)
there exists
x^{\prime}\in K^{[1]}
such that
\bigtriangledown\left(v\right)\cap\triangle\left(y\right)=\{x,x^{\prime}\}.
The edge
x^{\prime}
is not in
\mathcal{E}_{J}
since otherwise it would imply that
\{x,x^{\prime}\}\subset\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}
which contradicts
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}.
By Lemma
1.12
the 2-cell
y
admits a representation such that
y=[x,x^{\prime},e_{1},\dots,e_{k}]
where
e_{k}\cap x=\{w\}
thus in particular
e_{k}\notin J^{[1]}.
Therefore there is
1\leq i\leq k
such that
e_{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}
which is also a contradiction with
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}
and this shows that
x=y.

Let’s assume the claim true for all
x,y
such that
x\subset y
and
\operatorname{rk}(x)\leq r-1.
Take
x\in K_{J}^{[r]}
and
y\in K_{J}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}
and
x\subsetneq y.
Like before we can without loss of generality assume that
\operatorname{rk}(y)=r+1.
By induction we have that
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{w}\subsetneq\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}
for all
w\subsetneq x,
and, using this inclusion with
w=x\cap x^{\prime},
this implies in particular that
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}}\not\subset\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}
for all
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}\cap K_{x}
(since
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
by locality of
K
). Since
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}\neq\emptyset
there exists
w\in\triangle\left(x\right)\cap K_{J}.
By the diamond property
iv)
, there exists
x^{\prime}\in K^{[r]}
such that
\bigtriangledown\left(w\right)\cap\triangle\left(y\right)=\{x,x^{\prime}\}.
But
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}
since so does
w
and
w\subset x^{\prime},
therefore
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}.
This is a contradiction with our induction hypothesis and therefore
x=y.

The definition of
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}
and the injectivity proven above imply that if
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}\subset\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}
then

\mathcal{E}_{J}^{(x\cap y)}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}\cap\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}

and therefore
x=x\cap y
or equivalently
x\subset y
and this concludes the proof.
∎

The previous lemma ensures that the rank function of a midsection given in the following definition is well defined. This definition can be seen as a generalization of the notion of midsection used in
[
40
]
to classify ”causal slices” of causal triangulations and which was initially introduced in the form of a coloured graph dual to a causal slice in
[
3
]
.

Definition 3.3

(Midsection)
.

Let
K
be a local cell complex and
J\leq K.
We define the
midsection of
J
in
K
to be
(M_{J}^{K},\operatorname{rk}_{M_{J}^{K}})
where

M_{J}^{K}:=\{\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in K_{J}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{rk}_{M_{J}^{K}}(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x})=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)-1.

Figure 13
:
Midsection
M_{J}^{K}
associated to the boundary part
J
of a 3-cc
K
composed of three maximal cells (the center one having dots on its vertices).

We give an illustration of a midsection in Figure
13
. The next proposition shows that midsections are cc under rather general assumptions.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
K
be a local cell complex and
J\lneq K
such that
x\not\subset J^{[0]}
for all
x\in K\setminus J
and such that
K_{J}\neq\emptyset.
Then
(M_{J}^{K},\operatorname{rk}_{M_{J}^{K}})
is a cc and if
K
is pure then so is
M_{J}^{K}
and
\operatorname{Rk}(M_{J}^{K})=\operatorname{Rk}(K)-1.
Moreover if
J\cap x
is connected for all
x\in K^{[2]}_{J}
then
M_{J}^{K}
is graph-based.

Proof.

Since
K
is cell-connected,
J\neq K,
and by the condition
x\not\subset J^{[0]}
for all
x\in K\setminus J,
there is at least one edge of
K
having one vertex in
J^{[0]}
and one vertex in
K^{[0]}\setminus J^{[0]},
therefore
K_{J}\neq\emptyset.

By definition of
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}
and
\operatorname{rk}_{M_{J}^{K}},
the elements in
M_{J}^{K}
of rank
0,
i.e. vertices, are indeed 1-element sets and every set
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}
is indeed a set of vertices of
M_{J}^{K}.
Since for
x,y\in K,
if
x\in K_{J}
and
x\subset y
then
y\in K_{J},
the axioms of the Definition
1.1
are a direct consequence of Lemma
3.2
and the axioms for
K.

Let
R=\operatorname{Rk}(K).
By purity of
K,
every cell in
x\in K_{J}
is contained in a
R
-cell of
K,
which must then belong to
K_{J}
and constitute an
R-1
cell in
M_{J}^{K}
containing
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x},
showing that
M_{J}^{K}
is also pure.

By Lemma
1.12
, and since
K
is local,
K\cap x
is a cycle for every 2-cell
x
of
K.
Therefore, for every edge
\mathcal{E}^{x}_{J}
of
M_{J}^{K},
i.e. for every
x\in K^{[2]}_{J},
if
J\cap x
is connected then there are exactly two edges in
K^{[1]}_{J}
included in
K\cap x,
and therefore there are exactly two vertices of
M_{J}^{K}
in
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}.

∎

3.2
Non-degenerate, local and exactly collared relative cc

In this section we introduce what it means in our context to consider a ”cell complex relatively to another cell complex containing it”. We first explicitly define relative cell complexes, homomorphism between such objects as well as the notion of ”relative homomorphism” between relative cell complexes. Relative homomorphism are used mostly in the next chapter, to define the notion of relative reduction and relative collapses at the end of Section
4.1
.

Definition 3.5

(Relative cc and homomorphism)
.

A couple
(K,J)
of cc is called a
relative cc
if
J\leq K.

We say that a map
\phi:(K,J)\longrightarrow(K^{\prime},L)
is a
homomorphism of relative cc
if it is a cc-homomorphism
\phi:K\longrightarrow K^{\prime}
such that
\phi(J)=L.
A
relative cc-homomorphism
or a
homomorphism of
J
relative to
K
is a homomorphism
\phi:(K,J)\longrightarrow(K^{\prime},L)
of relative cc such that
\phi|_{K\setminus J}:K\setminus J\longrightarrow K^{\prime}\setminus L
is a poset isomorphism.

Such ”relative objects” are present in many different contexts, notably in topology with based space or algebraic topology with relative homology group. However our definition in effect reverses the focus that is usually put on such relative objects. While in topology one tends to ”ignore” the structure contained in the subspace, here the cell complex
K
in a relative cc
(K,J)
can be interpreted merely as a ”background” cell complex structure on which a relative homomorphism acts trivially. This point of view remains valid in the following definitions of the specific types of relative cell complexes we introduce next.

Definition 3.6

(Non-degenerate relative cc)
.

We say that a relative cc
(K,J)
is
non-degenerate
if for all
x\in K\setminus J
then
x\not\subset J^{[0]}.
We say that
(K,J)
is
degenerate
if it is not non-degenerate.

Examples of degenerate relative cell complexes
(K,J)
can be easily constructed when
K
has relatively few maximal cells and
J=\partial K.
For instance if
K
has only one maximal cell then such cell has automatically all its vertices on the boundary.

The following definition extends the notion of locality to relative cell complexes. It is the first time where the notion of midsection enters crucially in our formalism. As will be discussed after this definition, it was not obvious to us that this was the right notion of locality in this context. It will however become apparent from the proof of Theorem
3.14
that this notion of locality for relative cc contains exactly the assumptions needed for the duality to be well-behaved around the ingoing boundary of a cobordism.

Definition 3.7

(Local relative cc)
.

We say that a relative cc
(K,J)
is
local
if it is non-degenerate,
K
is local, every connected component
J_{0}
of
J
satisfies that
J_{0}\cap x
is connected for all
x\in K_{J_{0}}
and
M_{J_{0}}^{K}
is cell-connected. In particular
(K,\emptyset)
is local for any lcc
K.

The case in which
K\in\mathsf{C}^{R}
and
J\in\mathsf{B}^{R-1}
is a sub-cc of
\partial K
will be our main focus in what follows. In this case, the assumption in the definition of local relative cc that the midsection is cell-connected is in fact not redundant with the assumption that
K\in\mathsf{C}^{R}.
In other words, knowing that
K
is both non-pinching and cell-connected together with the assumption that
(K,J)
is non-degenerate is not sufficient to prove that
M_{J}^{K}
is cell-connected. This apparently remains the case even when adding an assumption on the connectedness of 2-cells: for example we could require that
\left[v,x\right]^{(2)}:=\{y\in K^{(2)}\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\in y\subset x\}
is connected (as a section, c.f. definition in Section
2.3
) for all
x\in J^{[0]}.
Also, the connectedness of the cells in
M_{J}^{K}
seems closely related to the connectedness of
\partial(J\cap x)
when this cc is well-defined, i.e. when
J\cap x
is pure. The purity of
J\cap x
is however not guaranteed in general and not necessary in order for the cell in
M_{J}^{K}
to be connected (this purity assumption will actually come up later in Section
4.2
as an assumption allowing to define a rank function on a poset called transition, but is not required for the results of this chapter).
These observations support the interpretation of the locality assumption on a relative cc
(K,J)
(more specifically the connectedness of the midsection
M_{J}^{K}
) as a condition allowing to freely choose the endpoint of any path in
K
having its last edge in
K_{J}.
If
p
is such a path, an edge in
M_{J_{0}}^{K}
indeed corresponds to a 2-cell
C\in K_{J_{0}},
which defines moves of the form
m_{p_{0}}^{C}
where
p_{0}
is a simple subpath of
C\cap p
(cf. Definition
2.27
of moves).

We now introduce the notion of exactly collared relative cc. This notion will be used later on in Corollary
3.15
in order to prove that the duality map defines an involution on the set of cobordism introduced in the next section.

Definition 3.8

(Exactly collared relative cc)
.

Let
(K,J)
be a non-degenerate cc. We say that
(K,J)
is
exactly collared
if the map from
M_{J}^{K}
to
J
defined by
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}\mapsto J\cap x
is a cc-isomorphism. In particular an exactly collared relative cc
(K,J)
such that
K
is local is a local relative cc and
(K,\emptyset)
is also exactly collared for all lcc
K.

We give an illustration of a simple example of an exactly collared cobordism in Figure
16
.
In the next sections of this chapter, we will be particularly interested in the case of relative cc
(K,J)
where
K\in\mathsf{C}
and
J\leq K
is a (possibly empty) union of boundary components of
\partial K.
We therefore mention the following lemma, as it can shed light on certain definitions given thereafter.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
K\in\mathsf{C}^{R}.
Then
J\leq\partial K
and
J\in\mathsf{B}^{R-1}
if and only if
J
is a (possibly empty) union of connected components of
\partial K.

Proof.

Let
R=\operatorname{Rk}(K).
A union of boundary components of
K
is clearly an element in
\mathsf{B}^{R-1},
by Lemma
1.21
. In order to prove the reverse implication it is sufficient to prove the case where
\partial K
is connected and to show that
J\neq\emptyset
implies
J=\partial K.
If
J\in\mathsf{B}^{R-1}
is non-empty then it is a closed
(R-1)
-cc. If by contradiction there exists a vertex in
\partial K\setminus J,
the connectedness of
\partial K
implies that this vertex is linked to a vertex in
J
via a path in
(\partial K)^{(1)}.
Hence there is an edge
e=\{v,w\}
in
\partial K
such that
v\in J
and
w\in\partial K\setminus J.
Now we have that
\overline{J}\leq\overline{\partial K}
and
\overline{e}^{\partial K}
(the dual of
e
in
\partial K
) is a
(R-2)
-cell of
\overline{\partial K}
contained in
\overline{v}^{\partial K},
an
(R-1)
-cell of
\overline{J}.
Therefore it implies that
\overline{e}^{\partial K}=\overline{e}^{J}\in J^{[R-2]}
but since
\overline{J}
is closed we have that
\overline{e}
is contained in two maximal cells of
J.
Since
\overline{w}^{\partial K}
is a maximal cell of
\overline{\partial K}\setminus\overline{J}
containing
\overline{e}^{J}
this contradicts the non-singularity of
\overline{\partial K}.

∎

3.3
Duality map for cobordisms and main results

The following set of relative cell complexes constitutes our notion of cobordism.

Definition 3.10

(Cobordism)
.

The set of
combinatorial cobordisms
, or simply
cobordisms
, is denoted and defined as

\mathsf{Cob}:=\{(K-J)\penalty\ |\penalty\ K\in\mathsf{C},\penalty\ J\leq\partial K,\penalty\ J\in\mathsf{B}^{\operatorname{Rk}(K)-1},\penalty\ (K,J)\text{ non-degenerate and local}\}.

We also use the notation
\mathsf{Cob}^{R}
to denote the elements in
\mathsf{Cob}
such that
\operatorname{Rk}(K)=R,
in which case
R
is also called the
rank
or
dimension
of the cobordisms in
\mathsf{Cob}^{R}.
The boundary of a cobordism is simply defined as
\partial(K-J):=\partial K.
The boundary components
J
of a cobordism
(K-J)
are called
ingoing (boundary) components
or
removed components
of the cobordism and the cells in
J
are sometimes called the
removed cells
. The boundary components in
\partial K\setminus J
are called
outgoing (boundary) components
of
(K-J).
We say that
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob}
is
exactly collared
if
(K,J)
is exactly collared.

We use the sign ”
-
” in
(K-J)
to distinguish such an element from a usual relative cc and we use the term ”removed” for
J
and its cells because the duality map we define later in Definition
3.13
indeed acts on
(K-J)
as if the cells in
J
are removed from
K.

There are numerous examples of cobordisms, as in particular every element
(K-J)
where
K
is a non-singular (i.e. non-branching) and non-pinching simplicial complex and
J
is a union of connected components of
\partial K
is an element of
\mathsf{Cob}.
In other words, the assumptions of locality and non-degeneracy are always satisfied for relative complexes
(K,J)
where
K\in\mathsf{C}
is simplicial.

Before introducing its generalization, we recall that in Definition
1.14
we defined the dual of a non-empty set
A\subset K^{[0]}
for a cc
K
by

\overline{A}^{K}:=\{z\in K^{[\operatorname{Rk}(K)]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ A\subset z\},

also denoted
\overline{A}
in case there is no ambiguity on the cc
K.
The following definition constitutes a canonical way to extend the latter definition in order to associate a dual cell to the cells on the boundary of a non-singular cell complex.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
K
be a cc and let
A\subset K^{[0]}
be non-empty. We define the
\sim
-dual of
A
to be

\widetilde{A}^{K}:=\overline{A}^{K}\sqcup\overline{A}^{\partial K},

where by convention
\overline{A}^{\partial K}=\emptyset
if
A\not\subset(\partial K)^{[0]}.
We also write
\widetilde{x}
for
\widetilde{x}^{K}
when there is no ambiguity on
K.

As explained in the introductory paragraph of this chapter, this definition for example implies that the
\sim
-dual of a maximal cell of the boundary
y\in\partial K^{[R-1]}
of a
R
-cc
K
is given by

\widetilde{y}=\{y,z\}

where
z\in K^{[R]}
is the unique
R
-cell of
K
containing
y.
Other examples of
\sim
-dual are given in Figures
15
and
16
.

For a collection of sets
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{P}\left(K^{[0]}\right),
we use the notation

\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}^{K}:=\{\widetilde{A}^{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ A\in\mathcal{A}\}.

For example if
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob},
we obtain
\widetilde{K\setminus J}^{K}=\{\widetilde{x}^{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in K\setminus J\}.
We simply write
\widetilde{K\setminus J}
instead of
\widetilde{K\setminus J}^{K}.
Note that if
K
is closed then
\widetilde{K}=\widetilde{K\setminus\emptyset}=\overline{K}.

We would like to also define a rank function for elements in
\widetilde{K}
when
K\in\mathsf{C}
and this is done by mean of the following lemma, which generalizes Lemma
1.16
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
K
be a non-singular cc and let
x,y\in K.
Then

\widetilde{x}^{K}\subsetneq\widetilde{y}^{K}\quad\text{ if and only if }\quad y\subsetneq x.

Proof.

First we note that Lemma
1.6
and Lemma
1.16
imply that if
x\in K\setminus\partial K
then

\overline{y}^{K}\subsetneq\overline{x}^{K}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad y\subsetneq x.

(13)

We suppose first that
\widetilde{x}^{K}\subsetneq\widetilde{y}^{K},
i.e.
\overline{x}^{K}\subset\overline{y}^{K}
and
\overline{x}^{\partial K}\subset\overline{y}^{\partial K}.
If
x\in K\setminus\partial K
then (
13
) implies that
y\subset x.
If
x\in\partial K
then
\overline{x}^{\partial K}\subset\overline{y}^{\partial K}
implies
y\subset x
as a consequence of Lemma
1.16
for the case of the closed cc
\partial K.
Since our assumption implies that
x\neq y
we obtain
y\subsetneq x.

Conversely, if
y\subsetneq x
then the definition of dual set implies
\widetilde{x}^{K}\subset\widetilde{y}^{K}.
If
x\in K\setminus\partial K
then the equivalence (
13
) implies that
\overline{x}^{K}\subsetneq\overline{y}^{K}
and in particular
\widetilde{x}^{K}\neq\widetilde{y}^{K}.
Similarly if
x\in\partial K
then Lemma
1.16
implies that
\overline{x}^{\partial K}\subsetneq\overline{y}^{\partial K}
and
\widetilde{x}^{K}\neq\widetilde{y}^{K}.

∎

As a consequence of Lemma
3.12
, the map
x\mapsto\widetilde{x}
is a bijection from the non-singular cc
K
to
\widetilde{K}
and the rank function

\operatorname{rk}_{\widetilde{K}}(\widetilde{x}):=\operatorname{Rk}(K)-\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)

where
x\in\widetilde{K}
is well defined for all
\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{K}.

Let
K,K^{\prime}
be disjoint posets with rank functions
\operatorname{rk}_{K}
and
\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\prime}}.
We define the disjoint union
K\sqcup K^{\prime}
to be a poset with rank function

\operatorname{rk}_{K\sqcup K^{\prime}}:=\operatorname{rk}_{K}+\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\prime}}.

For an element
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob},
this definition allows us to have a rank function on the poset defined as the disjoint union

\widetilde{K\setminus J}\sqcup\overline{\partial K\setminus J}=\widetilde{K\setminus J}^{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K\setminus J}^{\partial K}

used below in our definition of dual cobordism.
The latter union is indeed disjoint, since by non-degeneracy of
(K,J)
every cell
x\in\partial K\setminus J
satisfies
\overline{x}^{\partial K}\subsetneq\widetilde{x}^{K},
which implies in particular that
\overline{x}^{\partial K}\neq\widetilde{x}^{K}
for all such
x
and therefore

\widetilde{K\setminus J}^{K}\cap\overline{\partial K\setminus J}^{\partial K}=\emptyset.

More explicitly, if
(K,J)
is non-degenerate our notations imply the following expressions for the rank function on
\widetilde{K\setminus J}\sqcup\overline{\partial K\setminus J}:

\operatorname{rk}_{\widetilde{K\setminus J}\sqcup\overline{\partial K\setminus J}}(x^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{K}}(x^{\prime})=R-\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)&\textit{if }x^{\prime}=\overline{x}^{K}\textit{ for }x\in K\setminus\partial K,\\
\operatorname{rk}_{\widetilde{K}}(x^{\prime})=R-\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)&\textit{if }x^{\prime}=\widetilde{x}^{K}\textit{ for }x\in\partial K,\\
\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{\partial K}}(x^{\prime})=(R-1)-\operatorname{rk}_{\partial K}(x)&\textit{if }x^{\prime}=\overline{x}^{\partial K}\textit{ for }x\in\partial K.\end{cases}

where
R=\operatorname{Rk}(K).
The particular case
J=\emptyset
yields

(\widetilde{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K})^{[0]}=\overline{K^{[R]}}^{K}\sqcup\overline{(\partial K)^{[R-1]}}^{\partial K},

for the dual sets of rank
0.
For dual sets of rank
1\leq r\leq R-1
we have

(\widetilde{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K})^{[r]}=\overline{(K\setminus\partial K)^{[R-r]}}^{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K^{[R-r-1]}}^{\partial K}\sqcup\widetilde{(\partial K)^{[R-r]}}^{K}.

For example, if
e^{\prime}\in(\widetilde{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K})^{[1]}
then
e^{\prime}
is a dual set having one of the three following forms:

•

the ”usual” dual of a sub-maximal cell of
K
i.e.
e^{\prime}=\overline{y}^{K}
for
y\in K^{[R-1]},

•

the ”usual” dual of a sub-maximal cell of
\partial K,
i.e.
e^{\prime}=\overline{y}^{\partial K}
for
y\in(\partial K)^{[R-2]},

•

the
\sim
-dual of a maximal cell in
\partial K
, i.e.
e^{\prime}=\widetilde{y}^{K}=\{y,z\}
where
y\in(\partial K)^{[R-1]}
and
z\in K^{[R]}
is the unique maximal cell of
K
containing
y.

With this in mind, the next definition of duality map for cobordism is more transparent.

Definition 3.13

(Dual cobordism)
.

Let
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob},
we define the
dual cobordism
to be

\overline{(K-J)}:=\left((\widetilde{K\setminus J}\sqcup\overline{\partial K\setminus J})-\overline{\partial K\setminus J}\right).

This definition, above all, raises the following question: is
\overline{(K-J)}
also a cobordism? This question is answered positively by the main results of this chapter stated in the following theorem.

Theorem 3.14
.

If
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob}^{R}
then:

•

\overline{(K-J)}\in\mathsf{Cob}^{R},

•

\overline{(K-J)}
is exactly collared,

•

\overline{K_{J}}\in\mathsf{B}^{R-1}
and

\partial\overline{(K-J)}=\overline{\partial K\setminus J}\sqcup\overline{K_{J}}.

Proof.

See Section
3.5
.
∎

In the proof of Theorem
3.14
we will see that, as a consequence of Lemma
3.2
, we have
\overline{K_{J}}\cong\overline{M_{J}^{K}}.
In other words, the dual outgoing component
\overline{K_{J}}
is better understood as the dual of the midsection associated to the ingoing components
J.
We point out that although
\overline{K_{J}}
is a (closed) cell complex, it is not the case of
K_{J}
and in particular
\overline{\overline{K_{J}}}\not\cong K_{J}.

Definition
3.13
leads to the interpretation that the action of the duality map on cobordisms in effect exchanges the role of the ingoing and outgoing boundary components. This can be seen particularly well in the case of the cylinder depicted in Figure
16
of the next section.

As a first example, we can look at what Definition
3.13
implies for the special cases where
J=\emptyset
and
J=\partial K.
In the first case we get:

\overline{(K-\emptyset)}=\left((\widetilde{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K})-\overline{\partial K}\right).

We can think of this case as duality acting on a non-singular cc
K,
as no other data is specified. Such an example is depicted in Figure
15
. As a consequence of Theorem
3.14
, we have that
\widetilde{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K}\in\mathsf{C}
and

\partial(\widetilde{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K})=\overline{\partial K}.

The dual of
(K-\emptyset)
is therefore a cobordism with ingoing components equal to the entire boundary. For such cobordisms, corresponding to our second case
J=\partial K,
the duality gives

\overline{(K-\partial K)}=\left(\widetilde{K\setminus\partial K}-\emptyset\right).

An example of such a duality was given when discussing the Ising model in Section
0.1
. Another consequence of Theorem
3.14
is therefore that
\widetilde{K\setminus\partial K}\in\mathsf{C}
for all
(K-\partial K)\in\mathsf{Cob}.

Finally, the following corollary concerns the special case of exactly collared cobordisms, for which the involution property holds. To state this result, we defined the isomorphism
(K-J)\cong(K^{\prime}-J^{\prime})
between cobordism as
K\cong K^{\prime}
with an isomorphism mapping
J
to
J^{\prime}.
A consequence of this corollary is that
(K-J)
is exactly collared if and only if there exists
(K^{\prime}-J^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Cob}
such that
\overline{(K^{\prime}-J^{\prime})}=(K-J),
a property that motivated us to include the notion of ”exactness” in the term used to denote such cobordisms.

Corollary 3.15
.

If
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob}
is exactly collared then we have

\partial\overline{(K-J)}\cong\overline{J}\sqcup\overline{\partial K\setminus J}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\overline{(K-J)}}\cong(K-J).

Proof.

By Theorem
3.14
and using the assumption that
(K-J)
is exactly collared, i.e. that
J\cong M_{J}^{K},
and the identity
\overline{M_{J}^{K}}\cong\overline{K_{J}},
we directly obtain that
\partial\overline{(K-J)}\cong\overline{J}\sqcup\overline{\partial K\setminus J}.

The identity
\overline{\overline{(K-J)}}\cong(K-J)
is a consequence of the following computation, using the previous point and the assumption that
(K-J)
is exactly collared:

\displaystyle\overline{\overline{(K-J)}}

\displaystyle=\overline{(\widetilde{K\setminus J}\sqcup\overline{\partial K\setminus J}-\overline{\partial K\setminus J})}

\displaystyle=\left(\widetilde{\left((\widetilde{K\setminus J}\sqcup\overline{\partial K\setminus J})\setminus\overline{\partial K\setminus J}\right)}\sqcup\overline{\overline{K_{J}}}-\overline{\overline{K_{J}}}\right)

\displaystyle\cong(\widetilde{\widetilde{K\setminus J}}\sqcup\overline{\overline{J}}-\overline{\overline{J}})\cong(K-J),

where we also used Lemma
3.12
and Proposition
1.17
for the last identity.
∎

3.4
Basic examples in pictures

In this section, we use illustrations to give simple examples of the duality map on cobordisms of rank 2 and 3 and we describe the example of a torus-like
3
-cell having a dual representing a typical example of an inhomogeneous cell complex. We chose examples in these dimensions to give some intuition of the generality under which the duality is defined, while keeping the dimension of the representation under 3. In order to make the following examples more transparent, we use different symbols for the vertices of the dual cobordism, as explained in Figure
14
.

Figure 14
:
These are the different illustrations chosen to represent the vertices in the dual of a cell complex, when such vertex is the dual of a 1-cell (stripe), 2-cell (cross) and 3-cell (star).

The first example is a
3
-dimensional cobordism with empty ingoing component made out of two tetrahedra sharing a triangle. We can think of this case simply as a non-singular cell complex.
The dual of this cobordism is composed of five
3
-cells, one of which is illustrated below in the Figure
15
.

Figure 15
:
A cc
K\in\mathsf{C}

(K-\emptyset),
