\overline{(K-\emptyset)}
is represented inside
K.
In this example the entire boundary of the dual is made of removed cells (i.e. cells in the ingoing component). The cobordism
\overline{(K-\emptyset)}
has only two vertices not lying on the boundary corresponding to the dual of the two tetrahedra in
K.

The second example is a 2-dimensional cobordism
(K-J)
where
K
is a discretization of a 2-dimensional cylinder where the ingoing boundary component
J
is on the left of the illustration.
In this example,
\overline{(K-J)}\cong(K-J)
and both
(K-J)
and its dual have nine
2
-cells each containing four vertices. This is also an example of an exactly collared cobordism. In the lower part of Figure

we illustrate a cell obtained as the dual of a vertex lying on the outgoing boundary component.

Figure 16
:
An example of a 2-dimensional cobordism that can be seen as a discretization of the boundary of a cylinder. In this case the primal and dual cobordisms are isomorphic, with the location of the ingoing and outgoing components exchanged, the ingoing component being represented using cells with white stripes. This is an example of an exactly collared cobordism.

The last example is given by a simple discretization of a solid torus using a single
3
-cell, twelve 2-cells, twenty-four edges and twelve vertices, represented on the left of Figure

. The dual of this cell complex (seen as a cobordism of the form
(K-\emptyset)
), represented on the right of the figure, gives an example of a non-homogeneous cell complex which therefore does not admit a geometrical realization as a manifold. A similar construction is given in Figure 3 of

]
and, if subdivided appropriately, constitutes an example of an inhomogeneous vertex in a closed cc.

Figure 17
:
The illustration on the left gives an example of a 3-cc
K
composed of a single
3
-cell
x
and having a boundary that defines a discretization of a 2-dimensional torus. On the right, we get a representation of
\overline{(K-\emptyset)}=\left((\widetilde{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K})-\overline{\partial K}\right)
by making an identification of the vertices on the boundary of the discretized square
ABDC
following the usual construction of a 2-dimensional torus from a square hinted by the black and white arrows. That is to say: we identify the four vertices from the vertex
A
to the vertex
B
with the four vertices between
C
and
D
in this order and similarly for the vertices between
A
and
C
with the vertices between
B
and
D.
This identifications of vertices then automatically produce identifications of the edges having both vertices identified with two other vertices and similarly for the 2-cells (in this case triangles) having
\overline{x}
as a vertex and an edge on the boundary of the square
ABDC.
The top vertex
\overline{x}
corresponds to a non-homogeneous point in a geometric realization of
\widetilde{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K}.

3.5
Proof of the Theorem on duality

This section simply contains the proof of Theorem
3.14
stating that if
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob}^{R}
then:

•

\overline{(K-J)}\in\mathsf{Cob}^{R},

•

\overline{(K-J)}
is exactly collared,

•

\overline{K_{J}}\in\mathsf{B}^{R-1}
and
\partial\overline{(K-J)}=\overline{\partial K\setminus J}\sqcup\overline{K_{J}}.

Proof of Theorem
3.14
.

Let
R=\operatorname{Rk}(K).
We split the proof into three statements
1a
,
1b
and
2
as follows.

Suppose that
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob}.

1.

Then we have
K^{\prime}:=\widetilde{K\setminus J}\sqcup\overline{\partial K\setminus J}\in\mathsf{C},

(a)

for the special case
J=\emptyset,

(b)

and for
J
arbitrary as a consequence of the following statement: if
K^{\prime}\in\mathsf{C}
and
J_{0}
is a connected component of
\partial K\setminus J
such that
(K,J\sqcup J_{0})
is local then

K^{\prime}_{0}:=\widetilde{K\setminus(J\sqcup J_{0})}\sqcup\overline{\partial K\setminus(J\sqcup J_{0})}\in\mathsf{C};

2.

If we define
J^{\prime}:=\overline{\partial K\setminus J},
then
J^{\prime}\in\mathsf{B}^{R-1},

(K^{\prime},J^{\prime})
is exactly collared and

\partial K^{\prime}=J^{\prime}\sqcup\overline{K_{J}}.

To prove the point
1a
, we show that
K^{\prime}:=\widetilde{K}\sqcup\overline{\partial K}
satisfies Axioms
i)
,
ii)
,
iii)
and
iv)
and is a local non-singular non-pinching cell complex of rank
R.

Axiom
i)
is a consequence of Lemma
3.12
for the cells of
K^{\prime}
included in
\widetilde{K}
and is a consequence of Lemma
1.16
for the cells included in
\overline{\partial K}.
To prove
ii)
, we take
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}\cap y^{\prime}\neq\emptyset.
If
x^{\prime}=\widetilde{x}
and
y^{\prime}=\widetilde{y}
are included in
\widetilde{K}
then there exists a cell
w\supset x\cup y
of minimal rank, and by Lemma
3.12
we get
\widetilde{w}=\widetilde{x}\cap\widetilde{y}
(using the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
1.17
). If
x^{\prime}=\overline{x}
and
y^{\prime}=\overline{y}
are cells in
\overline{\partial K}
then
ii)
is a consequence of Proposition
1.17
. If say
x^{\prime}=\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{K}
and
y^{\prime}=\overline{y}\in\overline{\partial K}
then

\widetilde{x}\cap\overline{y}=(\overline{x}^{K}\cup\overline{x}^{\partial K})\cap\overline{y}^{\partial K}=\overline{x}^{\partial K}\cap\overline{y}^{\partial K},

so
\overline{x}^{\partial K}\cap\overline{y}^{\partial K}\neq\emptyset.
Therefore
x,y\in\partial K
and

\overline{x}^{\partial K}\cap\overline{y}^{\partial K}=\overline{w}^{\partial K},

where
w=\overline{\overline{x}\cap\overline{y}}\in\partial K.
Axiom
iii)
is satisfied for elements in
\widetilde{K\setminus\partial K}
or in
\overline{\partial K}
as a consequence of Axiom
iii)
for
K
and
\partial K
and by Lemma
3.12
and Proposition
1.17
respectively. The case
\overline{x}\subsetneq\widetilde{y}
is shown by finding a cell
x^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}
included in
\widetilde{y}
and having
\overline{x}
as a face by the following arguments. We have

\overline{x}^{\partial K}\subsetneq(\overline{y}^{K}\cup\overline{y}^{\partial K})

which in particular implies
\overline{x}^{\partial K}\subset\overline{y}^{\partial K}.
This in turn implies that
\overline{y}^{\partial K}\neq\emptyset
and thus
y\in\partial K.
If
\overline{x}\subsetneq\overline{y}
then the existence of
x^{\prime}
is given by Axiom
iii)
for
\overline{\partial K}.
If
\overline{x}=\overline{y}
then
x=y
and since
\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\prime}}(\widetilde{y})=\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{\partial K}}(\overline{y})+1
then
\overline{x}
is a face of
\widetilde{y}
and
x^{\prime}=\widetilde{y}
satisfies the desired conditions. The diamond property
iv)
is proven in a similar way. The only non-direct case to check is when
\overline{x}\in\overline{\partial K},

\widetilde{y}\in\widetilde{K},

\overline{x}\subset\widetilde{y}
and

\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\prime}}(\overline{x})=\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\prime}}(\widetilde{y})-2.

(14)

By the same argument as before,
y\in\partial K
and (
14
) implies that
y
is a face of
x.
We also have that
\widetilde{x}
is a coface of
\overline{x}
and a face of
\widetilde{y}
by (
14
). Since
y\subset x
this implies that

\{\overline{y},\widetilde{x}\}\subset\bigtriangledown\left(\overline{x}\right)\cap\triangle\left(\widetilde{y}\right).

(15)

If
z\in\bigtriangledown\left(\overline{x}\right)\cap\triangle\left(\widetilde{y}\right)
then either
z\in\overline{\partial K}
and

\overline{x}\subsetneq z\subset\overline{y}

which implies
z=\overline{y}
or
z\in\widetilde{K}
and

\widetilde{x}\subset z\subsetneq\widetilde{y}

which implies
z=\widetilde{x}.
Therefore the inclusion in (
15
) is in fact an equality and this shows Axiom
iv)
as well as that
K^{\prime}
is a cc.

We then prove that
K^{\prime}
is cell-connected.
K^{\prime}
is graph-based since the non-singularity of
K
implies that the edges of
K^{\prime}
are either composed of two maximal cells of
K
or of one maximal cell
y
of
\partial K
and the unique maximal cell of
K
containing
y.
The cc
K^{\prime}
is cell-connected by the following argument. If
x^{\prime}\in\widetilde{K}
is a cell of the form
x^{\prime}=\overline{x}^{K}
where
x\in K\setminus\partial K
then
x^{\prime}
is connected since
K
is non-pinching. If
x^{\prime}=\widetilde{x}
where
x\in\partial K
then
x^{\prime}
is connected if and only if there is and edge
e^{\prime}
of
K^{\prime}
with a vertex in
\overline{x}^{K}
and in
\overline{x}^{\partial K},
as both of these sets are connected since
K
is non-pinching. Let
y
be a maximal cell of
\partial K
containing
x.
The cell
y
is then included in a maximal cell
z
of
K,
we can then take
e^{\prime}=\{y,z\}.
Cells in
\overline{\partial K}
are connected since
\partial K
is non-pinching and this shows that
K^{\prime}
is cell-connected.

The cc
K^{\prime}
is connected by the following argument. If
v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime}
are vertices of
K^{\prime}
then by purity of
K
these vertices are included in maximal cells
z_{1}^{\prime}
and
z_{2}^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{K}.
These maximal cells are duals to vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
of
K
which are connected by a path in
K^{(1)}.
This path provides a finite sequence of maximal cells of
\widetilde{K}
starting with
z_{1}^{\prime}
and ending with
z_{2}^{\prime}
such that each element in the sequence shares a sub-maximal cell with the next element in the sequence and since the cells in
\widetilde{K}
are connected this ensures that there is a path linking
v_{1}^{\prime}
to
v_{2}^{\prime}
in
\widetilde{K}.

The following arguments show that
K^{\prime}
is a non-pinching non-singular cell complex of rank
R.
In order to show that
K^{\prime}
is non-pinching, we first note that
\partial K^{\prime}=\overline{\partial K}
therefore there is no
\partial K^{\prime}
-pinch in
K^{\prime}
since
\partial K
is cell-connected. Let us assume by contradiction that there is a
K^{\prime}
-pinch in
K^{\prime},
i.e. a cell
x^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}
such that
\overline{K^{\prime}}^{(1)}\cap\overline{x^{\prime}}
is not connected. If we take
x\in K
such that
\widetilde{x}=x^{\prime},
we can see that a path between two vertices
v,w
in
x
corresponds to a path between two vertices
\overline{\widetilde{v}}
and
\overline{\widetilde{w}}
in
\overline{K^{\prime}}^{(1)}\cap\overline{x^{\prime}}.
Therefore if
\overline{K^{\prime}}^{(1)}\cap\overline{x^{\prime}}
is not connected then so is
K^{(1)}\cap x,
which contradicts the assumption that
K
is cell-connected. Every cell in
K^{\prime}
is included in a
R
-cell since this is clear for the cells in
\widetilde{K}
and every
(R-1)
-cell
\overline{v}\in\overline{\partial K}
is contained in
\widetilde{v}\in(K^{\prime})^{[R]},
which is then pure of dimension
R.
Finally
K^{\prime}
is also non-singular since
K
is graph-based.

We then turn to the point
1b
. Proving this statement amounts to verify that all the properties shown for the point
1a
are still valid when restricted to cells not included in the sets
\widetilde{J_{0}}\sqcup\overline{J_{0}}.
Axioms
i)
,
iii)
and
iv)
are simply a consequence of the cc structure of
K^{\prime}
and the fact that whenever
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}_{0}
are two cells such that
x^{\prime}\subset y^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}
satisfies
x^{\prime}\subset z^{\prime}\subset y^{\prime}
then
z^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}_{0}.
Axiom
ii)
is also verified for
K^{\prime}_{0}
since if
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}_{0}
then
x^{\prime}\cap y^{\prime}
contains no maximal cell of
J_{0}
and therefore cannot be included in
\widetilde{J_{0}}\sqcup\overline{J_{0}}.
We have that
K^{\prime}_{0}
is clearly graph-based since so is
K^{\prime}
and the set of edges of
K^{\prime}_{0}
is a subset of the set of edges of
K^{\prime}.

Also
K_{0}^{\prime}
is cell-connected since so is
K^{\prime}.
The purity of
K^{\prime}_{0}
is verified if we can show that for every cell
x^{\prime}
of
K^{\prime}_{0}
contained in a maximal cell of
K^{\prime}
of the form
\widetilde{v}
where
v\in J_{0}^{[0]},
we have that
x^{\prime}
is also contained in a maximal cell of the form
\widetilde{w},
where
w\in K^{[0]}\setminus(J_{0}^{[0]}\sqcup J^{[0]}).
This is ensured by the non-degeneracy of
(K,J_{0}\sqcup J)
together with the connectedness of the cells in
K_{0}^{\prime}.

The cc
K^{\prime}_{0}
is also non-singular since the set of
(R-1)
-cells of
K_{0}^{\prime}
is a subset of the set of
(R-1)
-cells of
K^{\prime}
and their set of cofaces in
K_{0}^{\prime}
is identical to their set of cofaces in
K^{\prime}.

Now comes a key point of this proof where the locality of
(K,J)
enters crucially: showing that
K^{\prime}_{0}
is non-pinching. First we can see that there is no
K^{\prime}_{0}
-pinch in
K_{0}^{\prime}.
Indeed
K^{\prime}
has no
K^{\prime}
-pinch and
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{K^{\prime}_{0}}}\cap\widetilde{x^{\prime}}=\mathcal{G}_{\overline{K^{\prime}}}\cap\widetilde{x^{\prime}}
for all
x^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}_{0}
since if two maximal cells of
K^{\prime}_{0}
contain
x^{\prime}
and share a sub-maximal cell
y^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}
then
y^{\prime}
is also in
K^{\prime}_{0}.
It is therefore sufficient to show that
\partial K^{\prime}_{0}
contains no
\partial K^{\prime}_{0}
-pinch. For this we first prove the following relation:

\partial K_{0}^{\prime}=\overline{\partial K\setminus(J\sqcup J_{0})}\sqcup\overline{K_{J}}\sqcup\overline{K_{J_{0}}}.

(16)

In order to prove (
16
) we first observe that
\overline{\partial K\setminus(J\sqcup J_{0})}\leq\partial K_{0}^{\prime}
as a consequence of the following: every vertex
v\in(\partial K\setminus(J\sqcup J_{0})^{[0]}
satisfies that
y^{\prime}:=\overline{v}^{\partial K}\in(\partial K\setminus(J\sqcup J_{0}))^{[R-1]}
and
\widetilde{v}
is the only
R
-cell of
K^{\prime}
containing
y^{\prime}.
Since
(K,J\sqcup J_{0})
is non-singular, every
(R-1)
-cell of
K_{0}^{\prime}
not in
\overline{\partial K\setminus(J\sqcup J_{0})}
is of the form
y^{\prime}=\overline{e}^{K}
for some
e\in K^{[1]}
such that
\left|e\cap(J\sqcup J_{0})^{[0]}\right|\leq 1.
Hence
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(y^{\prime}\right)\right|=1
if and only if
e\in K_{(J\sqcup J_{0})}
and this proves (
16
).

We can now turn back to showing that there is no
\partial K^{\prime}_{0}
-pinch on
\partial K^{\prime}_{0}.
Since we assume that
K^{\prime}\in\mathsf{C},
the relation (
16
) implies that it is sufficient to prove that
\overline{K_{J_{0}}}
is non-pinching. By Lemma
3.2
, we have that the map
(M_{J_{0}}^{K})\ni\mathcal{E}_{J_{0}}^{x}\mapsto x\in K_{J_{0}}
is an isomorphism of posets. By Proposition
1.17
,
(\overline{M_{J_{0}}^{K}},\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{M_{J_{0}}^{K}}})
is a closed cc and for all
x\in K_{J_{0}}
we have

\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{M_{J_{0}}^{K}}}(\overline{\mathcal{E}_{J_{0}}^{x}})=R-1-\operatorname{rk}_{M_{J_{0}}^{K}}(\mathcal{E}_{J_{0}}^{x})=R-\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{K}}(\overline{x})=:\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{K_{J_{0}}}}(\overline{x}).

Hence we have that
(\overline{K_{J_{0}}},\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{K_{J_{0}}}})
is a cc and
\overline{K_{J_{0}}}\cong\overline{M_{J_{0}}^{K}}.
Therefore
\overline{K_{J_{0}}}
is non-pinching since
M_{J_{0}}^{K}
is cell-connected by locality of
(K-J).

We can then conclude that the point
1
is verified for all possible
J.

The point
2
is shown using (
16
) (with
J_{0}=\emptyset
) and observing that
J^{\prime}:=\overline{\partial K\setminus J}
is an element of
\mathsf{B}^{R-1}
since it is the dual of a union of local non-pinching closed cell complexes. Finally
\overline{(K-J)}
is exactly collared since, using the notation
L=\partial K\setminus J,
the map

\{\{y,z\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ L^{[R-1]}\ni y\subset z\in K^{[R]}\}\ni\{y,z\}\longmapsto y\in L^{[R-1]}

is a bijection and we have the isomorphism
\cong
in the following derivation:

\displaystyle M_{\overline{L}}^{\widetilde{K\setminus J}\sqcup\overline{L}}

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{E}_{\overline{L}}^{\widetilde{x}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in L\}

\displaystyle=\{\{\overline{e}\in\widetilde{K}^{[1]}_{\overline{L}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \overline{e}\subset\widetilde{x}\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in L\}

\displaystyle=\{\{\{y,z\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\in L^{[R-1]},\penalty\ z\in K^{[R]}_{L}\penalty\ :\penalty\ x\subset y\subset z\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in L\}

\displaystyle\cong\{\overline{x}^{L}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in L\}=\overline{L}.

∎

4  Composition of cobordisms and causal structure

The main goal of this chapter is to define a category whose morphisms are defined from the notion of cobordism introduced in Chapter
3
and whose objects are associated with boundary components of cobordisms.
Defining a category in particular requires to define the composition of two cobordisms whenever these cobordisms correspond to two morphisms satisfying that the target object of the first is the source object of the second. The definition of an object must then include enough information about the structure of any cobordism having this object as its image or as its source in order to guarantee that it is possible to ”compose” any two such cobordisms so that the union of the cells of both cobordism can be given the structure of a cobordism.

Defining how to compose cobordisms will therefore require a precise description of the structure of cobordism around their boundary components. The fundamental tools we introduce in order to describe such structure are two types of cc-homomorphisms called reductions and collapses, introduced in Section
4.1
. A reduction between two cc allows to consider the first cc as a subdivision of the second and a collapse corresponds to the notion dual to that of a reduction when defined on closed cc. Proposition
4.11
, the main result of this first section, shows that reductions and collapses induce partial orders on the set of pure cc.

Section
4.2
introduces a number of technical assumptions defined on relative cc constituting the notion of ”uniformity”. These assumptions allow to introduce a cc called transition
J(K)
associated to a uniform relative cc
(K,J).
The main result of this second section implies that using the uniformity assumption one can relate both a boundary component
J
of a cc
K
in
\mathsf{C}
and the associated midsection
M_{J}^{K}
to the transition
J(K)
using respectively a reduction
\rho_{J}^{K}
and a collapse
\pi_{J}^{K}.

The maps
\rho_{J}^{K}
and
\pi_{J}^{K}
satisfy the additional property of being ”compatible” poset homomorphisms, a notion we define in Section
4.3
together with the notion of orthogonal poset homomorphisms. Section
4.3
also includes other notions and technical results introduced in preparation for the next section.

In Section
4.4
, we prove a number of important results related to the notion of slice, a type of cobordism having in particular all its vertices on the boundary. These results include the ”Correspondence Theorem”
4.32
, which establishes the existence of a bijection between slices and sequences of reductions and collapses called ”slice sequences”.

Section
4.5
starts by introducing the notion dual to that of slice sequences called connecting sequences. Using the Correspondence Theorem we are then able to define the union of to elements in
\mathsf{C}
intersecting on one of their boundary components in such a way that it defines a connecting sequence and this can be directly translated into a composition operation on cobordisms.

Finally, in Section
4.6
we introduce braket notation to define a category whose morphisms correspond to a specific type of cobordisms called causal cobordisms.
We then use the previous results of this chapter to explain how this category can be interpreted as having connecting sequences and slice sequences as its set of objects and morphisms corresponding to cobordisms constructed as a succession of slices. We finish by showing that the duality map defines a duality on this category and that it admits two other idempotent functors, one of which is analogous to a ”time reversal” transformation. Since the composition of these three idempotent functors also gives the identity, we named these functors ”time reversal”, ”parity transformation” and ”charge conjugation”, the latter being the functor associated to the duality map.

4.1
Reductions and collapses

In this section, we introduce two types of surjective cc-homomorphisms called reductions and collapses.
A reduction can be understood as a map
\rho:J\longrightarrow L
between two cc obeying certain conditions that allow to interpret
J
as a subdivision of the cc
L.
The barycentric subdivision
\widecheck{L}
defined in Chapter
1
will turn out to be a particular example of a subdivision. A collapse can be understood as a map
\pi:J\longrightarrow L
such that if
J,L\in\mathsf{B}
then the dual map
\overline{\pi}:\overline{J}\longrightarrow\overline{L},
defined by
\overline{\pi}(\overline{x})=\overline{\pi(x)}
for
x\in J,
is a reduction.
Reductions and collapses are nevertheless both defined using five conditions allowing these maps to be defined on arbitrary cc.

These conditions may at first seem arbitrary, we therefore illustrated in Figure
18
some motivations for introducing four of these conditions for the case of a reduction using an example in dimension 1.
The second condition
(r2)
, which is not represented on this picture, can be interpreted as follows. If all the co-faces of a given cell
x
are mapped to the same cell
y
then the image of
x
has to be
y.
This is a desirable property for a notion of subdivision that may be a consequence of the other four conditions in general, but we have not been able to prove this except for the case of cc in
\mathsf{B}
as shown in Lemma
4.9
.
A number of basic results related to these maps are included in this section, among which Lemma
4.10
shows that a reduction is indeed dual to a collapse for the case of closed cc. The more involved proof of Proposition
4.11
shows that reductions and collapses
have the important property of inducing partial orders on the set of pure cc.

In the following sections the domain
J
and image
L
of a reduction and a collapse will generally consist of elements in
\mathsf{B}.
A direct consequence of one of their five defining conditions is that
\operatorname{Rk}(J)=\operatorname{Rk}(L)
and by Lemma
4.9
only four of these conditions are needed to characterize these maps for the case of closed cc. We close Section
4.1
by discussing the notions of relative reduction and relative collapse as maps between relative cc, as introduced in Section
3.2
. The notion of relative reduction will be used to consider subdivisions of a given boundary component
J
of a cc
K\in\mathsf{C}
as a map acting trivially on the cells in
K\setminus J.

As noted later in Remark
4.6
, condition
(r3)
(resp.
(c3)
) in particular implies that if a map
\phi:J\longrightarrow K
is a reduction (resp. a collapse) then it is in particular a cc-homomorphisms, i.e.
\phi
satisfies that for all
y\in\phi(J),
there exists
x\in J^{[\operatorname{rk}_{K}(y)]}
such that
\phi(x)=y.

For the purpose of this section, let us recall the following notations from Section
1.5
for the set of cells respectively above and below a cell
x
in a poset
K:

A(x):=\{y\in K\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\subset y\},\quad B(x):=\{y\in K\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\subset x\}.

It will also be convenient to denote by
S^{[r]}
the set of
r
-cells in a subset
S
of a cc of rank
R\geq r.

Let us start by introducing the notion of reduction.

Definition 4.1

(Reduction and subdivision)
.

Let
J,K,
be cc. We say that a map
\rho:J\longrightarrow K
is a
reduction
if
\rho
is a surjective poset homomorphism satisfying the following five conditions.

(
r1
)

\left|\rho^{-1}(v)\right|=1,
for all
v\in K^{[0]}
(i.e.
\rho|^{K^{[0]}}
is injective).

(
r2
)

\rho(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))=\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))
for all
x\in J;

(
r3
)

A(\rho(x))^{[r]}\subset\rho(A(x)^{[r]}),
for all
x\in J,\penalty\ r\geq 0;

(
r4
)

if
x\in J
satisfies
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))-1
then
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\rho^{-1}(\rho(x))\right|=2;

(
r5
)

if
x\in J
satisfies
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))
then

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\rho^{-1}(y)\right|=1,\quad\forall y\in\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right).

We shall use the notation
J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho}K
if
\rho:J\longrightarrow K
is a reduction and call
J
a subdivision of
K
or equivalently say that
K
is a reduction of
J,
written
J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}K,
if there exists a reduction
\rho:J\longrightarrow K.
A cell
y\in K
will be called
subdivided by

\rho
if
\left|\rho^{-1}(y)\right|>1.
For example, a subdivision does not subdivide vertices.

Figure 18
:
In this picture, we illustrate what undesirable features the conditions
(r1)
,
(r3)
,
(r4)
and
(r5)
of a reduction are preventing, in the case of a reduction
\rho:J\longrightarrow K
between two 1-dimensional cell complexes. The vertical arrows indicate which cells in
J
are mapped to each cell in
K,
for example all the cells contained in the grey areas are mapped to an edge in
K.
More explicitly, condition
(r1)
prevents more than two vertices to be mapped to the same vertex. Condition
(r3)
ensures that the vertex
v\in J
is contained in an edge in the pre-image of the edge
e\in K
since
\rho(v)\in e.
Condition
(r4)
does not allow branchings or boundaries in the pre-image of a cell and condition
(r5)
with
x=w\in J^{[0]}
ensures in particular that the type of branching on
w
shown on the figure does not occur or that a cell is not subdivided multiple times.

Since the definition of reductions involves the greatest lower bounds
\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
and
\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))
it is worth noting that such an element always exists by the following remark.

Remark 4.2
.

As noted in Remark
1.7

\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
always exists. As concerns
\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))
we first note that since
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)\subset\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))
by
(r3)
, a lower bound
b
of
\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))
is also a lower bound of
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right).
This implies that
b\subset\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right),
which exists by Remark
1.7
. If
\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\in\{\emptyset,\{y\}\}
for some
y\in J
then
\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))
is equal to
\emptyset
or
y,
respectively, so we can assume
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2.
If
\rho(x)=\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right),
which is a lower bound of
\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))
since
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)\subset\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)),
then every lower bound of
\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))
is contained in
\rho(x),
hence
\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))=\rho(x).
If we assume that
\rho(x)\subsetneq\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)
then
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)=\{\rho(y)\}
for some
y\in K
and therefore
\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)=\rho(y).
Using again that
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)\subset\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)),
we have that
y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right).
If
y^{\prime}\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\setminus\{y\}
then we have

\rho(x)\subset\rho(y^{\prime})\notin\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right).

(17)

There are then two possibilities. If there exists
y^{\prime}\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
such that
\rho(x)=\rho(y^{\prime})
then
\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))=\rho(x)
and, if not, then (
17
) implies that
\rho(y)\subset\rho(y^{\prime})
for all
y^{\prime}\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
and therefore
\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))=\rho(y),
which concludes our argument, showing that
\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))
always exists.

Lemma 4.3
.

The barycentric subdivision of a cc
K
is a subdivision of
K,
i.e.
\widecheck{K}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}K.

Proof.

Define
\rho:\widecheck{K}\longrightarrow K
by
\rho(\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\}):=x_{r},
clearly a surjective poset homomorphism and injective on
\rho^{-1}(K^{[0]})=\{\{v\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\in K^{[0]}\}.

Condition
(r2)
follows from the definition of
\rho,
which, for the case
\bigtriangledown\left(x_{r}\right)\neq\emptyset,
implies that

\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\}\right))=\rho(\{\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\subsetneq y\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x_{r}\right)\})=\bigtriangledown\left(x_{r}\right),

hence we get

\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\}\right))=\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x_{r}\right)=\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\subset\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x_{r}\right)\})\right).

The case
\bigtriangledown\left(x_{r}\right)=\emptyset
holds trivially.

Let
\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\}\in\widecheck{K}
and
y\in K
such that
\rho(\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\})\subset y
then by
iii)
there exists a cell of
\widecheck{K}
of the form
\sigma=\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\subset\dots\subset y\}
such that
\operatorname{rk}_{\widecheck{K}}(\sigma)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(y)
and
\rho(\sigma)=y
and therefore
\rho
also satisfies condition
(r3)
of a reduction.
For condition
(r4)
, if
r:=\operatorname{rk}_{\widecheck{K}}(\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\})=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x_{r})+1
then there exists a unique
0\leq i\leq r
such that
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x_{i})=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x_{i+1})-2
and therefore

\displaystyle\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\}\right)\cap\rho^{-1}(x_{r})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\{\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{i}\subsetneq y\subsetneq x_{i+1}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x_{i}\right)\cap\triangle\left(x_{i+1}\right)\}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x_{i}\right)\cap\triangle\left(x_{i+1}\right)\right|=2.

Finally, for condition
(r5)
, if
r:=\operatorname{rk}_{\widecheck{K}}(\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\})=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x_{r})
then
\operatorname{rk}(x_{i})=i
for all
0\leq i\leq r.
Hence for all
x_{r+1}\in\bigtriangledown\left(x_{r}\right),
we have

\displaystyle\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r}\}\right)\cap\rho^{-1}(x_{r+1})\right|=\left|\{\{x_{0}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq x_{r+1}\}\}\right|=1.

∎

Let us now turn to the notion dual to that of a reduction for the case of closed cc. Since reductions are defined on general cc we decided to formulate the definition of collapse also using five conditions and to prove in Lemma
4.10
that these are indeed dual notions when defined on elements in
\mathsf{B}.

Thus, in analogy with the interpretation of
(r4)
in Figure
18
, the dual condition
(c4)
of a collapse states that if a
r
-cell collapses into a cell of rank
r-1
then it must be the result of an identification of two of its faces.

Definition 4.4

(Collapse and expansion)
.

Let
J,K,
be cc. We say that
\pi:J\longrightarrow K
is a
collapse
if
\pi
is a surjective poset homomorphism and

(
c1
)

\left|\pi^{-1}(z)\right|=1,
for all maximal cell
z\in K;

(
c2
)

\pi(x)=\bigvee\pi(\triangle\left(x\right))
for all
x\in J
such that
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)\geq 1;

(
c3
)

B(\pi(x))^{[r]}\subset\pi(B(x)^{[r]})
for all
x\in J,\penalty\ r\geq 0;

(
c4
)

if
x\in J
satisfies
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\pi(x))+1
then
\left|\triangle\left(x\right)\cap\pi^{-1}(\pi(x))\right|=2;

(
c5
)

if
x\in J
satisfies
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\pi(x))
then

\left|\triangle\left(x\right)\cap\pi^{-1}(y)\right|=1,\quad\forall y\in\triangle\left(\pi(x)\right).

We will use the notation
J\vdash_{\pi}K
if
\pi:J\longrightarrow K
is a collapse and say that

J
is an expansion of
K
or equivalently that
K
is a collapse of
J,
written
J\vdash K,
if there exists a collapse
\pi:J\longrightarrow K.

Since the definition of collapse involves a least upper bound
\bigvee\pi(\triangle\left(x\right)),
we will also justify that condition
(c2)
makes sense.

Remark 4.5
.

To see that if
\operatorname{rk}(x)_{J}\geq 1
then
\bigvee\pi(\triangle\left(x\right))
exists in general, we first note that if
\bigtriangledown\left(\pi(x)\right)=\emptyset
then
\pi(x)
is a vertex and therefore
\pi(y)=\pi(x)
for all
y\in\triangle\left(x\right)
implying that
\bigvee\pi(\triangle\left(x\right))=\pi(x).
On the other hand if
\triangle\left(\pi(x)\right)\neq\emptyset
then
\left|\triangle\left(\pi(x)\right)\right|\geq 2
and
\bigvee\triangle\left(\pi(x)\right)=\pi(x).
Moreover, the fact that
\triangle\left(\pi(x)\right)\subset\pi(\triangle\left(x\right))
by condition
(c3)
implies that
\bigvee\pi(\triangle\left(x\right))\subset b
for all upper bounds
b
of
\pi(\triangle\left(x\right)).
Hence we have that
\pi(x)
is an upper bound for
\pi(\triangle\left(x\right))
which is contained in every upper bound of
\pi(\triangle\left(x\right)),
in other words,
\bigvee\pi(\triangle\left(x\right))=\pi(x).

Let us point out that a cc
J
can both be a reduction and a collapse of a given cc
K,
as illustrated in Figure
19
.

The next remark includes several observations to which we will refer several times in the next proofs.

Remark 4.6
.

A consequence of condition
(r3)
is that
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))\geq\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x),
for a reduction
\rho:J\longrightarrow K
and
(c3)
implies that
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\pi(x))\leq\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)
for a collapse
\pi:J\longrightarrow K.
This can be seen for example for the case of a reduction
\rho
by considering the instance
r=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))
in condition
(r3)
according to which

\{\rho(x)\}=A(\rho(x))^{[r]}\subset\rho(A(x)^{[r]}).

This implies
A(x)^{[r]}\neq\emptyset
and therefore
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)\leq r=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))
for all
x\in J.
The case of a collapse is similar. This argument also shows that conditions
(r3)
and
(c3)
in particular imply that reductions and collapses are cc-homomorphisms.

Figure 19
:
Example of cell complexes
J
and
K
such that
J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}K
and
J\vdash K.

Remark 4.7
.

If
J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}K
or
J\vdash K
and
J
is pure then
\operatorname{Rk}(J)=\operatorname{Rk}(K).
Indeed, for the case
J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}K,

\rho=\rho_{J}^{K}
is in particular a cc-homomorphism hence there exists a
\operatorname{Rk}(K)
-cell in
\rho^{-1}(z),
for all
z\in K^{[\operatorname{Rk}(K)]}
and therefore
\operatorname{Rk}(J)\geq\operatorname{Rk}(K).
Remark
4.6
also implies
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)\leq\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))
for all
x\in J
and we get
\operatorname{Rk}(J)=\operatorname{Rk}(K).

For the case
J\vdash K
we need to assume that
J
is pure. Figure
20
illustrates why this hypothesis is necessary. By Remark
4.6

\pi=\pi_{J}^{K}
is a cc-homomorphism and we also directly get the inequality
\operatorname{Rk}(J)\geq\operatorname{Rk}(K).
Since
\pi^{-1}(z)=\{w\}\subset J^{[\operatorname{rk}_{K}(z)]}
for any maximal cell
z\in K
then
w
is a maximal cell of
J
as otherwise if
w\subsetneq z^{\prime}
then
z=\pi(w)\subset\pi(z^{\prime})
and therefore
\pi(z^{\prime})=z
by maximality of
z,
which is a contradiction. Since
J
is pure,
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(w)=\operatorname{Rk}(J),
and the previous argument implies in particular that
\operatorname{Rk}(J)\leq\operatorname{Rk}(K)
and therefore
\operatorname{Rk}(J)=\operatorname{Rk}(K).

Figure 20
:
Example of a collapse between cell complexes of different ranks. By Remark
4.7
this requires the domain of the collapse
J
to be not pure. The triangle in
J
and all the cells contained in it are mapped to the lower vertex.

We also mention the following technical result that will be used in the proof of Lemma
4.9
. It implies in particular that if there exists a reduction from a pure cc
J
to
K\in\mathsf{B}
then
\partial J=\emptyset.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
J
be a pure cc and let
K\in\mathsf{B}.
If
\rho:J\longrightarrow K
is a reduction then

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2
for all non-maximal cell
x
in
J.

Proof.

To prove this claim, we assume by contradiction that there exists
x\in J
such that
\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)=\{y\}.
Since
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)\subset\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))
by condition
(r3)
, we have that
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)\right|\leq 1,
hence
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)=\emptyset,
since
K\in\mathsf{B}.
The inclusion
\rho(x)\subset\rho(y)
then implies that
\rho(x)=\rho(y).
We also have that
y
is a maximal cell, since if there exists
z\in J
such that
x\subsetneq y\subsetneq z
then the diamond property for
J
implies that
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2
which is a contradiction. Since both
\rho(x)=\rho(y)
and
y
are maximal cells in respectively
K
and
J
, we have that
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(y)=R=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))
by Remark
4.7
and therefore

\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(y)-1=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))-1.

Condition
(r4)
then implies that

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\rho^{-1}(\rho(x))\right|=2,

which is also a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
J
be a pure cc and let
K\in\mathsf{B}.
If
\rho:J\longrightarrow K
is a reduction then condition
(r3)
implies condition
(r2)
. Similarly, if
\pi:J\longrightarrow K
is a collapse then condition
(c3)
implies condition
(c2)
.

Proof.

Let us first prove the statement for reductions.
Since
K\in B
then either
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)\right|\geq 2
and
\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)=\rho(x)
or
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)=\emptyset.
The same is also true for the cells in
J,
by Lemma
4.8
.

Moreover, we have the two following properties. If
\bigwedge S,\bigwedge\rho(S)
exists then since
\rho
is a poset homomorphism we have
\rho(\bigwedge S)\subset\bigwedge\rho(S)
and if
S,T\in\mathcal{P}\left(K^{[0]}\right)
such that
S\subset T
and
\bigwedge S,\bigwedge T
exist and are non-empty then
\bigwedge T\subset\bigwedge S.
Using these properties and the fact that condition
(r3)
implies that
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)\subset\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)),
for the case
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)\right|\geq 2
we have that
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2
and

\rho(x)=\rho(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))\subset\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))\subset\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)=\rho(x),

hence
\rho(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))=\rho(x)=\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)).
If
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)=\emptyset
and
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2
then
\rho(y)=\rho(x)
for all
y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
by maximality of
\rho(x)
and therefore we can also conclude that

\rho(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))=\rho(x)=\bigwedge\rho(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))

and this concludes the proof for the case of reductions.

If
\pi:J\longrightarrow K
is a collapse then the dual statement of Lemma
4.8
is clearly true, namely that every cell which is not a vertex has more than two faces. The rest of the proof then follows using the dual version of the arguments above.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
K,L\in\mathsf{B}.
Then
L\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho}K
if and only if
\overline{L}\vdash_{\overline{\rho}}\overline{K},
where
\overline{\rho}:\overline{J}\longrightarrow\overline{K}
is defined by

x\longmapsto\overline{\rho}(x):=\overline{\rho(x)}.

Proof.

By Remark
4.7
,
\operatorname{Rk}(K)=\operatorname{Rk}(L)=:R.
Let
\rho:L\longrightarrow K
be a reduction. Then map
\overline{\rho}
is well defined by Lemma
1.16
, is clearly surjective and is a poset homomorphism since if
\overline{x}\subset\overline{y}
then
y\subset x
and

\overline{\rho}(\overline{x})=\overline{\rho(x)}\subset\overline{\rho(y)}=\overline{\rho}(\overline{y}).

First, if
\overline{y}\in\overline{K}^{[R]}
then
y=v\in K^{[0]}
and
\left|\rho^{-1}(v)\right|=1,
therefore

\left|(\overline{\rho})^{-1}(\overline{y})\right|=\left|\overline{\rho^{-1}(v)}\right|=1,

which shows that
\overline{\rho}
satisfies condition
(c1)
of a collapse (we recall that according to our notation we have
\overline{S}=\{\overline{s}\penalty\ |\penalty\ s\in S\}
for a subset
S\subset K
).

Let
y\in L^{[r]}.
We have the following relation

\displaystyle(\overline{\rho})^{-1}(\overline{y})

\displaystyle=\{\overline{x}\in\overline{K}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \overline{\rho}(\overline{x})=\overline{\rho(x)}=\overline{y}\}

\displaystyle=\{\overline{x}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in\rho^{-1}(y)\}=:\overline{\rho^{-1}(y)}.

Therefore, if
\overline{y}\subset\overline{\rho}(\overline{x})=\overline{\rho(x)}
then
\rho(x)\subset y,
hence there exists an
r
-cell
w\in\rho^{-1}(y)
such that
x\subset w.
This means that
\overline{w}\in\overline{\rho}^{-1}(\overline{y})
has rank
R-r=\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{L}}(\overline{y})
and
\overline{w}\subset\overline{x}
and
\overline{\rho}
satisfies condition
(c3)
.

The map
\overline{\rho}
satisfies condition
(c4)
since if
\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{L}}(\overline{y})=\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{K}}(\overline{\rho}(\overline{y}))+1
then
\operatorname{rk}_{L}(y)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(y))-1
and we have the following relation:

\left|\triangle\left(\overline{y}\right)\cap\overline{\rho}^{-1}(\overline{\rho}(\overline{y})\right|=\left|\overline{\bigtriangledown\left(y\right)}\cap\overline{\rho^{-1}(\rho(y))}\right|=\left|\overline{\bigtriangledown\left(y\right)\cap\rho^{-1}(\rho(y))}\right|=\left|\bigtriangledown\left(y\right)\cap\rho^{-1}(\rho(y))\right|=2,

by condition
(r4)
of a reduction.

Finally, condition
(c5)
is checked as follows. If
\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{J}}(\overline{x})=\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{K}}(\overline{\rho}(\overline{x}))
then
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))
and if
\overline{y}\in\triangle\left(\overline{\rho}(\overline{x})\right)
then
y\in\bigtriangledown\left(\rho(x)\right)
and

\left|\triangle\left(\overline{y}\right)\cap\overline{\rho}^{-1}(\overline{y})\right|=\left|\overline{\bigtriangledown\left(y\right)\cap\rho^{-1}(y)}\right|=1.

By Lemma
4.9
condition
(c2)
is automatically satisfied and we have therefore proven that
\overline{\rho}
is a collapse.

The reverse implication is proven using the same arguments while reversing the inclusion relations between cells and replacing vertices with maximal cells (i.e.
R
-cells) and vice versa.
∎

The following proposition is an important property of reductions and collapses. It will be used in particular in Proposition
4.31
where we show that subdivisions of a boundary component defines an operation on elements in
\mathsf{C}.
A sizeable part of the proof of the next result is about proving that condition
(r2)
is transitive and by Lemma
4.9
this is only necessary for the case of pure cc with non-empty boundary.

Proposition 4.11
.

\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}
and
\vdash
are partial orders on the set of pure cc, up to cc-isomorphism.

Proof.

We will show this result for
\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}.
The proof applies also to
\vdash
using the same arguments while reversing inclusion relations between cells and replacing vertices with maximal cells. Also, the arguments in the following proof involving condition
(r2)
are simplier for the case of collapses, since condition
(c2)
can be expressed without involving a least upper bound, as noted in Remark
4.5
.

The relation
\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}
is clearly reflexive. The main part of this proof focuses on showing that
\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}
is transitive. For this, we assume that
J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{1}}K,

K\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{2}}L
and check the five conditions for
\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}.

Condition
(r1)
for
\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}
is checked easily: if
y\in L^{[0]}
we have
\left|\rho_{2}^{-1}(y)\right|=1
since
\rho_{2}
is a reduction, i.e.
v=\rho_{2}^{-1}(y)\in K^{[0]}
and we have
\left|\rho_{1}^{-1}(v)\right|=1
since
\rho_{1}
is a reduction, hence
\left|(\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1})^{-1}(y)\right|=1.

Proving condition
(r3)
for
\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}
is relatively direct as well. If
y\in L^{[r]},x\in J
are such that
\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}(x)\subset y
then since
\rho_{2}
is a reduction there exists
w^{\prime}\in\rho_{2}^{-1}(y)^{[r]}
such that
\rho_{1}(x)\subset w^{\prime}.
Also, since
\rho_{1}
is a reduction there exists
w\in\rho^{-1}(w^{\prime})^{[r]}\subset(\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1})^{-1}(y)^{[r]}
such that
x\subset w
and this proves that

A(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)))^{[r]}\subset\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(A(x)^{[r]})).

The transitivity of the second condition is more technical. We first note that condition
(r2)
for
\rho_{1}
implies the following equality

\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)))=\rho_{2}(\bigwedge\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))).

(18)

In addition, since
\rho_{2}
is a poset homomorphism, we have

\rho_{2}(\bigwedge\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))\subset\bigwedge\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))),

(19)

hence it remains to show the reversed inclusion. By condition
(r3)
we have in particular that

\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\subset\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))

(20)

which impies that

\bigwedge\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)))\subset\bigwedge\rho_{2}(\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right))=\rho_{2}(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)),

(21)

where the equality is due to condition
(r2)
for
\rho_{2}.

The inclusion (
20
) implies that
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\right|\leq\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|.
Therefore we can split the remainder of the proof of the transitivity of
(r2)
in four cases:

•

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))\right|\geq\left|\rho_{1}(x))\right|\geq 2,

•

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\right|=\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|=1,

•

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|>0,\penalty\ \left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\right|=0
(uses the assumption of
J
pure!),

•

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2,\penalty\ \left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\right|=1.

In the first case, when
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\right|\geq 2
and
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2,
the Lemma
1.6
directly gives

\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)=\rho_{1}(x)=\rho_{1}(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))

and we get the desired equality using (
18
), (
19
) and (
21
).

In the second case we get
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|=\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)=1,
hence we can pick
y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
to be the unique co-face of
x.
Then the inclusion (
20
) implies that
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\subset\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))=\{y\}.
Therefore we obtain
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)=\{\rho_{1}(y)\}
and

\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)=\rho_{1}(y)=\rho_{1}(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)).

We can then also concludes the argument using (
18
), (
19
) and (
21
).

Third, if
\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\neq\emptyset
and
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)=\emptyset,
an argument similar as in the proof of Lemma
4.8
shows that
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2,
by the purity of
J.
We also have that
\rho(y)=\rho(x)
for all
y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right),
by maximality of
\rho(x).
Therefore we get

\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)))=\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x))=\bigwedge\{\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x))\}=\bigwedge\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))),

which corresponds exactly to condition
(r2)
for
\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}.

For the last case we have
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\right|\geq 2
and
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\right|=1
and condition
(r2)
for
\rho_{1}
implies that

\rho_{2}(\bigwedge\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)))=\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)))=\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)).

This implies that there exists
y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
such that
\rho_{1}(y)=\rho_{1}(x).
Therefore

\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(\bigwedge\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)))=\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x))\subset\bigwedge\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))),

and we can conclude using the inclusion (
19
).

We will show condition
(r5)
for
\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}
and finish the proof of transitivity with condition
(r4)
below.
If we assume that
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{L}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x))),
it implies that
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho_{1}(x))=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)
since the rank is increasing under subdivision as observed in Remark
4.7
. Hence conditions
(r5)
for
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
imply respectively the two following conditions:

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\rho_{1}^{-1}(y)\right|=1\quad\text{for all }y\in\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right);

(22)

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\cap\rho_{2}^{-1}(y)\right|=1\quad\text{for all }y\in\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x))\right).

(23)

Using that

f(A\cap f^{-1}(B))=f(A)\cap B

(24)

for a function
f:S\longrightarrow T
between two sets
S,T
and
A\subset S,\penalty\ B\subset T
and the fact that condition
(r3)
implies
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\subset\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)),
we get the following inequality for all
y\in\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x))\right):

\displaystyle\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap(\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1})^{-1}(y)\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap(\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1})^{-1}(y))\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\rho_{1}(\bigtriangledown\left(x\right))\cap\rho_{2}^{-1}(y)\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\rho_{2}^{-1}(y)\right|=1,

where the last equality is due to condition (
22
).

Moreover if we suppose that for some
y\in\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x))\right)
there are two distinct elements

w,w^{\prime}\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap(\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1})^{-1}(y)

then since
\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\subset\rho_{2}(\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right))
by condition
(r3)
, there exists
y^{\prime}\in\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\cap\rho_{2}^{-1}(y)
and this element is unique by condition (
23
). This implies that

\{w,w^{\prime}\}\subset\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\rho_{1}^{-1}(y^{\prime})\right|

which contradicts condition (
22
) and therefore we have proven
(r5)
for
\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}.

Finally, we show condition
(r4)
for
\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}
as follows. If
x\in J
satisfies

\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{L}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)))-1

then we can have the two following possible cases.

The first possible case is

rk_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho_{1}(x)),\quad\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho_{1}(x))=\operatorname{rk}_{L}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)))-1,

which respectively implies condition (
22
) as well as condition
(r4)
for
\rho_{2},
i.e.

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\cap\rho_{2}^{-1}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)))\right|=2.

(25)

Every element
w\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap(\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1})^{-1}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)))
satisfies
\rho(x)\subset\rho(w)
and
\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(w))=\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)),
hence

r+1=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(w)\leq\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho_{1}(w))\leq\operatorname{rk}_{L}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(w)))=\operatorname{rk}_{L}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)))=r+1.

Therefore
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho_{1}(w))=r+1
and
\rho_{1}(w)\in\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right).
Condition (
22
) implies that
\rho_{1}^{-1}(\rho_{1}(w))=\{w\}
for every such
w
and using (
25
) we get

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap(\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1})^{-1}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)))\right|=\left|\bigtriangledown\left(\rho_{1}(x)\right)\cap\rho_{2}^{-1}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)))\right|=2.

The second possible case is

rk_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho_{1}(x))-1,\quad\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho_{1}(x))=\operatorname{rk}_{L}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x))).

This case implies condition
(r4)
for
\rho_{1}:

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\rho_{1}^{-1}(\rho_{1}(x))\right|=2.

(26)

If
w\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap(\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1})^{-1}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)))
then
\rho(x)\subset\rho(w)
and
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(w))=r+1=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho_{1}(x))
by the same argument as above, therefore
\rho_{1}(w)=\rho_{1}(x).

This implies that

\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap(\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1})^{-1}(\rho_{2}(\rho_{1}(x)))=\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\rho_{1}^{-1}(\rho_{1}(x))

and we get the desired result by (
26
). This concludes the proof that
\rho_{2}\circ\rho_{1}
is a reduction and therefore
\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}
is transitive.

Finally, the relation
\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}
is antisymmetric by the following argument. Suppose
J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\phi}K
and
K\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\gamma}J
(which by Remark
4.7
implies
\operatorname{Rk}(J)=\operatorname{Rk}(K)
) and define
\phi_{r}:=\phi|^{K^{[r]}}
and
\gamma_{r}:=\gamma|^{J^{[r]}}.
We will prove by induction on
r
that
\phi_{r}:J^{[r]}\longrightarrow K^{[r]}
is a bijection for all
0\leq r\leq R
implying that
\phi
is an isomorphism by Lemma
1.2
. For the case
r=0
we know that
\phi_{0}
and
\gamma_{0}
are surjective and this implies that
\left|J^{[0]}\right|=\left|K^{[0]}\right|.
Hence
\phi_{0}
and
\gamma_{0}
are also injective and therefore bijective. Suppose that
\phi_{s}:J^{[s]}\longrightarrow K^{[s]}
and
\gamma_{s}:K^{[s]}\longrightarrow J^{[s]}
are bijective for all
s=0,1\dots r-1.
As noted in Remark
4.6
since
\phi
is a reduction we have
\phi^{-1}(K^{[r]})\subset J^{(r)}
and
J^{(r-1)}=\phi^{-1}(K^{(r-1)}).
Hence
\phi^{-1}(K^{[r]})\subset J^{[r]}
and
\left|K^{[r]}\right|\leq\left|J^{[r]}\right|.
By the same argument for
\gamma
we also get the reverse inequality and therefore
\phi_{r}:J^{[r]}\longrightarrow K^{[r]}
and
\gamma_{r}:K^{[r]}\longrightarrow J^{[r]}
are indeed bijections.
∎

The proof of antisymmetry in Proposition
4.11
only requires condition
(r3)
of reductions (and the same is true for collapse). As a consequence, the third condition together with the surjective poset homomorphism property are sufficient to define a partial order on the set of cc.

Before giving the definitions of relative reduction and collapse, we recall that by Definition
3.5
a relative cc-homomorphism or a homomorphism of
J
relative to
K
is a homomorphism
\phi:(K,J)\longrightarrow(H,L)
of relative cc (i.e. a cc-homomorphism
\phi:K\longrightarrow H
such that
\phi(J)=L
) such that
\phi|_{K\setminus J}:K\setminus J\longrightarrow H\setminus L
is a poset isomorphism.

Definition 4.12

(Relative reduction and collapse)
.

Let
(K,J),(H,L)
be relative cc. A
relative reduction (respectively a relative collapse)
or a
reduction (respectively collapse) of
J
relative to
K
is a cc-homomorphism
\phi:(K,J)\longrightarrow(H,L)
of
J
relative to
K
such that
\phi:K\longrightarrow H
is a reduction (respectively a collapse). We will also write
(K,J)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}(H,L)
and say that
(K,J)
is
a subdivision of
L
relative to
H
if there exists a reduction
\rho:(K,J)\longrightarrow(H,L)
of
J
relative to
K.
Similarly we will write
(K,J)\vdash(H,L)
and say that
(K,J)
is
an expansion of
L
relative to
H
if there exists a collapse
\pi:(K,J)\longrightarrow(H,L)
of
J
relative to
K.

Clearly Definition
4.12
implies in particular that a relative cc-homomorphism
\phi:(K,J)\longrightarrow(H,L)
is a relative reduction (respectively a relative collapse) if and only if
\phi|_{J}:J\longrightarrow L
is a reduction (respectively a collapse). By abuse of notation, we will sometimes also use the notation
\phi
to designate
\phi|_{J}
when
\phi:(K,J)\longrightarrow(H,L)
is a relative homomorphism.

It will be convenient to use the following notation on a cc
K
in order to specify how it is related to a relative cc-homomorphism.

Definition 4.13

(
K_{\phi}
and
K^{\phi}
)
.

Let
\phi:J\longrightarrow L
be a surjective cc-homomorphism and let
H
be a cc such that
L\leq H.
If there exists a cc
K\geq J
and a relative cc-homomorphism
\phi^{H}:(K,J)\longrightarrow(H,L)
such that
\phi^{H}|_{J}=\phi
then
K
is uniquely determined by
H
and
\phi:J\longrightarrow L
up to cc-isomorphism, we will therefore use the notation
K=H^{\phi}.

Similarly, let
\phi:J\longrightarrow L
be a cc-homomorphism and let
K
be a cc such that
J\leq K.
If there exists a cc
H\geq L
and a relative cc-homomorphism
\phi_{K}:(K,J)\longrightarrow(H,L)
such that
\phi_{K}|_{J}=\phi
then
H
is uniquely determined by
K
and
\phi:J\longrightarrow L
up to cc-isomorphism, we will therefore use the notation
H=K_{\phi}.

The uniqueness in Definition
4.13
follows from the observation that, say in the first case, whenever we take a relative cc
(K^{\prime},J)
such that there exists a relative cc-homomorsphism
\phi_{K^{\prime}}:(K^{\prime},J)\longrightarrow(H,L)
then by Definition
3.5
the map
\psi:K\longrightarrow K^{\prime}
defined by
\psi|_{J}=\operatorname{id}_{J}
and
\psi|_{K\setminus J}=\phi_{K^{\prime}}^{-1}|_{H\setminus L}\circ\phi_{K}|_{K\setminus J},
is a poset isomomorphism such that
\psi^{-1}
is also a poset isomomorphism, hence
\psi
is a cc-isomorphism by Lemma
1.2
. Moreover the isomorphism class of
K
does not depend on the choice of the realtive cc-homomorphisms
\phi_{K}
and
\phi_{K^{\prime}}.

Let us make the following remark about the previous definitions considering the example of a reduction
\rho:J\longrightarrow L.
In general it is wrong that given a relative cc
(H,L)
there exists a cc
K
such that
(K,J)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho}(H,L)
is a relative reduction or that given a relative cc
(K,J)
there exists a cc
H
such that
(K,J)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho}(H,L)
is a relative reduction.

One reason that this is wrong for the latter case is that it is possible for the reduction
\rho
to reduce two
r
-cells
x_{1},x_{2}
in
J
each sharing a face
y_{1},y_{2}\in J^{[r-1]}
with a
r
-cell in
y\in K_{J}
into one cell
x=\rho(x_{1})=\rho(x_{2}),
hence
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\subset x\cap y,
violating the intersection Axiom
ii)
for
H.

Similarly, a reason for the first case is that a relative subdivision of
(H,L)
can subdivide a cell
x
of
L
contained in a cell of
y\in H_{L}
only if every cell
w
of
L
strictly containing
x
is also subdivided in such a way that any intersection of an element in
\rho^{-1}(y)
with an element in
\rho^{-1}(w)
contains at most one cell, which is not the case in general.

We close this section with a remark about the interpretation of the notion of relative cc- automorphisms in our framework.

Remark 4.14
.

Consider two cc
J,L
such that for example
J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}L.
If
J,L
satisfy the stronger condition that there exist
K,H
such that
(K,J)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}(H,L)
then this introduces some freedom in the choice of
K
and
H
in the following sense. If we take any relative cc-automorphism
\alpha:(K,J)\longrightarrow(K_{\alpha},J)
(i.e. a relative cc-homomorphism
\alpha:(K,J)\longrightarrow(K_{\alpha},J)
such that
\alpha|_{J}:J\longrightarrow J
is an cc-automorphism) and any relative cc-automorphism
\beta:(H,L)\longrightarrow(H_{\beta},L)
then
(K_{\alpha},J)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}(H_{\beta},L).
Similarly if
(K,J)\vdash(H,L)
then
(K_{\alpha},J)\vdash(H_{\beta},L).
But in general
K\not\cong K_{\alpha}.
This freedom in the choice of
K
and
H
when obtaining a relative reduction could be interpreted in the context of cobordisms as a discrete notion of gauge symmetry.

4.2
Transition and uniform relative cc

An important motivation for introducing reductions and collapses is to establish a relation between a boundary component
J
of a cc
K\in\mathsf{C}
and the associated midsection
M_{J}^{K}.
In order to do that, we need to add a number of additional assumptions on the relative cc
(K,J)
that will go under the name of ”uniformity”. These include several rather technical assumptions related to a poset called ”transition”, written
J(K),
constructed from the cells in the collar
K_{J}.
The uniformity of
(K,J)
in particular implies that
J(K)\in\mathsf{B}
and in Proposition
4.31
we show that in the case
K\in\mathsf{C}^{R+1}
and
J\leq\partial K,

J\in\mathsf{B}^{R}
the assumption of uniformity also implies that there exist a reduction
\rho_{J}^{K}
and a collapse
\pi_{J}^{K}
such that

J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{J}^{K}}J(K)\dashv_{\pi_{J}^{K}}M_{J}^{K}.

In view of the definition of relative reduction and collapse given at the end of the last section, it is natural to ask if there exists a ”coarsest” relative reduction of a relative cell complex
(K,J)
i.e. some maximal cell complex
J(K)
with respect to the partial order
\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}
such that
(K,J)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}(K^{\prime},J(K))
for some
K^{\prime}.
In order to keep aside any question related to the existence of
J(K)
when referring to it, it will be more convenient to use an explicit definition of
J(K)
simply as a poset that one can associate to a relative cell complex.

Definition 4.15

(Transition)
.

Let
(K,J)
be a relative cc. For
x\in K_{J},
we define the
reduced cell of
x
to be

x_{J}:=\{e\cap J^{[0]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\in\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}\}.

We then define the
transition of
(K,J)
to be the poset

J(K):=\{x_{J}\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\in K_{J}\}.

Figure 21
:
This picture represents an example of a reduced cell
x_{J}
made of four vertices and four edges (thicker lines) where
x
is a 3-cell with 9 faces including
C_{1},C_{2}
and
C_{3}.
The cell
x
can be seen as the only 3-cell represented from the collar
K_{J}
of a 3-cc
K
where
J
is a 2-cc made of all the cells whose vertices are drawn on the grey plane. For example
J\cap x
contains the three 2-cells
C_{1},C_{2}
and
C_{3}
and their neighbouring 2-cells in
J
contain the ”unending” edges represented using the dots
\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot\cdot.

An example of a reduced cell is given in Figure
21
. We would like to also associate a rank function to
J(K),
but it is not clear in general how to associate a rank to a reduced cell as it can be constructed from different cells in
K_{J}.
Therefore we introduce the following notion of pure relative cc.

Let us first introduce some notations that will make the following formulations more convenient. If
A\subset J^{[0]},
we can consider the set

K_{J}(A):=\{y\in K_{J}\penalty\ |\penalty\ A\subset y\}.

We also define
K^{-}_{J}(A)
to be the set of minimal elements in
K_{J}(A).

Definition 4.16

(Pure relative cc and relative rank)
.

A relative cc
(K,J)
is said to be
pure
if
J\cap x
is pure for all
x\in K_{J}
and, dually, for all
x\in J,
all cells in
K^{-}_{J}(x)
have equal rank
r(x).
It is clear that in this case
r(x)\geq 1
for all
x\in J
and we define
\operatorname{rk}_{J}^{K}(x):=r(x)-1
to be the
relative rank of
x\in J
in
K.

This definition in particular implies that
\operatorname{rk}_{J}^{K}(x)\geq\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)
for all
x\in J.

The two conditions of relative purity from Definition
4.16
are always satisfied for example in the case of simplicial complexes, but there are instances of cc that do not satisfy either of the two conditions. An example of a relative cc
(K,J)
that does not satisfy the first condition is given in Figure
23
, in which there exists
x\in K_{J}
such that
J\cap x
is not pure.
Figure
23
provides an example of relative cc
(K,J)
that does not satisfy the second ”dual condition” of pure relative cc, i.e. such that there exists
x\in K_{J}
(the central vertex) such that
K_{J}^{-}[x]
contains two elements with different ranks (the thicker edge and the wavy triangle). One can in fact relate the example of Figure
23
to the example of the previous Figure
23
by using the duality map and defining a subdivision using the 2-cells
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}.

Figure 22
:
An example of a 3-cell
x\in K_{J}
of a relative cc that does not satisfy the first condition for pure relative cc since
J\cap x,
whose edges are represented with thicker lines, is not pure.

Figure 23
:
An example of a cell
x\in J
that does not satisfy the second condition of purity for the relative cc
(K,J),
i.e.
x
is the central vertex (black dot) and is both included in an edge (thicker line) and a 2-cell (wavy triangle) in
K_{J}.
There are five 3-cells in this picture: four tetrahedra and one 3-cell below the wavy triangle having seven vertices.

Since we would like to manipulate reduced cells as the cells of the transition, we need to ensure that these contain ”enough vertices”. Indeed if
x\in K_{J}
then
x_{J}\subset x\cap J^{[0]},
but in general there can be vertices in
x\cap J^{[0]}
not contained in an edge in
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}.

We therefore add an additional condition to our framework, namely that the cells of a relative cc
(K,J)
satisfy what we will refer to as the
uniformity condition
:

J\cap x\subset J\cap y\quad\text{if and only if}\quad x_{J}\subset y_{J},\quad\forall x,y\in K_{J}.

(
U
)

We shall now see what the assumption of purity and condition (
U
) imply for the cells of
J(K).
For this, we also introduce the following set associated to a cell
x\in J(K):

K_{J}[x]=\{y\in K_{J}\penalty\ |\penalty\ y_{J}=x\}

and denote by
K_{J}^{-}[x]
the set of minimal elements in
K_{J}[x].

The next lemma provides us with more information about the cells in
K_{J}^{-}[x]
for
x\in J(K).
In particular it implies that if
y\in K_{J}
contains
x\in J(K)
then there exists an element
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x]
such that
\operatorname{rk}_{J}^{K}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x^{\prime})-1
and
x\subset x^{\prime}\subset y.
This property is reminiscent of condition
(c3)
from the definition of a collapse and it will indeed allow us to show that the map
\pi_{J}^{K}
we will define in Proposition
4.21
is a collapse.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
(K,J)
be a pure relative cc such that (
U
) is satisfied and let
x\in J(K).
Then for all
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x]
and all maximal cells
w\in J\cap x^{\prime}
we have

\operatorname{rk}_{J}^{K}(x):=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x^{\prime})-1=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(w).

Moreover, for all
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x]
we have that
K_{J}(x)=K_{J}(x^{\prime}\cap J^{[0]})
and

K_{J}^{-}(x)=K_{J}^{-}[x]=K_{J}^{-}(x^{\prime}\cap J^{[0]}),

which implies that for any given
y^{\prime}\in K_{J}(x)
there exists
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x]
such that
x^{\prime}\subset y^{\prime}.

Proof.

First, it is clear that
K_{J}[x]\subset K_{J}(x).
An element
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x]
satisfies that
x\subset x^{\prime},
which implies the inclusion

K_{J}(x)\supset K_{J}(x^{\prime}\cap J^{[0]}).

The reverse inclusion is also valid by the following argument. Note that an element
y^{\prime}\in K_{J}(x)
satisfies

x^{\prime}_{J}=x\subset y^{\prime}_{J}

and (
U
) applied to
(K,J)
gives

x^{\prime}\cap J^{[0]}\subset y^{\prime}\cap J^{[0]},

which in particular implies that
y\in K_{J}(x^{\prime}\cap J^{[0]}).
Therefore we indeed have
K_{J}(x)=K_{J}(x^{\prime}\cap J^{[0]}).

Moreover, a minimal element in
K_{J}[x]
is also a minimal element in
K_{J}(x).
This is a consequence of the fact that if
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}[x]
and
y^{\prime}\in K_{J}(x)
satisfy
y^{\prime}\subset x^{\prime}
then

x\subset y^{\prime}_{J}\subset x^{\prime}_{J}=x

and therefore
y^{\prime}_{J}=x
i.e.
y\in K_{J}[x].

As a consequence of the results above, the purity of
(K,J)
implies that for all
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x]
we have

K_{J}^{-}[x]\subset K_{J}^{-}(x)=K_{J}^{-}(x^{\prime}\cap J^{[0]}).

Therefore if
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x]
then
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}(x^{\prime}\cap J^{[0]})
and since
J\cap x^{\prime}
is pure, Axiom
iii)
implies that a maximal cell
w
of
J\cap x^{\prime}
satisfies

\operatorname{rk}_{K}(w)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x^{\prime})-1.

Suppose by contradiction that there exists
w^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}(x)\setminus K_{J}^{-}[x]
and let
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x].
Then
w^{\prime}\cap x^{\prime}
cannot be an element of
K_{J}
as it would in particular contradict the minimality of
x^{\prime},
hence
w^{\prime}\cap x^{\prime}\in J.
But this implies that
J\cap w^{\prime}=J\cap x^{\prime}
which by condition (
U
) implies
x=x^{\prime}_{J}=w^{\prime}_{J}.
This contradicts our assumptions and concludes the proof.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
4.17
, if
(K,J)
is a uniform relative cc then the map

J(K)\ni x_{J}\longmapsto\operatorname{Rk}(J\cap x)

is well defined and we have
\operatorname{Rk}(J\cap x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)-1
whenever
x\in K_{J}^{-}[x_{J}].
We can therefore associate the rank function

\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(x_{J}):=\operatorname{Rk}(J\cap x)

to
J(K).

Definition 4.18

(Uniform relative cc)
.

A relative cc
(K,J)
is called
uniform
if the following four conditions are fulfilled:

•

(K,J)
non-degenerate,

•

(K,J)
is pure,

•

the uniformity condition (
U
) holds for
(K,J),

•

(J(K),\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)})\in\mathsf{B}.

It is nevertheless not clear to us whether the uniformity condition (
U
) and the assumption that
J(K)\in\mathsf{B}
are independent from the assumptions of purity and non-degeneracy. From the result of Proposition
2.14
on polytopes, it is reasonable to believe that every non-degenerate pure relative cc
(K,J)
where
K\in\mathsf{C}^{R}
for
R\leq 4
is uniform.

Let us introduce the corresponding notion for cobordisms.

Definition 4.19

(Uniform cobordism)
.

A cobordism
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob},
will be called
uniform
if
(K,\partial K)
uniform.

Note that in general, the dual of a uniform cobordism is not uniform. The next lemma provides an extension of the definition of
reduced cell
x_{J}
for cells
x\in J.

Lemma 4.20
.

If
(K,J)
is uniform and
x\in J
then there exists a unique cell
x_{J}\in J(K)
such that

K_{J}^{-}[x_{J}]=K_{J}^{-}(x)\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(x_{J})=\operatorname{rk}_{J}^{K}(x).

x_{J}
will be called the reduced cell associated to
x.

Proof.

In order to prove the first statement, it is sufficient to show that if
y,y^{\prime}
are distinct elements in
K_{J}^{-}(x)
then
y_{J}=y^{\prime}_{J}.

Since
y
and
y^{\prime}
are minimal elements in
K_{J}(x)
then
y\cap y^{\prime}
cannot be contained in
K_{J},
so
y\cap y^{\prime}\in J.
Hence
J\cap y=J\cap y^{\prime}
and condition (
U
) implies that
y_{J}=y^{\prime}_{J}.

Also, if
y\in K_{J}^{-}(x)
then by definition
\operatorname{rk}_{J}^{K}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(y)-1
and by Lemma
4.17
we have
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(y)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(w)+1
for all maximal cells in
J\cap y.
The second statement then follows since
y\in K_{J}^{-}[x_{J}]
by the first statement and therefore

\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(x_{J})=\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(y_{J})=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(w)=\operatorname{rk}_{J}^{K}(x).

∎

Proposition 4.21
.

Let
K\in\mathsf{C}^{R+1}
and
J\leq\partial K,

J\in\mathsf{B}^{R}
such that
(K,J)
is uniform and local.
Then we have the two following results.

1)

The poset homomorphism
\rho_{J}^{K}:J\longrightarrow J(K)
defined by

\rho_{J}^{K}(x)=x_{J}

is a reduction.

2)

The poset homomorphism
\pi_{J}^{K}:M_{J}^{K}\longrightarrow J(K)
defined by

\pi_{J}^{K}(\mathcal{E}^{x}_{J})=x_{J}

is a collapse.

Proof.

In order to prove the point
1)
we first prove that
\rho_{J}^{K}:J\longrightarrow J(K)
is a reduction. The map
\rho_{J}^{K}
is clearly a surjective poset homomorphism. Condition
(r1)
follows from the fact that if a vertex
v\in J^{[0]}
satisfies
v_{J}\in J(K)^{[0]}
then we have
v_{J}=\{v\}.

Condition
(r3)
is obtained in the following way. Suppose
x\in J
satisfies

x_{J}\subset y\in J(K)^{[r]}.

For
y^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[y]
we then have that
\operatorname{Rk}(J\cap y^{\prime})=r
and
x\in J\cap y^{\prime}.
By purity of
(K,J)
there exists an
r
-cell
x^{\prime}
in
J\cap y^{\prime}
such that
x\subset x^{\prime}.
If we name
w=x_{J}^{\prime}
then Lemma
4.20
implies that

K_{J}^{-}[w]=K_{J}^{-}(x^{\prime}).

Since
x^{\prime}
is a maximal cell of
J\cap y^{\prime}
we have that
y^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}(x^{\prime})
and by the previous relation this implies

y=y^{\prime}_{J}=w=x^{\prime}_{J},

which proves condition
(r3)
for
\rho_{J}^{K}.

Condition
(r2)
then follows from Lemma
4.9
.

We then turn to condition
(r4)
. Let
x\in J
such that
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(x_{J})-1
and let
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x_{J}].
Then
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x^{\prime})=\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(x_{J})+1,
hence
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x^{\prime})=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)-2.
Since
x\subset x^{\prime},
Axiom
iv)
for
K
provides us with two cells
y,y^{\prime}
such that

\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\triangle\left(x^{\prime}\right)=\{y,y^{\prime}\}.

By minimality of
x^{\prime},
we have that
y
and
y^{\prime}
are in
J
and

x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}(y)\cap K_{J}^{-}(y^{\prime})=K_{J}^{-}[y_{J}]\cap K_{J}^{-}[y^{\prime}_{J}],

where the equality is due to Lemma
4.20
. Also, if
w\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
satisfies
w_{J}=x_{J}
then
w\subset x^{\prime}
because
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x_{J}].
Since
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(w)=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)+1=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x^{\prime})-1,
we have
w\in\triangle\left(x^{\prime}\right)
and therefore
w\in\{y,y^{\prime}\}.
This completes the proof that

\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap(\rho_{J}^{K})^{-1}(x_{J})\right|=2.

Then condition
(r5)
can be proven by taking
x\in J
such that
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(x_{J}).
This implies that
x
is a maximal cell of
J\cap x^{\prime}
for all
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x_{J}].
If we take
y\in\bigtriangledown\left(x_{J}\right),

y^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[y]
and
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x_{J}]
such that
x^{\prime}\subset y^{\prime}
then we obtain

\operatorname{rk}_{K}(y^{\prime})=\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(y)+1=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x)+2.

Therefore there exists
x^{\prime\prime}\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)
such that

\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\triangle\left(y^{\prime}\right)=\{x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\}.

If
x^{\prime\prime}\in K_{J}
then
x^{\prime}\cap J=x^{\prime\prime}\cap J=x
which implies
y^{\prime}\cap J=x
but this contradicts the fact that
\operatorname{Rk}(J\cap y^{\prime})=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)+1.

Therefore
x^{\prime\prime}\in J,
hence
x^{\prime\prime}_{J}=y^{\prime}_{J}=y
and we have

\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap(\rho_{J}^{K})^{-1}(y)=\{x^{\prime\prime}\}.

Next we prove that
\pi_{J}^{K}:M_{J}^{K}\longrightarrow J(K)
is a collapse. Condition
(c1)
is clear since
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(z\right)\right|=1
for all
z\in J^{[R-1]}
and therefore
K_{J}^{-}[z]=K_{J}^{-}(z)
contains a unique cell
z^{\prime}
which is a maximal cell of
K,
in other words

(\pi_{J}^{K})^{-1}(z)=\{\mathcal{E}_{J}^{z^{\prime}}\}.

Condition
(c3)
is a consequence of Lemma
4.17
, since if
x\in J(K)
and
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y^{\prime}}\in M_{J}^{K}
are such that
x\subset y_{J}^{\prime}
then there exists
x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[x]
such that
x^{\prime}\subset y^{\prime}.
Hence we have
\operatorname{rk}_{M_{J}^{K}}(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}})=\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(x)
and Lemma
3.2
implies that
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y^{\prime}}.
Condition
(c2)
is again a consequence of Lemma
4.9
.

In order to prove condition
(c4)
, we take
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}}\in M_{J}^{K}
such that

\operatorname{rk}_{M_{J}^{K}}(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}})=\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(x^{\prime}_{J})+1=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x^{\prime})-1.

If
w\in J\cap x^{\prime}
is maximal cell then Axiom
iv)
for
K
gives

\bigtriangledown\left(w\right)\cap\triangle\left(x^{\prime}\right)=\{y,y^{\prime}\},

for
y,y^{\prime}\in K.
The maximality of
w
then implies that
\{y,y^{\prime}\}\subset K_{J}.

We therefore have that

w\subset y\cap y^{\prime}\in J,

which by Axiom
i)
implies that
w=y\cap y^{\prime}.
Therefore
y_{J}=y^{\prime}_{J}=x_{J}
and we indeed have

\left|\triangle\left(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}}\right)\cap(\pi^{-1})(x)\right|=2.

Finally, we can prove condition
(c5)
in the following way. Suppose that
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}}\in M_{J}^{K}
satisfies
\operatorname{rk}_{M_{J}^{K}}(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}})=\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(x^{\prime}_{J}).
If
w
is a maximal cell in
J\cap x^{\prime}
we have that
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(w)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x^{\prime})-1,
i.e.
w\in\triangle\left(x^{\prime}\right).
By Lemma
4.20
we also have that

x^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}(w)=K_{J}^{-}[w_{J}].

If we fix an element
y\in\triangle\left(x\right)
where
x:=x^{\prime}_{J}=w_{J}
then by Lemma
4.17
there exists
y^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}[y]
such that
y^{\prime}\subset x^{\prime}
and

\operatorname{rk}_{K}(y^{\prime})=\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(y)+1=\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x^{\prime})-1.

This implies that
y^{\prime}\in\triangle\left(x^{\prime}\right),
i.e.
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y^{\prime}}\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}}\right)
by Lemma
3.2
. Moreover if
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{w^{\prime}}\in\triangle\left(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}}\right)\setminus\{\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y^{\prime}}\}
satisfies
w^{\prime}_{J}=y
then by minimality of
y^{\prime}
we have
y^{\prime}\cap w^{\prime}\in J.
Hence it must be that
J\cap(y^{\prime}\cap w^{\prime})
has a unique maximal cell
u
and

\{y^{\prime},w^{\prime}\}\subset\bigtriangledown\left(u\right)\cap\triangle\left(x^{\prime}\right).

This is a contradiction with Axiom
iv)
for
K
since
J\cap x^{\prime}
is a pure cc such that

\operatorname{Rk}(J\cap x^{\prime})=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(x^{\prime})-1=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(y^{\prime})=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(u)+1

and therefore there exists a maximal cell
w
of
J\cap x^{\prime}
such that
w\in\bigtriangledown\left(u\right)\cap\bigtriangledown\left(x^{\prime}\right)\setminus\{y^{\prime},w^{\prime}\}.

Thus we can indeed conclude that

\triangle\left(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x^{\prime}}\right)\cap(\pi_{J}^{K})^{-1}(y)=\{\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y^{\prime}}\}.

∎

4.3
Compatible, reflective and orthogonal homomorphisms

For this section and what follows, we introduce the notation
x\vee y
to designate
\bigvee\{x,y\},
the
least upper bound (or supremum) of
{x,y}
, i.e. the smallest cell containing both
x
and
y
in a cc
K
in which an upper bound of
x
and
y
exists. If no cell contains both
x
and
y
we simply write
x\vee y=\overline{\emptyset}.

In addition to being a reduction and a collapse, we will see in this section that the reduction and collapse
\rho_{J}^{K}
and
\pi_{J}^{K}
introduced in Proposition
4.21
of Section
4.2
have a property that will be described as ”
\rho_{J}^{K}
is compatible with
\pi_{J}^{K}
” and constitutes the first definition of this section. Compatibility is therefore in particular a condition on two poset homomorphisms having the same image and this section also introduces the notions of reflectivity and orthogonality, which involve a condition on two homomorphisms having the same domain. These conditions will later on be key in defining specific sequences of reductions and collapses between elements in
\mathsf{B}^{R}
that will be used to construct cells of dimension
R+1.
The notion of ”augmented poset” introduced in the later part of this section will also be used for these constructions, together with a number of technical results proven here.

Definition 4.22

(Compatible homomorphisms,
\leftthreetimes
)
.

Let
j:J\longrightarrow I
and
l:L\longrightarrow I
be cc-homomorphisms. Then we say that
j
is
compatible with

l
if the three following conditions are satisfied:

1)

For all
w\in I
either
\left|j^{-1}(w)\right|=1
or
\left|l^{-1}(w)\right|=1.

2)

If
x,x^{\prime}\in J
are such that
x\vee x^{\prime}\neq\overline{\emptyset},
then

j(x\vee x^{\prime})=j(x)\vee j(x^{\prime}).

3)

If
y,y^{\prime}\in L
are such that
y\cap y^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
then

l(y\cap y^{\prime})=l(y)\cap l(y^{\prime}).

We write
j\leftthreetimes l
to denote that
j
is compatible with
l.
Note that if
j
and
l
are poset isomorphisms then
j
is compatible with
l.
