The next corollary provides typical examples of compatible homomorphisms.

Corollary 4.23
.

Let
K\in\mathsf{C}^{R}
and
J\leq\partial K,

J\in\mathsf{B}^{R-1}
such that
(K,J)
is uniform and local then

J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{J}^{K}}J(K)\dashv_{\pi_{J}^{K}}M_{J}^{K}\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{J}^{K}\leftthreetimes\pi_{J}^{K}.

Proof.

The first statement is a direct consequence of Proposition
4.21
.
As for the first condition of compatibility, suppose by contradiction that there exists
x\in J(K)
such that there are two distinct elements
x_{1},x_{2}\in J
satisfying
(x_{1})_{J}=(x_{2})_{J}=x,
as well as two distinct elements
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{1}},\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{2}}\in M_{J}^{K}
such that
(y_{1})_{J}=(y_{2})_{J}=x.
By the conditions
(r3)
,
(c3)
,
(r4)
and
(c4)
we can assume that

r:=\operatorname{rk}_{J(K)}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x_{i})=\operatorname{rk}_{M_{J}^{K}}(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{i}})=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(y_{i})-1,

for
i=1,2
where the two last equalities come from the definition of the midsection
M_{J}^{K}.
This implies that
y_{1},y_{2}\in K_{J}^{-}[x]
and therefore
y_{1}\cap y_{2}\in J^{[r]}.
But we have
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\subset J\cap(y_{1}\cap y_{2})
so this contradicts the intersection axiom
ii)
of
K.

In order to prove the condition
2)
of compatibility, take
x,x^{\prime}\in J
such that
x\vee x^{\prime}\neq\overline{\emptyset}.
We need to show that

\rho_{J}^{K}(x\vee x^{\prime})=(x\vee x^{\prime})_{J}=x_{J}\vee x^{\prime}_{J}=\rho_{J}^{K}(x)\vee\rho_{J}^{K}(x^{\prime}),

where the map
J\ni x\mapsto x_{J}\in J(K)
is defined in Lemma
4.20
, which also states that
K_{J}^{-}[x_{J}]=K_{J}^{-}(x)
and
\operatorname{rk}(y)-1=\operatorname{rk}(x)
for all
y\in K_{J}^{-}(x).

The inclusion
x_{J}\vee x^{\prime}_{J}\subset(x\vee x^{\prime})_{J}
is clear (and in particular
x_{J}\vee x^{\prime}_{J}\neq\overline{\emptyset}
) since if
y\in K_{J}^{-}(x\vee x^{\prime})
then in particular
x\subset y
and
x^{\prime}\subset y
hence the definition of reduced cells easily leads
x_{J}\subset y_{J}
and
x^{\prime}_{J}\subset y_{J}
and we have

x_{J}\vee x^{\prime}_{J}\subset y_{J}=(x\vee x^{\prime})_{J}.

Now consider
w\in K_{J}^{-}[x_{J}\vee x^{\prime}_{J}],
hence we have that
x_{J}\subset w_{J}
and
x^{\prime}_{J}\subset w_{J}.
The uniformity condition
U
implies that
J\cap y^{\prime}\subset J\cap w
for all
y^{\prime}\in K_{J}^{-}(x)\cup K_{J}^{-}(x^{\prime})
which in particular implies that
x\subset w
and
x^{\prime}\subset w.
Hence we have
x\vee x^{\prime}\subset w
and this implies that
y_{J}=(x\vee x^{\prime})_{J}\subset w_{J}
which leads the desired equality.

The condition
3)
of compatibility is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.2
since if
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y},\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y^{\prime}}\in M_{J}^{K}
are such that
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}\cap\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset,
then

\displaystyle\pi_{J}^{K}(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}\cap\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\pi_{J}^{K}(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y\cap y^{\prime}})=(y\cap y^{\prime})_{J}

\displaystyle=\{e\cap J^{[0]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\in\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y\cap y^{\prime}}\}=\{e\cap J^{[0]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\in\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}\cap\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle=\{e\cap J^{[0]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\in\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y}\}\cap\{e\cap J^{[0]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\in\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle=x_{J}\cap y_{J}=\pi_{J}^{K}(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y})\cap\pi_{J}^{K}(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y^{\prime}}).

∎

Next we define the notions of reflective and orthogonal cc-homomorphisms. We will see later that midsections of slices provide examples of orthogonal collapses, as a consequence of the Correspondence Theorem
4.32
. Reflective reductions will typically be used in the context of unions of cell complexes in
\mathsf{C}
as introduced in Definition
4.35
.

Definition 4.24

(Reflective (
\mathbin{\top}
) and Orthogonal (
\perp
) homomorphisms)
.

Let
j:I\longrightarrow J
and
l:I\longrightarrow L
be two cc-homomorphisms.

1)

We say that
j
and
l
are
reflective
, noted
j\mathbin{\top}l,
if whenever two elements
w,w^{\prime}\in I
satisfy that both
j(w)\vee j(w^{\prime})\neq\overline{\emptyset}
and
l(w)\vee l(w^{\prime})\neq\overline{\emptyset}
then we have that
w\vee w^{\prime}\neq\overline{\emptyset},
as well as the two equalities

j(w\vee w^{\prime})=j(w)\vee j(w^{\prime})\quad\text{ and }\quad l(w\vee w^{\prime})=l(w)\vee l(w^{\prime}).

2)

Dually, we say that
j
and
l
are
orthogonal
, noted
j\perp l,
if whenever two elements
w,w^{\prime}\in I
satisfy that both
j(w)\cap j(w^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
and
l(w)\cap l(w^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
then we have that
w\cap w^{\prime}\neq\emptyset,
as well as the two equalities

j(w\cap w^{\prime})=j(w)\cap j(w^{\prime})\quad\text{ and }\quad l(w\cap w^{\prime})=l(w)\cap l(w^{\prime}).

Note that if
j
and
l
are poset isomorphisms then
j
and
l
are reflective and orthogonal.

In the next lemma we introduce the notion of augmented poset with a corresponding rank function. These will be key tools in the proof of the Correspondence Theorem
4.32
in the next section.

Lemma 4.25
.

Let
M,J,L
be mutually disjoint cc and let
\phi_{J}:M\longrightarrow J
and
\phi_{L}:M\longrightarrow L
be two orthogonal cc-homomorphisms. Then the map from
M
to
\mathcal{P}\left(J^{[0]}\sqcup L^{[0]}\right)
defined by

x\mapsto\phi_{J}(x)\sqcup\phi_{L}(x)

(27)

is an injective poset homorphism such that the inverse is a poset homomorphism. The poset

(\phi_{J}\sqcup\phi_{L})(M):=\{\phi_{J}(m)\sqcup\phi_{L}(m)\penalty\ |\penalty\ m\in M\}

will be called the augmented poset associated to
\phi_{J}
and
\phi_{L}
and will be assigned the rank function

\operatorname{rk}_{(\phi_{J}\sqcup\phi_{L})(M)}(x\sqcup y)=\operatorname{rk}_{M}(m(x,y))+1,

where
m(x,y)
is the unique element in
M
such that

\phi_{J}(m(x,y))\sqcup\phi_{L}(m(x,y))=x\sqcup y.

Proof.

It is sufficient to show that for all
x,x^{\prime}\in M,

\phi_{J}(x)\sqcup\phi_{L}(x)\subset\phi_{J}(x^{\prime})\sqcup\phi_{L}(x^{\prime})\quad\Rightarrow\quad x\subset x^{\prime}.

Suppose by contradiction that there exists
x,x^{\prime}\in M
such that
\phi_{J}(x)\sqcup\phi_{L}(x)\subset\phi_{J}(x^{\prime})\sqcup\phi_{L}(x^{\prime})
and there exists
v\in x\setminus x^{\prime}.
Hence we get
\phi_{I}(v)\subset\phi_{I}(x)\subset\rho_{I}(x^{\prime}),\penalty\ I=J,L
and this implies that
\phi_{J}(v)\cap\phi_{J}(x^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
and
\phi_{L}(v)\cap\phi_{L}(x^{\prime})\neq\emptyset.
The orthogonality condition
2)
implies that
v\in x^{\prime}
which is a contradiction.
∎

The next lemma indicates how compatibility, reflectivity and orthogonality of cc-homomorphisms behave under duality, and is a relatively direct consequence of the definitions.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
I,J,L\in\mathsf{B}^{R}.
Then the two following statements hold.

A)

If
j:J\longrightarrow I
and
\l:L\longrightarrow I
are cc-homomorphisms then

j\leftthreetimes l\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\overline{j}\rightthreetimes\overline{l}.

B)

If
j:I\longrightarrow J
and
l:I\longrightarrow L
are cc-homomorphisms then

j\perp l\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\overline{j}\mathbin{\top}\overline{l}.

Proof.

For the claim
A)
, the first condition of the compatibility of
\overline{l}
with
\overline{j}
is obtained from the fact that if
A
is subset of cells in a closed cc then
\left|\overline{A}\right|=\left|A\right|
by Lemma
1.16
. As for the other conditions for compatibility and the claim
B)
, they follow from the fact that if
x,y
are cells in a closed cc the
\overline{x}\cap\overline{y}=\overline{x\vee y}
and equivalently
\overline{x}\vee\overline{y}=\overline{x\cap y}.
More precisely if
x\vee y=\overline{\emptyset}
then no maximal contains both
x
and
y
hence
\overline{x}\cap\overline{y}=\emptyset,
and if otherwise
\overline{x}\cap\overline{y}
is the set of maximal cells containing both
x
and
y,
i.e. containing
x\vee y.

∎

4.4
Slices and slice sequences

In this section we prove an number of important results related to the notion of slice, a uniform cobordism having in particular the property of having all vertices on its boundary. Proposition
4.28
shows that a slice has exactly two boundary components and Proposition
4.31
proves that one can define the subdivision of a boundary component of an element in
\mathsf{C}
using a relative reduction. Using these two propositions we prove the main result of this chapter: the Correspondence Theorem
4.32
. This theorem establishes a bijection between slices (up to cc-isomorphism) and sequences of the form

J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}J^{\prime}\dashv M\vdash L^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}L

where
J,J^{\prime}M,L^{\prime},L\in\mathsf{B},
introduced in Definition
4.29
as ”slice sequences”.

The notion of slice we introduce next is to be interpreted as a fundamental building bloc of what will become our notion of causal cobordism.

Definition 4.27

(Slice)
.

A cobordism
(S-J)
is a
slice
if it is uniform and

S^{[0]}=(\partial S)^{[0]},\quad J\notin\{\partial S,\emptyset\}.

(28)

We will denote the set of slices by
\mathsf{Cob}_{s}.

An example of a sub-cc of a slice of rank 3 composed of three maximal cells was given in Figure
13
from Section
3.1
on midsections. We give a similar illustration of a slice in Figure
24
.

In particular every maximal cell of a slice
S
in the previous definition has at least one vertex in
J
and one vertex in
\partial S\setminus J
by the non-degeneracy of
(S,\partial S).

The following result establishes a number of important properties of slices.
Note that this result does not use the assumption that
(S-J)
is uniform, except for the fact that
(S,\partial S)
is non-degenerate.

Proposition 4.28
.

Let
(S-J)
be a slice and let
L:=\partial S\setminus J.
Then
M_{L}^{S}=M_{J}^{S}
and
(S-L)
is a slice. Moreover
J
and
L
are connected, i.e.
\partial S
has only two connected components.

Proof.

Proving that
M_{L}^{S}=M_{J}^{S}
amounts to prove that
S_{L}=S_{J}.
The latter is clear from the property (
28
), which implies that
\emptyset\neq x\cap J^{[0]}\neq x
if and ony if
\emptyset\neq x\cap L^{[0]}\neq x,
for all
x\in S.

We next show that
L
is connected. Suppose by contradiction that
L=L_{1}\sqcup L_{2}
where
L_{i}\in\mathsf{B}
for
i=1,2
and let
R=\operatorname{Rk}(L)=\operatorname{Rk}(J).
The case
R=0
is clear since the connectedness of
S
implies that there is a path from
L_{1}
to
L_{2}
going through
J,
which means that there is a vertex in
J
included in two edges and this is a contradiction with the definition of a boundary cell, i.e. that a sub-maximal cell of the boundary - here a 0-cell - is included in only one maximal cell. The strategy of the proof for the case
R\geq 3
is to show that our hypothesis implies that there exists an edge of
S
having one vertex in
L_{1}
and the other vertex in
L_{2},
which contradicts the non-degeneracy of
(S,L).

By connectedness of
S,
there exists a path
p:v_{1}\rightsquigarrow v_{2}
in
S
from
v_{1}\in L_{1}^{[0]}
to
v_{2}\in L_{2}^{[0]}
and we may suppose that
p
is simple and
\left|p^{[0]}\cap L_{i}^{[0]}\right|=1
for
i=1,2,
that is to say,
p
intersects
L
only on two vertices. Let
\{v_{1},w\}
be the first edge of
p.
If
w\in L_{2}
we are done, so we suppose that
w\in J^{[0]}.
By our assumptions,
p
enters a connected component
J_{0}
of
J
from the vertex
v
and leaves
J_{0}
through an edge of the form
\{w^{\prime},v_{2}\},
where
w\in J_{0}^{[0]}.
By locality of
(S,J)
the midsection
M:=M_{J_{0}}^{S}
is connected, hence there exists a path
p_{M}:\{w,v_{1}\}\rightsquigarrow\{w^{\prime},v_{2}\}
in
M.
Each edge of the path
p_{M}
corresponds to a 2-cell in
S_{J}
which by locality intersects
J
in a simple path
p_{0},
or a single vertex
v,
in which case we set
p_{0}=\emptyset_{v}
(cf. definitions in Section
1.3
). So using each such 2-cell
C
in
p_{M}
we can successively deform
p
using a path move of the form
m_{C}^{p_{1}}
where
p_{1}\subset p
is the simple path
p_{1}=\{v_{1},w\}\ast p_{0}.
At each iteration the resulting path might cross
L
in more than two vertices. If it does so by also crossing through
L_{2}
this would imply that there is an edge of
S
crossing from
L_{1}
to
L_{2}
which would produce the desired contradiction. We can therefore assume that deforming
p
can only increase the number of vertices it crosses in
L_{1},
in which case we can simply re-define
v_{1}
to be the last vertex the path crosses in
L_{1}.
In this way we can successively deform and shorten the path
p
until the last step, corresponding to the last edge in
p_{M}
which again produces an edge of
S
crossing from
L_{1}
to
L_{2},
which concludes our argument.

In order to show that
(S-L)\in\mathsf{Cob}
we note that since
(S,L)
is non-degenerate and
M_{L}^{S}=M_{J}^{S}
it only remains to show that
x\cap L
is connected for all
x\in S_{L}.
Assume on the contrary that
L\cap x=X_{1}\sqcup X_{2}
for some
x\in S_{L},
where
X_{i}\leq L
for
i=1,2
and
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are not connected. Then since
S
is cell-connected there exists a path
p:v_{1}\rightsquigarrow v_{2}
in
S\cap x
from
v_{1}\in X_{1}
to
v_{2}\in X_{2}.
By locality of
(S,J)
we have that
\mathcal{E}_{L}^{x}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}
is a connected cell of
M_{L}^{S}=M_{J}^{S}.
Therefore the previous argument again implies that there must exist an edge of the form
\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
where
v_{i}\in X_{i},\penalty\ i=1,2,
which is a contradiction.
As a consequence
(S-L)\in\mathsf{Cob}
is clearly also a slice.

By exchanging the roles of
J
and
L
we have that
J
is also connected, which concludes our proof.
∎

As a consequence of Proposition
4.28
, a cobordism
(S-L)
is a slice if and only if
S\in\mathsf{C},

S^{[0]}=(\partial S)^{[0]},

(S,\partial S)
is uniform and local and
\partial S
has exactly two connected components, one of which is
L.
We will therefore
also call such cc
S
a slice
and denote
the set of slices
by
\mathsf{C}_{s}.
We will also denote the
midsection associated to a slice

S
by

M^{S}:=M_{J}^{S}=M_{L}^{S}.

The remainder of this section focuses on a how to characterize a slice using the notion of a slice sequence. The correspondence between these two objects will also allow us to provide sufficient conditions for the composition of combordisms, as done in the next section, and will be at the basis for the construction of the category of causal cobordisms given in Section
4.6
.

Definition 4.29

(Slice sequence)
.

A sequence of local mutually disjoint cell complexes
M,J,J^{\prime},L,L^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{B}
and maps

J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{J}}J^{\prime}\dashv_{\pi_{J}}M\vdash_{\pi_{L}}L^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}_{\rho_{L}}L

will be called a
slice sequence
if
\rho_{J},\pi_{J},\rho_{L},\pi_{L}
satisfy

\rho_{J}\leftthreetimes\pi_{J},\quad\rho_{L}\leftthreetimes\pi_{L},\quad\pi_{J}\perp\pi_{L}.

The next results are included as a preparation for the Correspondence Theorem
4.32
.

Lemma 4.30
.

Let
K\in\mathsf{C}
and let
J^{\prime}
be a connected component of
\partial K.
If
\rho:J\longrightarrow J^{\prime}
is a reduction where
J
is disjoint from
K
then the map from
K_{J^{\prime}}
to
\mathcal{P}\left(\left(K^{[0]}\setminus(J^{\prime})^{[0]}\right)\sqcup J^{[0]}\right)
defined by

y^{\prime}\longmapsto\left(y^{\prime}\setminus(J^{\prime})^{[0]}\right)\sqcup\left(\bigcup_{x\in J,\penalty\ \rho(x)\subset y^{\prime}}x\right)

(29)

is an injective poset homomorphism such that the inverse is also a poset homomorphism.

Proof.

Let us denote by
\phi
the map (
29
), which clearly is a poset homomorphism since so is
\rho.

The injectivity of
\phi
is a direct consequence of condition
(r1)
and the observation that any vertex
v\in\rho^{-1}((J^{\prime})^{[0]}\cap y^{\prime})
satisfies that
\phi(v)\subset y^{\prime}
hence
v
is included in
\left(\bigcup_{x\in J,\penalty\ \rho(x)\subset y^{\prime}}x\right).

From the last observation one also get that the inverse of
\phi
is a poset homomorphism since if
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in K_{J^{\prime}}
satisfy that
\phi(x^{\prime})\subset\phi(y^{\prime})
then it implies that
x^{\prime}\setminus(J^{\prime})^{[0]}\subset y^{\prime}\setminus(J^{\prime})^{[0]}
and

\rho^{-1}((J^{\prime})^{[0]}\cap x^{\prime})\subset\rho^{-1}((J^{\prime})^{[0]}\cap y^{\prime}).

Therefore
(r1)
implies that
(J^{\prime})^{[0]}\cap x^{\prime}\subset(J^{\prime})^{[0]}\cap y^{\prime}
and it follows that
x^{\prime}\subset y^{\prime}.

∎

Proposition 4.31
.

Let
K\in\mathsf{C}
and let
J^{\prime}
be a connected component of
\partial K
such that
(K,J^{\prime})
is uniform. If
\rho:J\longrightarrow J^{\prime}
is a reduction such that
\rho\circ\rho_{J^{\prime}}^{K}:J\longrightarrow J^{\prime}(K)
is compatible with
\pi_{J^{\prime}}^{K}:M_{J^{\prime}}^{K}\longrightarrow J^{\prime}(K)
and
J
is disjoint from
K
then the cc
K^{\rho}
(introduced in Definition
4.13
) is an element in
\mathsf{C}
and
J
is a connected component of
\partial K^{\rho},
in other words

(K^{\rho},J)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho}(K,J^{\prime})

defines a relative reduction. Moreover, we have that
\rho_{J}^{K^{\rho}}=\rho\circ\rho_{J^{\prime}}^{K}
and
(K^{\rho},J)
is uniform.

Proof.

Since Proposition
4.11
implies that
\rho\circ\rho_{J^{\prime}}^{K}
is also a reduction we can without loss of generality assume that
\rho_{J^{\prime}}^{K}=\operatorname{id}_{J^{\prime}(K)},
i.e.
J^{\prime}=J^{\prime}(K)
and
\rho=\rho_{J^{\prime}}^{K}\circ\rho.

Define
(J^{\prime})^{c}:=K\cap(K^{[0]}\setminus(J^{\prime})^{[0]}),
i.e. the sub-cc of
K
containing the cells in
K
not intersecting
J^{\prime}.
Let
\phi
be the map defined by (
29
) which by Lemma
4.30
defines a poset isomorphism

\phi:K_{J^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\phi(K_{J^{\prime}}).

We define
(K^{\rho},\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}})
by

\displaystyle K^{\rho}

\displaystyle:=(J^{\prime})^{c}\sqcup J\sqcup\phi(K_{J^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{rk}_{K}|_{(J^{\prime})^{c}}+\operatorname{rk}_{J}+\operatorname{rk}_{K}|_{K_{J^{\prime}}}\circ\phi^{-1}.

It then follows that
K_{J}^{\rho}=\phi(K_{J^{\prime}}).
Also, since
J^{\prime}=\pi_{J^{\prime}}^{K}(M_{J^{\prime}}^{K})
as
\rho_{J^{\prime}}^{K}=\operatorname{id}_{J^{\prime}}
we have that

\text{for all }y^{\prime}\in K_{J^{\prime}},(y^{\prime})_{J^{\prime}}=y^{\prime}\cap({J^{\prime}})^{[0]}.

(30)

Let’s now check the axioms of cc for
K^{\rho}.
To make our notations more explicit, for a cell
x
in a cc
L
we will denote by
\bigtriangledown_{L}\left(x\right)
the set of co-faces of
x
in
L
and similarly for faces. Since these axioms are satisfied for
K
they are automatically satisfied when all cells involved are in
J
or
(J^{\prime})^{c}\sqcup\phi(K_{J})
by the poset isomorphism property of
\phi.
This allows us to only check the axioms in the case of inclusions of cells between the sets
J
and
(J^{\prime})^{c}\sqcup\phi(K_{J}).

To check Axiom
i)
we only need to consider the case where
x\in J,

y\in K^{\rho}_{J}
and
x\subsetneq y.
In this case we have that
\rho(x)\subsetneq\phi^{-1}(y)
and therefore

\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}}(x)\leq\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))<\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\phi^{-1}(y))=\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}}(y).

To check Axiom
ii)
for the non-direct cases amounts to show that
x\cap y\in K^{\rho}
for
x\in K_{J}^{\rho}\sqcup J
and
y\in K_{J}^{\rho}.
If
x\in K_{J}^{\rho}
and
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in K_{J^{\prime}}
are such that
\phi(x^{\prime})=x
and
\phi(y^{\prime})=y
then we have two possible cases: either
\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{x^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{y^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
or
\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{x^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{y^{\prime}}=\emptyset.
In the first case one has that
x^{\prime}\cap y^{\prime}\in K_{J^{\prime}}
and

\phi(x^{\prime}\cap y^{\prime})=\phi(x^{\prime})\cap\phi(y^{\prime})=x\cap y\in K^{\rho}_{J}.

For the case
\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{x^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{y^{\prime}}=\emptyset,
then by property (
30
), we have that

x^{\prime}\cap y^{\prime}=(x^{\prime})_{J^{\prime}}\cap(y^{\prime})_{J^{\prime}}\in J^{\prime}(K)=J^{\prime}.

By Proposition
4.21
we know that
\pi_{J^{\prime}}^{K}
is a collapse, hence by
(c3)
we can pick
x^{\prime}_{0}\in B(x^{\prime})^{[r+1]}
and
y^{\prime}_{0}\in B(y^{\prime})^{[r+1]}
where
r=rk(x^{\prime}\cap y^{\prime})=\operatorname{rk}_{M_{J^{\prime}}^{K}}(\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{x^{\prime}_{0}})=\operatorname{rk}_{M_{J^{\prime}}^{K}}(\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{y^{\prime}_{0}}).
By Lemma
4.17
this implies that
x^{\prime}_{0},y^{\prime}_{0}\in K^{-}_{J^{\prime}}[x^{\prime}\cap y^{\prime}],
and since
\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{x^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{y^{\prime}}=\emptyset,
we also have that
\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{x_{0}^{\prime}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{J^{\prime}}^{y_{0}^{\prime}}=\emptyset.
The latter implies that
\left|(\pi_{J^{\prime}}^{K})^{-1}(x^{\prime}\cap y^{\prime})\right|>1
which by condition
1)
of compatibility implies that there exists
x\in J
such that
\rho^{-1}(x^{\prime}\cap y^{\prime})=\{w_{0}\}.
Therefore we obtain

\phi(x^{\prime})\cap\phi(y^{\prime})=\bigcup_{w\in\rho^{-1}(B(x^{\prime}\cap y^{\prime}))}w=w_{0}\in J.

For the case where
x\in J
and if
y^{\prime}=\phi^{-1}(y)\in K_{J^{\prime}}
then one has to show that

x\cap\left(\bigcup_{w\in J\penalty\ :\penalty\ \rho(w)\subset y^{\prime}}w\right)\in J.

Let
w_{0}:=(y^{\prime})_{J^{\prime}},
then
\{w\in J\penalty\ |\penalty\ \rho(w)\subset y^{\prime}\}=\rho^{-1}(w_{0}),
hence we need to show that there exists a unique maximal cell in
B(x)\cap\rho^{-1}(w_{0}).
Suppose by contradiction that
w_{1},w_{2}
are two such maximal cells. Since we have
w_{i}\subset x
for
i=1,2,
we have that
w_{1}\vee w_{2}\neq\overline{\emptyset}.
Condition
2)
of compatibility then implies that

\rho_{J^{\prime}}^{K}(w_{1}\vee w_{2})=\rho(w_{1})\vee\rho(w_{2})\subset w_{0},

where the inclusion is due to the fact that
\rho(w_{i})\subset w_{0}
for
i=1,2.
But this implies that
w_{1}\vee w_{2}\in\rho^{-1}(B(w_{0}))
and contradicts the maximality of
w_{1}
and
w_{2}.
This finishes the proof of Axiom
ii)
for
K^{\rho}.

Axiom
iii)
can be inferred from the case
J\ni x\subsetneq y\in K^{\rho}_{J}
which is proven as follows. By the poset isomorphism property of
\phi
we have that
\rho(x)\subset\phi^{-1}(y)\in K_{J^{\prime}}.
Therefore by Axiom
iii)
for
K
we can suppose without loss of generality that
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(y)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))+1.
If
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))
then we have that
y\in\bigtriangledown_{K^{\rho}}\left(x\right)
as desired. If
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)<\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))
then, as noted in Remark
4.6
, there exists
w\in\rho^{-1}(\rho(x))
such that
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(w)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x)).
Therefore using
iii)
for
J
we can find a co-face of
x
contained in
w
which is then also contained in
y.

For Axiom
iv)
it is also sufficient to focus on the case where
x\in J,y\in K_{J}^{\rho},

x\subsetneq y
and

\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}}(y)-2.

Since
\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}}(y)=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\phi^{-1}(y)),
this leave two possibilities: either we have
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))=\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}}(x)
or
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))=\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}}(x)+1.
In the first case where
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))=\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}}(x),
Axiom
iv)
for
K
implies that

\left|\bigtriangledown_{K}\left(\rho(x)\right)\cap\triangle_{K}\left(\phi^{-1}(y)\right)\right|=2.

To conclude this first case, it remains to show that there is a bijection between
\bigtriangledown_{K}\left(\rho(x)\right)\cap\triangle_{K}\left(\phi^{-1}(y)\right)
and
\bigtriangledown_{K^{\rho}}\left(x\right)\cap\triangle_{K^{\rho}}\left(y\right).

If
w^{\prime}\in\bigtriangledown_{K}\left(\rho(x)\right)\cap\triangle_{K}\left(\phi^{-1}(y)\right)
then either
w^{\prime}\in K_{J^{\prime}}
or
w^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}.
Since
w^{\prime}\in K_{J^{\prime}}
if and only if

\phi(w^{\prime})\in\bigtriangledown_{K^{\rho}}\left(x\right)\cap\triangle_{K^{\rho}}\left(y\right)
and
\phi
is bijection, we are good for this case.
If
w^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
then
w^{\prime}\in\bigtriangledown_{J^{\prime}}\left(\rho(x)\right)
and
(r5)
implies that the map

\bigtriangledown_{J}\left(x\right)\cap\rho^{-1}(\bigtriangledown_{K}\left(\rho(x)\right))\ni w\longmapsto\rho(w)\in\bigtriangledown_{J^{\prime}}\left(\rho(x)\right)

is a bijection, and this concludes the case
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))=\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}}(x).

In the second case where
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))=\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho}}(x)+1
condition
(r4)
implies that

\left|\bigtriangledown_{J}\left(x\right)\cap\rho^{-1}(\rho(x))\right|=2.

Moreover, if
w\in\bigtriangledown_{K^{\rho}}\left(x\right)\cap\triangle_{K^{\rho}}\left(y\right)
then
\rho(w)\subset\phi^{-1}(y)
and

\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)+1=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(w)\leq\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(w))<\operatorname{rk}_{K}(y)=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)+2.

Therefore
\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(w))=\operatorname{rk}_{K}(\rho(x))
and
\rho(w)=\rho(x).
Thus
w\in\bigtriangledown_{J}\left(x\right)\cap\rho^{-1}(\rho(x))
and this also proves that

\left|\bigtriangledown_{K^{\rho}}\left(x\right)\cap\triangle_{K^{\rho}}\left(y\right)\right|=2,

which concludes the proof of
iv)
and shows that
K^{\rho}
is a cc

The cc
K^{\rho}
is graph-based if
K^{\rho}_{J}
is graph-based, which is the case since
e\in(K^{\rho}_{J})^{[1]}
if and only if
e^{\prime}:=\phi^{-1}(e)\in K^{[1]}_{J^{\prime}}
and therefore

\left|e\right|=\left|e^{\prime}\setminus(J^{\prime})^{[0]}\sqcup\rho^{-1}(J^{[0]}\cap e)\right|=2

by condition
(r1)
.

The previous argument also implies that
K^{\rho}_{J}
is cell-connected. Since
K
is connected and cell-connected, this shows that
K^{\rho}
is connected and cell-connected.

One has that
K^{\rho}
is clearly pure and
R:=\operatorname{Rk}(K^{\rho})=\operatorname{Rk}(K).
And
K^{\rho}
is non-branching if
\left|\bigtriangledown\left(y^{\prime}\right)\right|=2
for all
y^{\prime}\in(K^{\rho}_{J})^{[R-1]}
which is a direct consequence of the poset isomorphism property of
\phi.

A
K^{\rho}
-pinch cannot occur in
(J^{\prime})^{c}
nor
K^{\rho}_{J}
since the poset isomorphism property of
\phi
would produce a
K
-pinch. If
x\in J
then

\mathcal{G}_{\overline{K^{\rho}}}\cap\overline{x}^{K^{\rho}}=\phi(\overline{\rho(x)}^{K})\sqcup\{\{\phi(z),\phi(z^{\prime})\}\subset K^{[R]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \rho(x)\subset z\cap z^{\prime}\in K^{[R-1]}\}\cong\mathcal{G}_{\overline{K}}\cap\overline{\rho(x)}^{K}.

Therefore there also cannot be a
K^{\rho}
-pinch in
J
since this would contradict that
K
has no
K
-pinch on
J^{\prime}.

This in addition shows that
K^{\rho}\in\mathsf{C}.

By Remark
4.7
we have
\operatorname{Rk}(J)=\operatorname{Rk}(J^{\prime})=R-1
and every cell in
J
is included in a cell
y\in J^{[R-1]}.
By Remark
4.6
such a
y\in J^{[R-1]}
satisfies that
\rho(y)\in(J^{\prime})^{[R-1]}.
Therefore we have

\left|\bigtriangledown_{K^{\rho}}\left(y\right)\right|=\left|\bigtriangledown_{K}\left(\rho(y)\right)\right|=1,

hence
J
is indeed a connected component of
\partial K^{\rho}.

We have that
\rho_{J}^{K^{\rho}}=\rho
by the following arguments.
If
v^{\prime}
is the vertex in
e\cap J^{\prime}
for
e\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{J^{\prime}}
then by
(r1)
we have that
\rho^{-1}(\{v^{\prime}\})
contains exactly one vertex
v
from
J.
We will therefore consider the vertices
v^{\prime}
and
v
as identical to simplify the discussion. With this convention, a cell
y\in J^{\prime}(K)
can be seen as having its vertices in
J.
Moreover, if
y^{\prime}\in K_{J^{\prime}}^{-}[y]
then
\phi(y^{\prime})\in(K^{\rho})_{J}^{-}(x)
for all
x\in J
such that
\rho(x)\subset y.
Since we also assume that
J^{\prime}=J^{\prime}(K)
and
\rho_{J^{\prime}}^{K}=\operatorname{id}_{J^{\prime}}
we have that
y=y_{J^{\prime}}
for all
y\in J^{\prime}.
Therefore, if we take
x\in J
and
y^{\prime}\in K_{J^{\prime}}^{-}[\rho(x)_{J^{\prime}}]
then

x_{J}=\phi(y^{\prime})_{J}=y^{\prime}_{J^{\prime}}=\rho(x)_{J^{\prime}}=\rho(x)

and this proves that
\rho=\rho_{J}^{K^{\rho}}.

This also directly implies that
J(K^{\rho})=J^{\prime}(K)
and that
(K^{\rho},J)
is uniform.
∎

Theorem 4.32

(Correspondence)
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between slice sequences and slices, up to cc-isomorphism. More precisely, a sequence

J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{J}}J^{\prime}\dashv_{\pi_{J}}M\vdash_{\pi_{L}}L^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}_{\rho_{L}}L

is a slice sequence if and only if the poset
(S,\operatorname{rk}_{S})
defined by

S:=(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M)\sqcup J\sqcup L,

\operatorname{rk}_{S}:=\operatorname{rk}_{(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M)}+\operatorname{rk}_{J}+\operatorname{rk}_{L},

is an element in
\mathsf{C}_{s}
such that

\partial S=J\sqcup L,\quad M=M^{S},\quad J(S)=J^{\prime},\quad L(S)=L^{\prime},

\phi_{I}=\phi_{I}^{S}\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{ for all }\penalty\ \penalty\ \phi\in\{\rho,\pi\},\penalty\ \penalty\ I\in\{J,L\}.

Figure 24
:
On the top of this illustration we represented the portion of a slice
S
of rank 3 with boundary components
J
and
L
and midsection
M=M^{S}.
For the sake of clarity, we did not draw the 2-cells in the collar
S_{J}=S_{L}.
We singled out one maximal cell
x
of
S
and represented the cells in
S\cap x.
The lower part of the figure explains how, starting with a slice sequence as in Theorem
4.32
, the cells in
M\cap m
for some
m\in M
are used to construct the cells in
S\cap x
using the reductions
\rho_{J},\rho_{L}
and the collapses
\pi_{J},\pi_{L}.
We also specified how the cells in
(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M\cap m)
look like in this case.

Proof.

We first show that a slice sequence is uniquely defined from a slice. This can be obtained directly as a consequence of Corollary
4.23
if we also show that if
S
is a slice with boundary components
J
and
L
then

\pi_{J}^{S}\perp\pi_{L}^{S}.

In order to prove that
\pi_{J}^{S}
and
\pi_{L}^{S}
are orthogonal, i.e. satisfy the point
2)
of Definition
4.24
, let us consider
m_{1},m_{2}\in M^{S}
such that
\pi_{J}^{S}(m_{1})\cap\pi_{J}^{S}(m_{2})\neq\emptyset
and
\pi_{L}^{S}(m_{1})\cap\pi_{L}^{S}(m_{2})\neq\emptyset.
By Proposition
4.28
we have that
m_{i}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{i}}=\mathcal{E}_{L}^{y_{i}}
for some
y_{i}\in S_{J}=S_{L},
for
i=1,2,
and the definition of
\pi_{J}^{S}
in Proposition
4.21
implies that
\pi_{J}^{S}(m_{i})=(y_{i})_{J}
for
i=1,2.
By assumption we therefore have that
(y_{1})_{J}\cap(y_{2})_{J}\neq\emptyset
and
(y_{1})_{L}\cap(y_{2})_{L}\neq\emptyset,
hence
y_{1}\cap y_{2}\neq\emptyset.
Lemma
3.2
implies
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{1}\cap y_{2}}=\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{1}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{2}}
hence we get the following inclusion for
I=J,L
:

\displaystyle(y_{1}\cap y_{2})_{I}

\displaystyle=\{e\cap J^{[0]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\in\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{1}\cap y_{2}}\}=\{e\cap J^{[0]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\in\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{1}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{2}}\}

\displaystyle\subset\{e\cap J^{[0]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\in\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{1}}\}\cap\{e\cap J^{[0]}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\in\mathcal{E}_{J}^{y_{2}}\}=(y_{1})_{I}\cap(y_{2})_{I}.

To prove the reverse inclusion, and conclude that
\pi_{J}^{S}
and
\pi_{L}^{S}
are orthogonal, suppose by contradiction that there exists a vertex
v
in
(y_{1})_{I}\cap(y_{2})_{I}\setminus(y_{1}\cap y_{2})_{I}
for
I=J
or
I=L.
If
\operatorname{rk}(y_{1}\cap y_{2})=1
we directly obtain a contradiction as in this case
v\in y_{1}\cap y_{2}=(y_{1}\cap y_{2})_{I}.
If
\operatorname{rk}(y_{1}\cap y_{2})\geq 2,
we have in particular that
v
is by assumption contained in an edge
e\subset\mathcal{E}_{I}^{y_{1}}
and since
v\notin(y_{1}\cap y_{2})_{I},
there is no edge in
\mathcal{E}_{I}^{y_{1}\cap y_{2}}
containing
v.
As a consequence, if
x
is a minimal cell in
A(v)\cap S_{I}^{y_{1}\cap y_{2}}
then
\operatorname{rk}(x)\geq 2.
But
x
is then also a minimal cell in
S(v)
and this contradicts the assumption that
(S,\partial S)
is pure, since both
e
and
x
are minimal cells and
1=\operatorname{rk}(e)\neq\operatorname{rk}(x).

We then show how to obtain a slice from a slice sequence. By Proposition
4.31
, it is sufficient to consider the case
J=J^{\prime}
and
L=L^{\prime}
since if in this case we obtain a slice
S
(satisfying the desired conditions) the latter result using
\rho=\rho_{I}
for
I=J,L
indeed leads
\rho_{I}^{S}=\rho_{I}
and
I(S)=I^{\prime}.

Our goal is then to prove that
(S,\operatorname{rk}_{S})\in\mathsf{C}_{s}
where we recall that
(S,\operatorname{rk}_{S})
is defined by

S:=(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M)\sqcup J\sqcup L,

\operatorname{rk}_{S}:=\operatorname{rk}_{(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M)}+\operatorname{rk}_{J}+\operatorname{rk}_{L},

and
(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M)
is the augmented poset associated to
\pi_{J}
and
\pi_{L}.

As usual we start by proving that
S
is a cc. By Lemma
4.25
the Axioms
i)
and
iii)
are directly derived from the same axioms for the cc
J,L
and
M.

As for Axiom
ii)
, it is sufficient to show that if
m,m^{\prime}\in M,
then

x:=(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(m)\cap(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(m^{\prime})\in S\cup\{\emptyset\}.

If either
(\pi_{J}(m)\cap\pi_{J}(m^{\prime})=\emptyset
or
(\pi_{L}(m)\cap\pi_{L}(m^{\prime})=\emptyset
then Axiom
ii)
for respectively
L
and
J
implies that
x\in L
or
x\in J.
If both
(\pi_{J}(m)\cap\pi_{J}(m^{\prime})
and
(\pi_{L}(m)\cap\pi_{L}(m^{\prime})
are non-empty, then as a consequence of the orthogonality of
\pi_{J}
and
\pi_{L}
we have that
m\cap m^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and

x=(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(m\cap m^{\prime})\in S.

And in order to show Axiom
iv)
for
S
the only cases that are not directly obtained from Axiom
iv)
for
J,L
or
M
are inclusions of the form
w\subsetneq(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(m),
for some
m\in M
and
w\in J\sqcup L
such that

\operatorname{rk}_{S}(w)=\operatorname{rk}_{S}((\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(m))-2=\operatorname{rk}_{M}(m)-1.

To deal with these cases we set
(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(m):=x\sqcup y
and assume without loss of generality that
w\in J,
hence
w\subset x=\pi_{J}(m).
As noted in Remark
4.6
, condition
(c3)
implies that
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(\pi_{J}(m))\leq\operatorname{rk}_{M}(m).
Therefore we have

\operatorname{rk}_{S}(w)=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(w)\leq\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)\leq\operatorname{rk}_{M}(m)

and this leaves us with two possibilities: either
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{M}(m)-1
or
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{M}(m).
In the first case we have
w=x,
so that
(c4)
implies

\left|\triangle\left(m\right)\cap(\pi_{J})^{-1}(x)\right|=2.

By Lemma
4.25
an element
m^{\prime}\in M
belongs to
\triangle\left(m\right)\cap(\pi_{J})^{-1}(x)
if and only if it satisfies that
\operatorname{rk}_{S}(\pi_{J}(m^{\prime})\sqcup\pi_{L}(m^{\prime}))=\operatorname{rk}_{S}(x)+1
and
\pi_{J}(m^{\prime})=x.
Moreover any such
m^{\prime}
satisfies
\pi_{L}(m^{\prime})\subset y
hence
m^{\prime}\in\triangle\left(m\right)\cap(\pi_{J})^{-1}(x)
if and only if

\pi_{J}(m^{\prime})\sqcup\pi_{L}(m^{\prime})\in\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\triangle\left(x\sqcup y\right).

Therefore we indeed have

\left|\triangle\left(m\right)\cap(\pi_{J})^{-1}(x)\right|=\left|\bigtriangledown\left(x\right)\cap\triangle\left(x\sqcup y\right)\right|

which concludes the case when
x=w.

The second case is when
w\subsetneq x=\pi_{J}(m)
and therefore
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(\pi_{J}(m))=\operatorname{rk}_{M}(m).
In this case
(c5)
implies

\left|\triangle\left(m\right)\cap(\pi_{J})^{-1}(x^{\prime})\right|=1\quad\forall x^{\prime}\in\triangle\left(x\right).

Since
w\in\triangle\left(x\right)
we have
\triangle\left(m\right)\cap(\pi_{J})^{-1}(w)=\{w^{\prime}\}
and therefore

\bigtriangledown\left(w\right)\cap\triangle\left(x\sqcup y\right)=\{x,\pi_{J}(w^{\prime})\sqcup\pi_{L}(w^{\prime})\}.

This concludes the proof that
S
is a cc.

S
is graph-based since every edge in
(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M)
is of the form
\pi_{J}(v)\sqcup\pi_{L}(v)
for some vertex
v\in M^{[0]}
and
\operatorname{rk}_{J}(\pi_{J}(v))=\operatorname{rk}_{L}(\pi_{L}(v))=0
by Remark
4.6
.

S
is also clearly connected and cell-connected, as every cell
\pi_{J}(m)\sqcup\pi_{L}(m)\in(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M)
satisfies that
J\cap\pi_{J}(m)
and
L\cap\pi_{L}(m)
are connected and can be linked by an edge of the form
\pi_{J}(v)\sqcup\pi_{L}(v)
where
v\in m.

By Remark
4.7
we have
\operatorname{Rk}(J)=\operatorname{Rk}(M)=\operatorname{Rk}(L)=R.
It follows that
S
is pure of rank
R+1
since the surjectivity of
\pi_{J}
and
\pi_{L}
implies that every cell in
J\sqcup L
is contained in a cell of the form
\pi_{J}(m)\sqcup\pi_{L}(m)
for some
m\in M^{R}
and hence

\operatorname{rk}_{S}(\pi_{J}(m)\sqcup\pi_{L}(m))=\operatorname{rk}_{M}(m)+1=R+1.

S
is non-branching as a consequence of Lemma
4.25
for the cells in
(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M)
and since
J
and
L
are also non-branching.

By Lemma
4.25
and the closeness of
M
every
R
-cell in
S\setminus(J\sqcup L)
is contained in two
(R+1)
-cells. Therefore every
R
cell contained in one
(R+1)
-cell must be contained in either
J
or
L
and by the surjectivity of
\pi_{J}
and
\pi_{L}
it is the case for all maximal cells of
J
and
L,
hence
\partial S=J\sqcup L.

S
is non-pinching since an
S
-pinch would imply an
M
-pinch by Lemma
4.25
and a
\partial S
-pinch would imply a
J
-pinch or an
L
-pinch by the previous derivation, all of which are excluded since
M,J,L
are non-pinching.

As a consequence we indeed have that
S\in\mathsf{C}.

It remains to show that
S
is a slice. First, we have that
K^{[0]}=J^{[0]}\sqcup L^{[0]}
since every cell in
(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M)
has rank higher than or equal to one. Clearly
S_{J}=S_{L}=(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M)
and by Lemma
4.25
this implies
M=M^{S}.

(S,\partial S)
is also clearly non-degenerate and it is pure by the following argument. If, say,
y\in S_{J}
then
y=\pi_{J}(m)\cap\pi_{L}(m)
for some
m\in M
and
J\cap y=J\cap\pi_{J}(m)
is a pure cc. Hence if
y
has minimal rank among elements in
S_{J}(x)
then
\pi_{J}(m)=x.
By condition
(c3)
we can assume that
\operatorname{rk}_{M}(m)=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)
therefore

\operatorname{rk}_{S}(y)=\operatorname{rk}_{M}(m)+1=\operatorname{rk}_{J}(x)+1=\operatorname{rk}_{J}^{S}(x)+1,

and this shows that all elements in
S^{-}(x)
have same rank.

Concerning uniformity, for all
\pi_{J}(m)\sqcup\pi_{L}(m)\in(\pi_{J}\sqcup\pi_{L})(M)=S_{\partial S}
we have that

(\pi_{J}(m)\sqcup\pi_{L}(m))_{J}=\{v\in\pi_{J}(m)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \exists w\in m,\penalty\ \pi(w)=v\}=\pi_{J}(m)=\left(\pi_{J}(m)\sqcup\pi_{L}(m)\right)\cap J^{[0]}

and similarly for
L.
Since we also assumed that
J(S)=J\in\mathsf{B}
and
L(S)=L\in\mathsf{B}
it follows that
(S,\partial S)
is indeed uniform.

Finally, we have that
\pi_{I}=\pi_{I}^{S}
for
I\in\{J,L\}
by the following argument. It is sufficient to show this for the case
I=J
in which for any
m\in M
we have

\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}=\{\pi_{J}(v)\sqcup\pi_{L}(v)\penalty\ |\penalty\ v\in m\},

where
x=\pi_{J}(m)\sqcup\pi_{L}(m).
By identifying each vertex
v\in m
with the edge
\pi_{J}(v)\sqcup\pi_{L}(v)\in(M^{S})^{[0]}
we obtain
\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x}=m
and therefore

\pi_{J}^{S}(\mathcal{E}_{J}^{x})=x_{J}=x\cap J^{[0]}=\pi_{J}(m),

where in the second equality we used the assumption
J=J(S).

∎

4.5
Connecting sequences and union of cc

In this section we start by introducing the notion dual to that of a slice sequence called a ”connecting sequence” consisting of reductions and collapses from and to elements
M,J,I,L,M^{\prime}\in\mathsf{B}
related in the following way:

M\vdash J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}I\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}L\dashv M^{\prime}.

Such a sequence is designed to define the union of elements
K,H\in\mathsf{C}
such that
I=K\cap H\in\mathsf{B}
is a connected component of the boundary of
K
and
H.
In this case
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathsf{B}
are chosen as
M=M_{I}^{K},

M^{\prime}=M_{I}^{H}
and the cc
J,L\in\mathsf{B}
are the associated transitions i.e.
J=I(K)
and
L=I(K).

Connecting sequences are thus used in Definition
4.34
to give a precise meaning to the notion of cobordisms ”connecting through” a given boundary component. It will then be natural to give the definition of the union of two cc in
\mathsf{C}
sharing a boundary component and prove in Corollary
4.36
that it defines an element in
\mathsf{C}
as a consequence of the Correspondence Theorem shown in the previous section.

Definition 4.33

(Connecting sequence)
.

A sequence of mutually disjoint local cell complexes
M,J,

I,L,M^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{B}
and maps

M\vdash_{\pi_{J}}J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}_{\rho_{J}}I\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{L}}L\dashv_{\pi_{L}}M^{\prime}

will be called a
connecting sequence
if
\pi_{J},\rho_{J},\pi_{L},\rho_{L}
satisfy

\rho_{J}\leftthreetimes\pi_{J},\quad\rho_{L}\leftthreetimes\pi_{L},\quad\rho_{J}\mathbin{\top}\rho_{L}.

Definition 4.34

(Connecting boundary components)
.

Let
K,H\in\mathsf{C}^{R}
and
J,L\in\mathsf{B}^{R-1}
be connected components of
\partial K
and
\partial H,
respectively, such that
(K,J)
and
(H,L)
are uniform. Then we say that
K
and
H
connect through

I\in\mathsf{B}^{R-1}
if there exist cell complexes
K^{\rho_{J}},H^{\rho_{L}}\in\mathsf{C}^{R}
and relative reductions
(I,K^{\rho_{J}})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{J}}(J,K)
and
(I,H^{\rho_{L}})\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{L}}(L,H)
such that

M_{J}^{K}\vdash_{\pi^{K}_{J}}J(K)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}_{\rho_{J}\circ\rho_{J}^{K}}I\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{L}\circ\rho_{L}^{H}}L(H)\dashv_{\pi_{L}}M_{L}^{H}

(31)

is a connecting sequence, where
\pi^{K}_{J},\rho_{J}^{K},\pi_{L}^{H}
and
\rho_{L}^{H}
are defined in Proposition
4.21
. In this case we say that
\rho_{J}
and
\phi_{L}

are connecting
K
and
H
through
I.

The next definition gives a candidate object for what should be the union of two cell complexes in
\mathsf{C}
with ”similar” boundary components. It will be shown in the following lemma that it indeed uniquely determines an element in
\mathsf{C}.

For the purpose of defining the composition of cobordisms as morphisms in a category, it is sufficient to consider the case
J\cong I\cong L
in the following definition.

Definition 4.35

(Union of cell complexes with connecting boundary components)
.

Let
K,H\in\mathsf{C}^{R}
and
J,L\in\mathsf{B}^{R-1}
be boundary components of respectively
K
and
H
such that
(K,J)
and
(H,L)
are uniform. Let

(K^{\rho_{J}},I)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{J}}(K,J),\quad(H^{\rho_{L}},I)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{L}}(H,L)

be two relative reductions connecting
K
and
H
through
I
and such that
K^{\rho_{J}}\cap H^{\rho_{L}}=I.
We define the
union of
K
and
H
by
\rho_{J}
and
\rho_{L}

to be the poset

K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho_{J}\penalty\ \rho_{L}}}{{\cup}}H:=K^{\rho_{J}}\cup H^{\rho_{L}}

with rank function

\operatorname{rk}_{\rho_{J},\rho_{L}}=\operatorname{rk}_{K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho_{J}\penalty\ \rho_{L}}}{{\cup}}H}:=\operatorname{rk}_{K^{\rho_{J}}}+\operatorname{rk}_{H^{\rho_{L}}}-\operatorname{rk}_{I}.

In the particular case were
J=I=L,
i.e.
\rho_{J}=\rho_{L}=\operatorname{id}_{I},
we will simply write

K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho_{J}\penalty\ \rho_{L}}}{{\cup}}H=K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle I}}{{\cup}}H.

Corollary 4.36
.

(K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho_{J}\penalty\ \rho_{L}}}{{\cup}}H,\operatorname{rk}_{\rho_{J},\rho_{L}})
as in Definition
4.35
is an element of
\mathsf{C}^{R}
uniquely determined by
K,H
and the reductions
\rho_{J}:I\longrightarrow J,

\rho_{L}:I\longrightarrow L
up to cc-isomorphism. In particular, if
(K-J),(H-L)\in\mathsf{Cob}
are uniform cobordisms such that
\partial H=J\sqcup L
and
K\cap H=J
then

(K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle J}}{{\cup}}H-L)\in\mathsf{Cob}.

Proof.

As pointed out in Definition
4.13
,
K^{\prime}:=K^{\rho_{J}}
and
H^{\prime}:=H^{\rho_{L}}
are uniquely determined by
K,H,\rho_{J}
and
\rho_{L}
, hence it remains to show that
K^{\prime}\cup H^{\prime}\in\mathsf{C}^{R}.
Notice first that
\operatorname{rk}_{\rho_{J},\rho_{L}}|_{I}=\operatorname{rk}_{I}.

The central argument of this proof is the following. By our assumptions, and considering that we are using the same notation as in Definition
4.34
, we obtain the connecting sequence (
31
). As a consequence, and by Lemmas
4.26
and
4.10
, the following sequence

\overline{M_{J}^{K}}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho^{\prime}_{J}}\overline{J(K)}\dashv_{\pi^{\prime}_{J}}\overline{I}\vdash_{\pi^{\prime}_{L}}\overline{L(H)}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}_{\rho^{\prime}_{L}}\overline{M_{L}^{H}}

where

\rho^{\prime}_{J}:=\overline{\pi_{J}^{K}},\quad\pi^{\prime}_{J}:=\overline{\rho_{J}\circ\rho_{J}^{K}},\quad\pi^{\prime}_{L}:=\overline{\rho_{L}\circ\rho_{L}^{H}},\quad\rho^{\prime}_{L}:=\overline{\pi_{L}^{H}},

is a slice sequence. Therefore Theorem
4.32
provides us with a slice
S
of rank
R
such that

S=\overline{M_{J}^{K}}\sqcup(\pi_{J}^{\prime}\sqcup\pi^{\prime}_{L})(\overline{I})\sqcup\overline{M_{L}^{H}},\quad\partial S=\overline{M_{J}^{K}}\sqcup\overline{M_{L}^{H}},\quad M^{S}=\overline{I}.

Lemmas
3.2
and
4.25
imply that there is an isomorphism of posets

S\cong\overline{K_{I}^{\rho_{J}}}\sqcup\overline{I}\sqcup\overline{H_{I}^{\rho_{L}}}.

Hence by Lemma
1.16
the duality map
x\longmapsto\overline{x}
defines a poset anti-isomorphism between
S
and
K_{I}^{\rho_{J}}\sqcup I\sqcup H_{I}^{\rho_{L}},
i.e. a bijection between the two posets which reverses inclusion relations. Moreover, for any element
x\in(\pi_{J}^{\prime}\sqcup\pi^{\prime}_{L})(\overline{I})
we have

\operatorname{rk}_{S}(x)=\operatorname{rk}_{\overline{I}}(x)+1=(R-1)-\operatorname{rk}_{I}(\overline{x})=R-\operatorname{rk}_{\rho_{J},\rho_{L}}(\overline{x}),

therefore
\operatorname{rk}_{\rho_{J},\rho_{L}}(\overline{x})=R-\operatorname{rk}_{S}(x)
for all
x\in S.

By the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
1.17
this implies that the axioms of cc as well as all properties defining elements in
\mathsf{C}
(i.e. graph-based, pure, non-branching, non-pinching, connected, cell-connected) are satisfied for the cells in
K_{I}^{\rho_{J}}\sqcup I\sqcup H_{I}^{\rho_{L}}
as the same properties are true for the cells in
S.

∎

Remark 4.37
.

If
\alpha:(K,J)\longrightarrow(K_{\alpha},J)
and
\beta:(H,L)\longrightarrow(H_{\beta},L)
are relative cc-automorphisms then
K_{\alpha}
and
H_{\beta}
also connect through
I
but in general
K\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho_{J}\penalty\ \rho_{L}}}{{\cup}}H\not\cong K_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\circ\rho_{J}\penalty\ \beta\circ\rho_{L}}}{{\cup}}H_{\beta}.

4.6
The category of causal cobordisms

We saw in the previous section that the notion of connecting sequence incorporates the conditions determining when the composition (i.e. the union) of two cobordisms is defined. It is therefore natural to use connecting sequences as the set of objects in order to define a category having morphisms defined as a certain class of cobordisms. Let
J,L\in\mathsf{B}^{R}
for some
R\geq 0
and define
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathsf{B}^{R}}(J,L)
to be the set of cc-homomorphisms
\phi:J\longrightarrow L.
The duality map then induces a bijection from
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathsf{B}^{R}}(J,L)
to
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathsf{B}^{R}}(\overline{J},\overline{L})
defined by
\overline{\phi}(x):=\overline{\phi(x)},\penalty\ x\in J.
Therefore, as a consequence of Lemma
4.10
, the duality map defines a bijection between connecting sequences and slice sequences. In order to introduce a category on which the duality map acts as a functor it is therefore also natural to include slice sequences as objects of the category.

The category of causal cobordisms we obtain as a result will be defined using the so-called braket notation commonly used in quantum mechanics.
In our context kets and bras are a convenient way to represent connecting and slice sequences and use them to define larger sequences.

In order to define the braket notation it will be convenient to introduce a lighter notation for connecting and slice sequences as follows.

Let us use the notation
M\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}J
for
M,J\in\mathsf{B}^{d}
to denote an object we call a
semi-sequence
of
dimension
d
defined as a shorthand for the notation

M\vdash_{\pi}J^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}_{\rho}J,

where
J^{\prime}\in\mathsf{B}^{d}
is the
transition of
M\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}J
and
\pi\rightthreetimes\rho
are some given data of the semi-sequence.

We define the composition of two semi-sequences of a given dimension using the orthogonality condition as follows. If
M_{0}\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}J
and
M_{1}\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}J
are semi-sequences then their
composition
is defined to be the expression

M_{0}\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}\dashv M_{1},

if this expression defines a connecting sequence, i.e.

M_{0}\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}J=M_{0}\vdash_{\pi_{0}}J_{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}_{\rho_{0}}J,\quad M_{1}\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}J=M_{1}\vdash_{\pi_{1}}J_{1}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}_{\rho_{1}}J\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{0}\mathbin{\top}\rho_{1}.

Similarly, the composition of two semi-sequences
M\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}J
and
M\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}L
is defined as

J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}\dashv M\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}L,

if the latter expression defines a slice sequence.
Unless stated otherwise, we set the convention that if
(K,J)
is a uniform relative cc then the semi-sequence
M_{J}^{K}\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}J
stands for

M_{J}^{K}\vdash_{\pi_{J}^{K}}J(K)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}_{\rho_{J}^{K}}J.

This notation will now be used to introduce the braket notation and give the definitions of states and sequences of states.

Definition 4.38

(Braket notation, sets of states
\mathcal{S}
and sequences of states
\mathcal{Q}
)
.

We introduce the following braket notation, where each
ket

|J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}\rangle
and
bra

\langle J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}|
is
labelled
by three elements
J_{i}\in\mathsf{B},\penalty\ i=1,2,3,
satisfying the following conditions:

•

|M,L,M^{\prime}\rangle
denotes the
ingoing state
corresponding to the connecting sequence

M\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}L\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}\dashv M^{\prime},

•

\langle J,M,L|
denotes the
outgoing state
corresponding to the slice sequence

J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}\dashv M\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}L,

•

\langle J^{\prime},M,J|N,L,N^{\prime}\rangle
indicates that
M\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}J
is equal to
N\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}L,

•

|N^{\prime},J,N\rangle\langle L,M,L^{\prime}|
indicates that
J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}\dashv N
is equal to
L\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}\dashv M.

We define the
set of states

\mathcal{S}
to be the union of the
set of ingoing states

\mathcal{S}^{in}
and the
set of outgoing states

\mathcal{S}^{out}.

We define a
sequence of states
to be an expression
s_{0}\dots s_{n}
where
s_{i}\in\mathcal{S},\penalty\ i=0,\dots,n
where
n\in\mathbb{N}
is the
length
of the sequence and such that
s_{0}\dots s_{n}
constitutes an expression composed of alternating ingoing and outgoing states in accordance with the above conditions. We denote by
\mathcal{Q}
the
set of sequences of states
where for each state
s
we denote by
\operatorname{id}_{s}:=s
the sequence of states of length 1 containing
s
called the
identity sequence on
s.
If
\sigma:=s_{0}\dots s_{n}\in\mathcal{Q}
we define the
image of
\sigma
by
\mathfrak{Im}(\sigma):=s_{0}
and the
domain of
\sigma
by
\mathfrak{D}(\sigma):=s_{n}.

Two sequences of the form
\sigma=s_{0}\dots s_{n},\gamma=s_{n}\dots s_{m},
where
n,m\in\mathbb{N},\penalty\ n\leq m
can be
composed
to form the sequence

\sigma\circ\gamma:=s_{0}\dots s_{m}.

By Remark
4.7
all the labels of states belonging to a given sequence of states are elements in
\mathsf{B}
of the same rank.
We define the
set of
d
-dimensional states

\mathcal{S}_{d}
to be the set of states labelled by elements in
\mathsf{B}^{d}.
Similarly, the
set of
d
-dimensional sequences

\mathcal{Q}_{d}
is defined as the set of sequences of states with labels in
\mathsf{B}^{d}.

This provides us with all the notions required to define the category of causal cobordisms.

Definition 4.39

(Category
\mathscr{C}_{d}
of causal cobordism of rank
d
)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{*}.
The
category of
d
-dimensional causal cobordisms

\mathscr{C}_{d}
is defined by having the set of states
\mathcal{S}_{d-1}
as its set of objects and set of morphisms from
a
to
b
defined by

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(a,b)=\{\sigma\in\mathcal{Q}\penalty\ |\penalty\ a=\mathfrak{D}(\sigma),\penalty\ b=\mathfrak{Im}(\sigma)\}.

The composition of morphisms in
\mathscr{C}_{d}
is defined via the composition of sequences and the identity on
s\in\mathcal{S}_{d-1}
is given by
\operatorname{id}_{s}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(s,s).

Since it is clear that the latter defines a category, it remains to justify how it is related to cobordisms.
Unless specified otherwise, we will conventionally assume that the elements in
\mathsf{B}
used to label any two given states in a sequence of states are mutually disjoint cc unless the two states are consecutive states in the sequence of states, in which case they only share one semi-sequence.

By the Correspondence Theorem
4.32
, we have a correspondence:

\langle J,M,L|\quad\text{is associated to}\quad(S-L)\in\mathsf{Cob}_{s},\quad\text{where}\quad\partial S=J\sqcup L,\penalty\ M^{S}\cong M,

and where the slice
S
corresponds to the slice sequence

J\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}\dashv M\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}L.

In this case we consider the two following bras as identical:

\langle J,M,L|=\langle S-L|.

Then for example if
\langle S-L|,\langle S^{\prime}-L^{\prime}|
are outgoing states we call
|M,L,M^{\prime}\rangle
an
interpolating ingoing state
if

\langle S-L|M,L,M^{\prime}\rangle\langle S^{\prime}-L^{\prime}|\in\mathcal{Q}.

The latter requirement is equivalent to stating that
\partial S^{\prime}=L\sqcup L^{\prime}
and
M\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}L\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}\dashv M^{\prime}
is the connecting sequence

M^{S}\vdash_{\pi_{L}^{S}}L(S)\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}_{\rho_{L}^{S}}L\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}_{\rho_{L}^{S^{\prime}}}L(S^{\prime})\dashv_{\pi_{L}^{S^{\prime}}}M^{S^{\prime}}.

Corollary
4.36
implies that one can define the union
(S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{\cup}}S^{\prime}-L^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Cob}.
We can therefore define the
composition of
\langle S-L|
with
\langle S^{\prime}-L^{\prime}|
via the ingoing state
|M,L,M^{\prime}\rangle
by

\langle S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{\cup}}S^{\prime}-L^{\prime}|:=\langle S-L|M,L,M^{\prime}\rangle\langle S^{\prime}-L^{\prime}|,

which in particular implies that
M=M^{S}
and
M^{\prime}=M^{S^{\prime}}.

More generally, we recursively define outgoing states of the form
\langle K-J|
where
K
is a union of slices
S_{0},\dots,S_{n}
using the relation:

\langle S_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{i}}}{{\cup}}_{i=1,\dots,n}S_{i}-L_{n}|:=\langle S_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{i}}}{{\cup}}_{i=1,\dots,n-1}S_{i}-L_{n-1}|M_{n-1},L_{n-1},M_{n}\rangle\langle S_{n}-L_{n}|,

where
\partial S_{i}=L_{i-1}\sqcup L_{i}
and
M_{i}=M^{S_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,n.

At this point it is natural to introduce the following notion of causal cobordism.

Definition 4.40

(Causal cobordisms
\mathsf{Cob}_{c}
)
.

A cobordism
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob}
is said to be
causal
if there exist slices
S_{0},\dots,S_{n}
and interpolating ingoing states
|M^{i},L_{i},M^{i+1}\rangle
for
i=1,\dots,n
such that

(K-J)=(S_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{i}}}{{\cup}}_{i=1,\dots,n}S_{i}-L_{n}).

The set of causal cobordisms is denoted by
\mathsf{Cob}_{c}.
We will also call a bra of the form
\langle K-J|
where
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob}_{c}
a causal cobordism.
For each
J\in\mathsf{B}
we conventionally define an element called the
empty causal cobordism at
J
denoted
(J-J)
to be a causal cobordism with boundary
J
and rank
\operatorname{Rk}(J)+1.

Empty cobordisms are simply introduced to make some expressions of sequences of states more general. We will in particular use the bra
\langle J-J|
in an expression of the form:

|M,J,M^{\prime}\rangle\langle J-J|N,L,N^{\prime}\rangle\quad\text{implying that}\quad|M,J,M^{\prime}\rangle=|N,L,N^{\prime}\rangle.

It will also be convenient to introduce a notation indicating that a slice is either the first or the last slice of a causal cobordism as follows. Let us consider a causal cobordism
(K-J)\in\mathsf{Cob}_{c}
given by

(K-J)=(S_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L_{i}}}{{\cup}}_{i=1,\dots,n}S_{i}-L_{n}),

for some slices
S_{0},\dots,S_{n}
and ingoing states
|M^{i},L_{i},M^{i+1}\rangle,\penalty\ i=1,\dots,n
such that
J=L_{n}.
Writing
L:=L_{0}
we have that
\partial K=J\sqcup L.

We say that a slice
\langle S-J|=\langle J^{\prime},M^{S},J|
is the
ingoing slice of
\langle K-J|
and use the notation

\langle J^{\prime},M^{S},J|\leq_{\text{in}}\langle K-J|,

if
(S-J)=(S_{n}-L_{n}).

Similarly, we say that a slice
\langle S-L^{\prime}|=\langle L,M^{S},L^{\prime}|
is the
outgoing slice of
\langle K-J|
and use the notation

\langle L,M^{S},L^{\prime}|\leq_{\text{out}}\langle K-J|,

if
S=S_{0}.

Using this notation, we can more explicitly express the sets of morphisms between states as follows. The expressions associated to morphisms in
\mathscr{C}_{d}
are of four different kinds depending on whether the image and domain are ingoing or outgoing states:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(|M,J,M^{\prime}\rangle,|N^{\prime},L,N\rangle):=\left\{\penalty\ |N^{\prime},L,N\rangle\langle K-J|M,J,M^{\prime}\rangle\penalty\ :\penalty\ \langle K-J|\in\mathsf{Cob}_{c}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(\langle J^{\prime},M,J|,|N^{\prime},L,N\rangle):=\left\{\penalty\ |N^{\prime},L,N\rangle\langle K-J|\penalty\ :\penalty\ \langle K-J|\in\mathsf{Cob}_{c},\penalty\ \langle J^{\prime},M,J|\leq_{\text{in}}\langle K-J|\right\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(|M,J,M^{\prime}\rangle,\langle L^{\prime},N,L|):=\left\{\penalty\ \langle K-J|M,J,M^{\prime}\rangle\penalty\ :\penalty\ \langle K-J|\in\mathsf{Cob}_{c},\penalty\ \langle L^{\prime},N,L|\leq_{\text{out}}\langle K-J|\right\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(\langle J^{\prime},M,J|,\langle L^{\prime},N,L|):=\left\{\penalty\ \langle K-J|\in\mathsf{Cob}_{c}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \begin{array}[]{c}\langle J^{\prime},M,J|\leq_{\text{in}}\langle K-J|,\\
\langle L^{\prime},N,L|\leq_{\text{out}}\langle K-J|\end{array}\right\}.

It is seen that only the morphisms in the last set
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(\langle J^{\prime},M,J|,\langle L^{\prime},N,L|)
correspond exactly to the notion of cobordisms we defined in Definition
4.40
. The other sets of morphisms include additional ingoing states corresponding to connecting sequences associated with one or both of their boundary components. The morphisms of the first set
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(|M,J,M^{\prime}\rangle,|N^{\prime},L,N\rangle)
actually define the
sub-category of
d
-dimensional ingoing states

\mathscr{C}_{d}^{in}
with
\mathcal{S}^{in}_{d}
as the set of objects. Similarly, one can single out the
sub-category of
d
-dimensional outgoing states

\mathscr{C}_{d}^{out}
having
\mathcal{S}^{out}_{d}
as objects and morphisms defined by the last set
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(\langle J^{\prime},M,J|,\langle L^{\prime},N,L|).

We can now turn to the definition of the duality map for elements in
\mathscr{C}^{d}.
For this, it is sufficient to specify how the duality map acts on sequences of states in
\mathcal{Q}_{d}
as done in the following definition.

Definition 4.41

(Duality map on
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{Q}
)
.

The dual of a sequence of states is defined via the following rules:

•

if
s=|M,L,M^{\prime}\rangle
then
\overline{s}=\langle\overline{M^{\prime}},\overline{L},\overline{M}|
is the outgoing state characterized by the slice sequence

\overline{M^{\prime}}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}\dashv\overline{L}\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}\overline{M},

that we call the
dual sequence
of
M\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}L\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}\dashv M^{\prime};

•

if
s=\langle J,M,L|
then
\overline{s}=|\overline{L},\overline{M},\overline{J}\rangle
is the ingoing state characterized by the connecting sequence

\overline{L}\vdash\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\succ$}}\overline{M}\mathrel{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\prec$}}\dashv\overline{J},

•

if
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}
is a sequence of states then

\overline{\sigma}=\overline{\sigma_{2}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \overline{\sigma_{1}}.

The definition of the duality map on
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{Q}
for example implies:

\displaystyle\overline{\langle J,M,L|M,L,M^{\prime}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\overline{|M,L,M^{\prime}\rangle}\penalty\ \penalty\ \overline{\langle J,M,L|}=\langle\overline{M^{\prime}},\overline{L},\overline{M}|\overline{L},\overline{M},\overline{J}\rangle.

(32)

As a direct consequence of the above we obtain the following result.

Corollary 4.42

(Duality map on
\mathscr{C}^{d}
)
.

Let
a,b\in\mathcal{S}^{d}
and let
\sigma
be a morphism in
\mathscr{C}^{d}.
We have the following equivalence:

\sigma\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(a,b)\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\overline{\sigma}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(\overline{b},\overline{a}).

In other words,
\mathscr{C}_{d}
is self-dual as a category under the contravariant functor
\mathtt{C}=\overline{(\cdot)}
induced by the duality map. It is also clear that
\mathtt{C}
can be restricted to a functor between the sub-categories
\mathscr{C}_{d}^{in}
and
\mathscr{C}_{d}^{out}.

Let us furthermore note the following contravariant functor
\mathtt{T}={(\cdot)}^{-1}
in
\mathscr{C}_{d}.
For any state
s\in\mathcal{S},
let the
reverse state

{s}^{-1}
be the state defined by the same labels in reversed order, for example

{|M^{\prime},L,M\rangle}^{-1}=|M,L,M^{\prime}\rangle.

Then
s\in\mathcal{S}^{in}
if and only if
{s}^{-1}\in\mathcal{S}^{in}
and similarly for
\mathcal{S}^{out}.
We also define the
reverse sequence of states

{\sigma}^{-1}
of a sequence
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\penalty\ \sigma_{2}
by

{\sigma}^{-1}={\sigma_{2}}^{-1}\penalty\ {\sigma_{1}}^{-1},

which evidently implies that

\sigma\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(a,b)\quad\text{if and only if}\quad{\sigma}^{-1}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(b,a).

Finally, it is worth mentioning a third (covariant) functor
\mathtt{P}=(\cdot)^{*}
defined as follows. The functor
\mathtt{P}
acts on states as a map
s\longmapsto s^{*},
where
s^{*}
is the state corresponding to the sequence dual to the sequence characterizing
s
labelled by the dual of the labels of
s
in the same order. For example,

|M^{\prime},L,M\rangle^{*}=\langle\overline{M^{\prime}},\overline{L},\overline{M}|.

The functor
\mathtt{P}
acts on morphisms in
\mathscr{C}_{d}
simply by acting on each states of the corresponding sequence of states, i.e. if
\sigma=\sigma_{1}\penalty\ \sigma_{2}
then

\sigma^{*}=\sigma_{1}^{*}\penalty\ \sigma_{2}^{*}.

This in turn implies the equivalence:

\sigma\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(a,b)\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\sigma^{*}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}(a^{*},b^{*}).

These functors satisfy
\mathtt{T}^{2}=\mathtt{C}^{2}=\mathtt{P}^{2}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathscr{C}_{d}}
and are in particular related by the relation
\mathtt{C}=\mathtt{P}\penalty\ \mathtt{T},
as can be seen for the specific example used in (
32
):

\displaystyle\left({\left(\langle J,M,L|M,L,M^{\prime}\rangle\right)}^{-1}\right)^{*}

\displaystyle=\left(|M^{\prime},L,M\rangle\langle M,L,J|\right)^{*}

\displaystyle=\langle\overline{M^{\prime}},\overline{L},\overline{M}|\overline{L},\overline{M},\overline{J}\rangle=\overline{\langle J,M,L|M,L,M^{\prime}\rangle}.

It is then suggestive to view
\mathtt{T}
as the analogue of a ”time reversal” transformation,
\mathtt{P}
as the analogue of a ”parity transformation” and the duality map
\mathtt{C}
as the analogue of a ”charge transformation”.
